--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical introduction. While the previous page (noted in your metadata) transitioned from the Gospel of Matthew to a "Book of Judgment," this specific text is actually a philosophical treatise on logic and definitions, likely part of David the Invincible’s *Definitions of Philosophy*, a foundational text in medieval Armenian education. It moves from the spiritual preparation of the student to the formal logical methodology used to analyze existence.</meta>

...shall be placed. Having tasted [of these things], and having bid farewell to all worldly cares, those described are led with a certain prudent unity toward these pursuits. And from that point, through the true knowledge <term>Knowledge: "makats'ut'i" (մակացութի), a technical term in Armenian Neoplatonism equivalent to the Greek "episteme" or scientific knowledge.</term> of beings, they quickly transform their very desire toward the same goal. Now, the knowledge of beings, as we are about to learn, is wisdom.

And now—since he has led us to this contest with much abundance, prudent preoccupation, and wise desire—let us clothe ourselves for this divine contest with the love of wisdom <note>original: իմաստասիրութբ (imastasirut'p), the Armenian term for "philosophy," literally "love of wisdom."</note>. Let us not consider the task at hand to be difficult, but rather, by looking toward the divine promises, let us consider all labor to be humble and secondary to them. 

But it seems good to me to make a brief delay in the narrative and to arrive at the distinctions of the arts. They say that in all inquiries, one must seek these four "heads" <note>These are the four Aristotelian questions used to investigate any subject.</note>:
1. Whether it is <note>Existence</note>.
2. What it is <note>Essence or Definition</note>.
3. Of what kind it is <note>Quality</note>.
4. Why it is <note>Purpose or Cause</note>.

And rightly are these sought; for among things, some are non-existent, such as the **Stag-goat** <note>original: Եղջերուաքաղ (Eghjeruak'agh); a classical logical example (Greek: tragelaphos) of a creature that can be imagined and named but does not exist in nature.</note> and the **Aralez** <note>original: արալէզ (aralēz); supernatural creatures from Armenian mythology, dog-like spirits said to lick the wounds of fallen heroes to bring them back to life. Here, they are used as an example of an "imaginary" being.</note>, and whatever else our thoughts create. But others possess existence.

And of those that possess existence, some possess it in a doubtful way, such as the "unspoken" <unclear>heaven</unclear> and the antipodes <note>original: հակոտնեայքն (hakotneayk'n), those living on the opposite side of the world.</note>. For we are in doubt concerning these: whether they exist or whether they do not. But some

<column-break/>

possess existence without doubt, such as man, horse, and eagle, which confessedly exist. 

Now, regarding the non-existent and those that possess existence doubtfully, one must seek "Whether they are." But regarding those that possess existence without doubt, one must not seek the "Whether," but rather the "What." 

Now, the "What" is known to us either by name or by definition. By name, when seeing something, we ask "What is it?" and we say, "It is a man." But by definition, when we say: "A living being, rational, mortal, capable of mind and intellect" <note>This is the classic Porphyrian/Aristotelian definition of a human being.</note>.

And since all things have not only commonality with one another, but also differences—for living beings have not only a commonality with one another according to genus <note>original: սեռ (seṛ)</note>, insofar as they are living beings, but also differences. For some are rational and some are irrational; some are mortal and some are immortal. For this reason, the "Of what kind" is also sought, so that through it we may learn the differences. For by asking "Of what kind is it?" we say: "Rational" or "Irrational," "Mortal" or "Immortal." 

And it should be known that often the "Of what kind" and the "What" appear as the same to some. For when the "What" is recognized by definition, then the "Of what kind" is seen within the "What." For the "What" is recognized along with the definition, but when the "What" is recognized [only] by name, then the "Of what kind" [is distinct] from the "What."

<meta>catchword: but (իսկ)</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the four fundamental logical questions (existence, essence, quality, and cause) used to analyze any subject. It uses examples like the mythological Aralez and the definition of "man" to explain how we distinguish between imaginary things and real beings.</summary>

<keywords>logic, philosophy, David the Invincible, Aralez, Stag-goat, definition of man, Aristotelian categories, existence, genus, Armenian Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical introduction to philosophy by David the Invincible. The previous page established the four fundamental questions of inquiry: 1. Does it exist? 2. What is it? 3. What kind is it? 4. Why is it? This page concludes the discussion on the "why" (purpose) and begins defending the very existence of philosophy against skeptics who claim it is merely an empty name.</meta>

...and if it does not appear [to be non-existent], then it is necessary to ask **of what kind** it is. And since all things that have come into being are naturally disposed toward a certain **perfection** <note>original: "katarumn" (կատարումն), referring to the Aristotelian concept of "teleos" or completion.</note>, and because neither the Creator, nor Nature, nor Art creates anything in vain or without purpose, we therefore importantly ask **"why"** it is. 

For instance: why do the stars exist? For the sake of human rest <note>Ancient philosophy often held that the movements of stars and the transition to night were designed for human recovery.</note>. And why does man exist? To adorn the sensible world. <margin>Ibid.</margin> For if the human race did not exist, the whole [of the cosmos] would not be complete, as Plato makes clear in the dialogue of the *Timaeus* <note>original: "i Timēosi tramabanut'e" (ի Տիմէոսի տրամաբանութե). Timaeus is Plato's famous work on the creation of the universe.</note>. For he speaks thus regarding the Creator: "That after making the heavens and the things within them, he proclaimed to the intelligible powers <note>the lesser gods or divine forces</note> in this manner: 'There are yet three mortal kinds <note>birds, water creatures, and land creatures</note> which have not yet come to be; without them, the heaven is incomplete.'"

The meaning of these words is this: there are yet three mortal kinds lacking to us, which are not yet created. And these three mortal kinds are: those of the air, those of the water, and those of the earth—among which is man. Without these, as he says, the heaven is incomplete. He called the **world** "heaven," using the "container" to signify the "contained," since the heaven contains the world. 

Thus, **philosophy** <term>Philosophy: "imastasirut'iun" (իմաստասիրութիւն), literally "love of wisdom."</term> is present particularly for us, and it is the most beautiful and honorable of all human works. While it does not appear [as non-existent] to most, to some others it does seem non-existent—just as those who have been blind since birth slander colors. Regarding these people, the Tyrian <note>original: "ptgheratsin" (պտղերացին). This likely refers to Porphyry of Tyre, a famous Neoplatonist commentator.</note> says that those blind from birth cannot

<column-break/>

slander colors, because they do not know the nature of color at all. Let us bring forth the arguments of these people and their errors, and let us speak to the best of our ability, clearly rebuking their foolishness. But as to what kind of arguments these are, we shall learn in the following investigation.

->**Thus with all the present**<-
->**activity.**<-

<image-desc>A vertical decorative ornament in the right margin of the second paragraph, consisting of stylized foliage and a long vertical flourish.</image-desc>
Now, as we promised in the first section, we shall set forth the arguments of those who wish to destroy the existence of philosophy, and we shall refute them to the best of our ability. And the first premise of those who deny the existence of philosophy is expressed in this manner: 

"There are either **beings** or **names**. If they are only names, they do not fall under definitions, nor under knowledge. Therefore, they are unknowable. But according to you, philosophy is knowledge; yet it is evident that it is unknowable, because it is [merely] a name."

<margin>92</margin>Now, in this undertaking, we encounter opposition and counter-argument through two modes. **Opposition** is the task of making the truth firm from the beginning and never confusing the matter at hand by following the subtle arguments of those who would destroy philosophy. <margin>40</margin> We employ opposition and counter-argument by saying: "It is not that the **being** is in...

<summary>This page argues that every creature, including humans, has a purposeful role in completing the cosmos, citing Plato's Timaeus. It then begins a formal logical defense of philosophy against skeptics who claim it is an empty name rather than a real "being" that can be known.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Timaeus, Porphyry, purpose of man, existence of philosophy, logic, refutation of skeptics, four questions of inquiry</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues David the Invincible's defense of philosophy. Having previously discussed the four types of inquiry, he now addresses the problem of **homonyms** (words with the same name but different meanings) and begins refuting the Skeptics (like Pyrrho) who argue that because the world is in constant flux, true knowledge is impossible. The catchword "it is fitting" (պատկա) appears at the bottom of the second column.</meta>

...homonyms, as well as those broad terms which lie in between. For as to what homonyms are, we are to learn in what follows <note>David is referring to the "Categories" of Aristotle, which traditionally begins with a discussion of homonyms.</note>. But against the objections [of the skeptics] we say this: even if we grant what a homonym is, it does not follow that it is unknowable. For although homonyms cannot be brought under a single definition, they are not for that reason unknowable. For it is a rule of logical inquiry that one must seek three things regarding homonyms: first, how many meanings the homonym has; second, which of these meanings the discourse is about; and third, to then describe or define that specific meaning which the discourse concerns.

Now, let us confirm what has been said with an illustration: when the word used is **"dog"** <note>The term "dog" (շուն/shun) was the standard ancient example for a homonym: it can mean the animal, the Dog Star (Sirius), or a seal (sea-dog).</note>, because this sound is a homonym. First, one must examine how many meanings it has: such as whether it signifies the celestial [star], the marine [sea-dog], or the terrestrial [animal]. Second, one must state which of these meanings is intended—that is, which "dog" the discourse is about. And third, to bring that specific thing which the word concerns under a description or definition. For instance, if the word is about the terrestrial dog, we say it is a "four-footed, barking animal." 

Now, in this way, we also seek these three things regarding **"Being"** [existence]. For we seek first how many meanings it has, and we say it is expressed according to ten significations, that is—

<column-break/>

—according to the **ten categories** <note>The *Categories* are Aristotle's classification of all possible things that can be said about a subject: Substance, Quantity, Quality, Relation, Place, Time, Position, State, Action, and Passion.</note>. And second, we seek which of these signified things the discourse is about: whether about substance, or quantity, or quality, or the others. And third, we place the signified thing under a definition or description. But if we place it under a definition and description, it is clear that it is not unknowable.

Now, the second argument of those who would destroy the existence of philosophy <note>These are the Skeptics, who argued that since the physical world never stays the same, we can never truly "know" it.</note> proceeds with an explanation of this sort: 

<margin>Pyrrho</margin> All beings are in **flux and flowing** <note>original: "i hosman ew i corman" (ի հոսման և ի ծորման). This echoes the famous "Panta Rhei" (everything flows) doctrine of Heraclitus.</note>; they never encounter a state of rest. For a change beyond words occurs in beings, and before one can even speak of them, 

<margin>Democritus</margin> they have already changed. And they bring forth an illustration like this: 

<margin>Pyrrho</margin> just as in a flowing river, one cannot wet his feet twice in the same water—and as some, further extending this doubt, say that one cannot even wet his feet **once** in the same water, because that water is swiftly flowing away and passing by—so too beings, being in flux and flowing, never encounter rest. And it is evident that they do not fall under the scope of knowledge. For in the very moment of observing them to know them, they change; and at different times they exist differently, and no one can reach or know them. Now, if philosophy is the knowledge of beings, and yet 

<margin>Democritus</margin> beings are unknowable due to being in flux and flowing, then philosophy is impossible. And again, they say in another way: 

<margin>Pyrrho</margin> that for a beautiful understanding, it is fitting for the one who knows...

<summary>David the Invincible explains how logical definitions can resolve the ambiguity of homonyms and introduces the Skeptic argument that knowledge is impossible because all things are in a constant state of flux, like a flowing river.</summary>
<keywords>homonyms, logic, definitions, ten categories, Skepticism, Pyrrho, flux, Heraclitus, Democritus, being</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues David the Invincible's defense of philosophy against the Skeptics. Having addressed the issue of homonyms, David now moves to the Skeptic argument that knowledge is impossible because objects in the world are constantly changing (flux). He uses the Aristotelian distinction between the Knower (the soul) and the Known (the object) to frame the debate.</meta>

...it is fitting <note>original: "պատկանի" (patkani). This word completes the sentence started on the previous page.</note> to know. And they call the soul the "knower," while the "known" is the underlying object. Now, there is a beautiful fittingness—that is, a grasping and a capable comprehension. It is argued that knowing must not be moved nor exist in a diverse state. For if the **known** moves and changes, it would be necessary for the **knower**—that is, the soul—to move and change along with it. And perhaps, wherever the known thing is white, it would be necessary for the soul to be white; and if the known thing is water, it would be necessary for the soul to be water, so that like may know like <note>original: "նմանեանն զնմանն" (nmaneann znmann). This refers to the ancient philosophical principle *similis simili cognoscitur*—the idea that the mind must resemble what it perceives.</note>. But since the soul does not change along with the known object, it is evident that it cannot know the known. Therefore, philosophy cannot be the knowledge of beings, because they do not fall under [stable] knowledge, and so forth.

<margin>
David <note>The abbreviation "դթ" (DT) likely refers to David (Dawitʻ), the author.</note>
</margin>

**T**hese are their arguments. And we say four other things: first and foremost, that philosophy does not belong to the particulars—which are in a state of flux and flowing—but to universals, which do not change but remain always the same. **S**econd, we say that what they claim about philosophy—that it is concerned with particulars—is false. It is not because of this that one is unable to attain or know anything. For this reason, the knower—that is, the soul—does not change along with the known—that is, the subject. For according to this reasoning, the soul remains the same; it knows something of the things that exist, and tomorrow it does not change with the known things, but remains always the same. And again, by this reasoning, a doctor, being healthy by nature, would not be able to recognize pains without suffering them himself; that is, when he is healthy, he would not be able to practice his art upon the pain,

<column-break/>

but would be forced to become sick in order to know the sickness. However, many times the soul knows things before the objects themselves change. As Plato also makes clear, saying: "The souls of the virtuous are naturally disposed not only to follow after sensible things, but often they know them even before they occur."

<margin>
Useful
</margin>

Now, these things are so. The **T**hird argument of those who would destroy philosophy is this: If the fluctuating things <gloss>known</gloss> fall under sense perception. As Plato also makes clear by saying that the Creator granted us sight and hearing so that through them we might direct the philosophical genus <note>meaning the mind or the capacity for reason</note>. **N**ow, having established this, you say confessedly: If we show through your own division that the parts of philosophy cannot truly be parts of philosophy,

<margin>
David
Useful
</margin>

it is clear that they say the whole—that is, philosophy—cannot be composed. And they say that according to you, philosophy is divided into the **theoretical** and the **practical**. **A**nd again, the theoretical is divided into the **natural** <note>physics</note>, the **mathematical**, and the **theological** <note>metaphysics</note>. Now, if we destroy these parts, it is clear that philosophy cannot be composed; for if the parts do not exist, the whole cannot be composed. And they say that the mathematical <note>original: "ուսումնականն" (usumnakann), referring to the Quadrivium of arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy.</note> is not a part of philosophy, as Plato also thinks. For he does not consider mathematics to be a part of philosophy, but a kind of preliminary training, like grammar <note>original: "քերթողականն" (kʻertʻoghakann), literally "the poetic/literary art."</note>. Wherefore he also inscribed above his academy... <note>David is about to reference the famous inscription: "Let no one ignorant of geometry enter."</note>

<summary>David refutes the Skeptic claim that knowledge is impossible due to the world's constant flux, arguing that philosophy deals with unchanging universals and that the soul does not need to "become" the object it knows. He begins addressing the Skeptic's systematic deconstruction of the divisions of philosophy (theoretical and practical).</summary>
<keywords>Skepticism, Epistemology, Flux, Plato, Universals vs Particulars, Theoretical Philosophy, Practical Philosophy, Mathematics as Propedeutica, David the Invincible</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues David the Invincible's defense of philosophy against the Skeptics. Having established that the soul can indeed know objects despite their changing nature, David now addresses the divisions of theoretical philosophy—Physical, Mathematical, and Theological—and moves toward a fourth skeptical challenge regarding whether knowledge can truly be "universal."</meta>

<margin>99</margin>
...“Let no one unskilled in geometry enter.” <note>original: "աներկբաշաին մի՛ ներսամտեսցէ" (anerkbashayin mi nersamts'ē). This is a translation of the famous Greek inscription over Plato's Academy: *Ageōmetrētos mēdeis eisitō*.</note> Now, concerning the **physical** <term>physical: the study of nature and material reality</term>, I say that we have already overturned their position via the second argument. They claimed that "beings are in a state of flux and flowing, and they never reach a state of rest."

As for the **theological** <term>theological: the study of divine or immaterial things</term>, they try to destroy it by saying: "Divine things are never subject to the senses, nor can they be subject to [human] knowledge." Therefore, they conclude, divine things are unknowable.

So much for these points. But as we say, you have said nothing yet against the **practical** part of philosophy, which stands in contrast to the **theoretical**. It is clear that you have not destroyed all of philosophy. For the practical is a part of philosophy, as **Plato** makes clear when he says: "I do not call a 'philosopher' one who is merely a polymath <note>original: "յոդնագէտ" (yodnagēt)—one who knows many facts or 'know-it-all.'</note> or one who is able to speak at great length; rather, I call a philosopher the one who possesses within themselves a life conducted with unstained behavior." This is the "practical" philosopher.

So much for that. But let us come and provide an answer regarding the **mathematical**, the **physical**, and the **theological**. Concerning the **mathematical**, we say that Plato does not consider it *not* to be a part of philosophy, but rather a part of the supreme philosophy—that is, the theological. Since he considers it a part of the highest science, and since he knows the mathematical is a branch of philosophy, he makes this clear by calling it "knowledge." <note>original: "մակացութի" (makats'ut'iun). In this context, it refers to a structured science or certain knowledge.</note> Now, if it is knowledge, it is clearly a part of philosophy. Furthermore, **Aristotle** explicitly calls the mathematical a part of philosophy.

<column-break/>

As for the **physical**, we answered by overturning the second argument, which claimed that beings are in flux and flowing. And regarding the **theological**, we say that although the divine is unknowable in its own essence, yet by looking at his creations, his handiwork, and the well-ordered movement of the world, we arrive at a thought of the Creator. For the invisible is most quickly recognized through the visible.

Having thus overturned these various points, let us also bring this third argument to a halt so we may proceed to the vision before us.

<margin>note</margin> <note>original: "պիտ" (pit). An abbreviation for "պիտանի" (pitani), meaning 'useful' or 'noteworthy.'</note> The **fourth argument** encountered by the opponents of philosophy is as follows, according to this pattern:

Philosophy is either knowledge, or it is not knowledge. If it is not knowledge, then it can be neither an art nor a science. But if it is knowledge, it must be either **universal** or **particular**. If it were particular knowledge, it would be "worse" <note>In this context, 'worse' means 'inferior' or 'lesser in scope.'</note> than the other arts and sciences—I mean, for example, grammar or rhetoric. For those have universal definitions.

If it were inferior to other arts, how could you call it the "art of arts" and the "science of sciences"? <note>This is a classic definition of philosophy in the late antique tradition.</note> On the other hand, if it is **universal** knowledge, it cannot exist within a single person. For universal knowledge is a universal accident; and a universal accident cannot exist within a particular subject. For instance, "universal whiteness" exists through the whole [category], otherwise it could not exist within a single hand or a drop of milk. Now, if philosophy...

<summary>David the Invincible defends the three branches of theoretical philosophy (Physical, Mathematical, Theological) and the Practical branch, arguing that God can be known through the order of creation. He then begins to tackle a fourth skeptical dilemma concerning whether philosophy can be considered "universal knowledge."</summary>
<keywords>David the Invincible, Plato, Aristotle, types of philosophy, flux, teleology, universal vs particular, skeptical arguments</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the defense of philosophy against Skeptical arguments. Having previously addressed the physical, mathematical, and theological branches of philosophy, David now tackles the logical problem of how a "universal" science like philosophy can exist within a "particular" individual like Socrates or Plato.</meta>

[Philo]sophy, as a universal science, cannot exist in a particular subject <term>subject: in logic, the individual being (like a person) in which a quality or knowledge resides</term>, that is, in Socrates or in Plato. In resolving this doubt, we say: **If** philosophy is a universal science, it is not for these reasons that it cannot consist of universal science. For that which is receptive to a universal **accident** <term>accident: a property that can change without changing the essence of the thing, like the color of one's skin</term> is one thing, and that which is receptive to universal philosophy is another. For the body is receptive to a universal accident, while the mind is receptive to universal philosophy. 

And although a single individual cannot receive a universal accident—for as we said, Socrates cannot receive "universal white"—for if Socrates were able to receive "universal white," then whiteness would no longer be able to appear in anyone else. But while perhaps Socrates cannot receive "universal white" with his body, he can receive the **definition** <note>original: "զբան" (z-ban) – meaning the word, reason, or logical definition</note> of "universal white" with his mind. For Socrates can know that "universal white" is a "color that dilates the eyes." <note>This is the classic Aristotelian definition of the color white (Greek: *diakritikon opseōs*), which was thought to physically expand the visual sense.</note>

And this must be shown according to another level. For a body cannot receive opposites generically and without confusion in the same part and at the same time. For it cannot receive white and black generically and without confusion in the same part and at the same time. We say "in the same part" because in different parts of the body, it *can* receive opposites at the same time. And we say "at the same time"...

<column-break/>

...because in the same part, opposites can exist without confusion at different times. And though it is said that the body cannot receive opposites generically and without confusion in the same part and at the same time, the mind is nevertheless able to receive within itself the definitions of universal opposites all at once. For it can know that "universal white" is a color that dilates the eyes, and that "universal black" is a color that contracts the eyes. <note>original: "բաղդատող աչաց" (baghdatogh ach'ats') – the Greek term *synkritikon*, referring to how black was thought to compress the vision.</note> 

If, then, the mind—which distinguishes those things existing in individuals—is able to receive universal opposites generically and without confusion at the same time, how is the mind not able to receive universal philosophy, which is the "knowledge of all beings"? 

And because perfect instruction is that which not only resolves opposing doubts, but also is able to establish itself, we have resolved the previously stated doubt. Let us henceforth demonstrate philosophy in this order and according to this degree: 

**If God exists, philosophy exists.** 

But God truly exists. For although the Epicurean <note>Followers of Epicurus, whom ancient Christian and Neoplatonic scholars often (sometimes unfairly) characterized as atheists because they believed the gods did not interfere in human affairs.</note> says that there is no God, we say that if God exists, there is **Providence**. For God not only creates, but also takes care of that which is created. Now, if there is Providence, there is also **Wisdom**, for He does not provide irrationally, but with Wisdom. And if there is Wisdom, there is also a **desire for Wisdom**. And if there is a desire for Wisdom, there is the **love of wisdom** <note>In Armenian, "love of wisdom" (*imastasirut'iwn*) is the literal translation of the Greek *philosophia*.</note>.

<summary>This page argues that while a human body cannot hold universal properties (like being "all whiteness"), the human mind can hold universal concepts. It concludes with a logical chain proving that the existence of a wise and caring God necessitates the existence of philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Universal vs. Particular, Socrates, Plato, White and Black, Aristotelian Optics, Logic, Mind vs. Body, Providence, Epicureans, Etymology of Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory arguments for the study of philosophy. Having previously addressed the ontological status of philosophy (whether it can exist within an individual), the author now transitions to the classic "Protrepticus" argument: that even the denial of philosophy requires the act of philosophizing.</meta>

If it is the love of wisdom, then it is also philosophy; for philosophy is nothing other than the love of wisdom.

### Along with these things, there is an addition
### Second Discourse

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop-cap 'I' depicting a male face within the upper curve and foliage patterns extending from the body of the letter.</image-desc> Although some used to say that it was not possible to discuss philosophy or whether it exists, nevertheless, truth—taken up like an ally <note>original: "նիզակակից" (nizakakits) – literally "spear-mate" or comrade-in-arms</note>—destroys the arguments of those who tried to abolish the existence of philosophy. For those whose way of life is far from the truth, namely the Pyrrhonists <note>The Pyrrhonists were ancient Skeptics who argued that certain knowledge is impossible</note>, wishing to destroy everything, they also wished to destroy the mother of demonstration—that is, philosophy. They attempted to destroy philosophy by means of demonstration; thus, they tried to destroy philosophy by using philosophy itself. 

Plato, addressing those suffering from this view <note>The "sickness" of skepticism</note>, said this to them regarding their claim that nothing is certain <note>original: "հաստուն" (hastun) – firm, certain, or established</note>: "You say that nothing is certain? How do you say that nothing is certain? Do you say it as those who have apprehended <term>apprehended: in logic, to 'grasp' or 'reach' a truth with the mind</term> this fact, or as those who have not?" If you say it as those who have apprehended it, then it is clear that you have reached a truth, and therefore something is certain. But if you say it as those who have not apprehended it, who will believe you, since you are speaking of things you have not reached and do not know? 

Furthermore, Aristotle, in a certain "Exhortation" <note>This refers to Aristotle's lost work, the *Protrepticus*</note> in which he encourages beginners in philosophy, says the following: "Even if one must not philosophize, one must philosophize; and if one must philosophize, one must philosophize. Thus, in every way, one must philosophize." That is to say, even if one says that philosophy does not exist, he uses demonstration <term>demonstration: a logical proof that leads to a necessary conclusion</term>

<column-break/>

to abolish philosophy. But if he has need of demonstration, it is clear that he is philosophizing, for philosophy is the mother of demonstration. And if someone says that philosophy exists, he again philosophizes, for he requires demonstration by which he shows that it exists. Therefore, in every way, one philosophizes—both he who would abolish it and he who would not. For each of them has need of demonstrations by which their statements may be made credible. And if one needs demonstration, it is clear that he is philosophizing, for as we said, philosophy is the mother of demonstration.

But leaving aside the question of "if it exists," let us inquire into "what is philosophy" and "what kind of thing is it?" And one must know that philosophy is a certain kind of thing that exists as a whole, yet is also multifaceted, having various parts. Therefore, we shall define it as a single thing, but we shall divide it as something multifaceted. For it is inappropriate to give only a little to other arts and to divide them, while not defining or dividing philosophy, from which all arts proceed. 

And one must know that Plato mentions definition and division elsewhere—speaking of definition in the *Phaedrus* <note>original: "փետրունում" (p'etrunum) – a transliteration of the Greek title 'Phaedrus'</note> regarding logic, saying: "O youth, there is one beginning for thinking beautifully <note>meaning: thinking correctly or virtuously</note>: to know what the subject of thought is; otherwise, one must necessarily miss everything." That is to say, if someone wishes to think and reflect truly about anything, he must first know the nature of that thing—which is its definition.

<summary>This page presents the classic logical defense of philosophy, arguing that even the attempt to deny philosophy requires the use of philosophical tools like demonstration. It concludes by transitioning from the existence of philosophy to the necessity of defining and dividing its various parts, citing Plato's Phaedrus.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, Skepticism, Pyrrhonists, Aristotle, Protrepticus, Plato, Phaedrus, Demonstration, Definition, Logic</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the methodology of philosophy. Having established that philosophy is necessary even to argue against its existence, the author now moves toward the formal tools of the discipline: definition and division. These were essential components of the "Organon" or the logical toolkit used by ancient and medieval scholars to categorize all knowledge.</meta>

For he who does not know the definition misses all those things that naturally follow from it. For example, if someone wants to know what medicine is, but does not first know the nature of medicine—that it concerns the human body—and instead thinks it concerns wood, then whatever words he speaks regarding medicine are in vain.

As for division, <unclear>Istiwates</unclear> <note>Likely a corruption of a Greek name, perhaps referring to Plato or Aristotle; the following quote regarding Prometheus and division appears in Plato's *Philebus*</note> mentions it in his logic, saying that by means of the method of division, nothing can boast of escaping; for nothing escapes from division.

For when we say that among living beings, some are rational and some are irrational, there is nothing that exists outside of these; for there is no living being that is not either rational or irrational. Again, he himself says in that same logic regarding division: "this division was granted to us by Prometheus <note>original: "պռոփիթեայ" (Prophit’ea)</note> by means of the heavenly fire." And one must know that those who tell myths say that Prometheus once stole fire from the gods and gave it to humans; which is an allegory. For first and foremost, Prometheus discovered the method of division. For the method of division is compared to fire; and because of this, they say he granted fire to humans.

And that the method of division is compared to fire is evident from this: just as fire separates the homogeneous <note>original: "հոմատեռն" (homatern) – things of the same kind or species</note>, the heterogeneous <note>original: "այլասեռն" (aylasern) – things of different kinds or genera</note>, and the congeneric <note>original: "համասեռն" (hamasern) – things belonging to the same general category</note>. For example, the fusibles <term>fusibles: materials that can be melted, specifically metals</term>, namely silver, gold, tin, copper, and lead. For these are congeneric, since they share the genus of "fusibles," and they are separated from each other by fire. Indeed, by fire, silver is separated from gold, and lead from copper.

---

<page-num>9</page-num>

And as for the heterogeneous: for example, when we say that by means of fire, dross <note>original: "ժանգն" (zhangn) – literally "rust," but here meaning impurities or slag</note> is separated from silver and from copper. In this way, division is naturally compared to fire, separating the homogeneous and the heterogeneous.

And as for the homogeneous: for example, when we say that among living beings, some are rational and some are irrational; these are congeneric because they share a common genus.

And as for the heterogeneous: for example, when by means of division, we separate the number ten in grammar from other different kinds. For grammar is a general art that falls under quality <term>quality: in ancient logic, a category describing the nature or properties of a thing</term>, whereas the number ten belongs to arithmetic, which falls under quantity <term>quantity: in ancient logic, a category describing the amount, size, or number of things</term>.

Now, this being so, let us come and define philosophy and perform its division. But we must examine which should be done first: to define or to divide?

And one must know that it is necessary first to define and then to divide, for this reason: because definition refers to the singular <note>original: "միակ" (miak) – the one, unity, or the individual concept</note>, while division refers to multiplicity. Now, just as the singular is prior to multiplicity, in the same way, definition—which is compared to the singular—is prior to division, which is compared to multiplicity.

And that definition is compared to the singular and division to multiplicity is evident from this: just as the singular is a single thing and a single nature, likewise the definition makes many things into one thing and completes a single nature. For "man"... <note>The text cuts off as it begins to define 'man' as a singular concept made of many attributes</note>

<summary>This page explains the logical methods of definition and division, using the myth of Prometheus's fire as an allegory for the power of logic to separate and categorize different types of knowledge. It argues that definition must always precede division because unity (the definition of a thing) logically comes before multiplicity (the division of a genus into species).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of logical methodology, specifically the transition from the process of "division"—which splits broad categories into specifics—to the process of "definition." The previous page concluded with the allegory of Prometheus as the discoverer of division, comparing the analytical process to fire that separates elements. This page applies that logic to the definition of "man" and outlines a structured curriculum for understanding the art of definition itself.</meta>

...living, rational, mortal, and capable of mind and intellect. The universal man is complete in a single nature; but division, by contrast, splits the one into many and into the infinite. For it takes substance and divides it into the corporeal and the incorporeal; and the corporeal into the internal and the external, and likewise for the others in their order.

Now let us come and define philosophy; however, the ability to follow a logical sequence <note>original: "հետեւաբանութի" (hetewabanut'iun) – literally the 'science of following,' referring to logical consequence or syllogism</note> is not possible unless we first learn what a logical sequence is and how it arises. Likewise, it is impossible to define anything unless one first learns what a definition is. Therefore, knowing this, we shall arrange some nine chapters by number:

<margin>1</margin> First, what is a definition?
<margin>2</margin> Second, in which we say how a definition differs from a "definitive description" and a "descriptive definition" <note>This refers to the technical distinction between an essential definition (what a thing is) and a description (how it appears or its properties)</note>.
<margin>3</margin> Third, from where the term "definition" is derived.
<margin>4</margin> Fourth, from where a definition is taken <note>The "topics" or sources from which one derives the components of a definition</note>.
<margin>5</margin> Fifth, in which we say which is a perfect definition and which is imperfect, and what is a "vicious" and what is a "healthy" definition.
<margin>6</margin> Sixth, in which we say how many definitions of philosophy there are.
<margin>7</margin> Seventh, in which we say that these many definitions of philosophy are sufficient—neither fewer nor more.
<margin>8</margin> Eighth, in which we say their order.
<margin>9</margin> Ninth, in which we say who the discoverers of these definitions are.

# Along with these things. And here
## begins the Lectures. Third Lecture.

Let us proceed to the first chapter: what is a definition? A definition is a concise statement revealing the nature of the subject matter. But because we are about to learn that every definition is naturally composed of a genus <note>original: "սեռ" (seṙ)</note> and of constitutive differences <note>original: "բաղկացուցիչ զանազանութեանց" (bałkacʻucʻičʻ zanazanutʻeancʻ) – the specific traits that distinguish a species within a genus</note>, let us say how much this present definition corresponds to a genus. These same things are those [composed] of constitutive differences; but so that this teaching may be certain for us, let us take the definition of man as an example. And one must know that man is a living, rational, mortal [being], capable of mind and intellect.
<insert><image-desc>A small marginal circle on the right containing the Armenian letter Գ (Gim, the numeral 3) with decorative strokes above and below, marking the third lecture.</image-desc></insert>
Now, here "living [being]" was taken in place of the genus; for it is common and applies to many: to man, and to dog, and to horse, and to others of this kind. But the other words are joined in place of constitutive differences. Now, in the same way, in the present definition, "statement" <note>original: "բան" (ban) – translates the Greek 'logos,' meaning speech, reason, or statement</note> was taken in place of the genus; for it is common and applies to many: for it applies to the musical [statement] and to the other, the grammatical [statement]. But the other words come joined in place of constitutive differences. Now let us come and relate the parts of the definition. And one must know that he said "statement" for the purpose of distinguishing it from a "name"; for the name also defines the nature of the subject, such as "man," and that which the definition [reveals] by means of...

<meta>This page continues the curriculum on the art of definition. Having established the nine chapters of study on the previous page, the author now begins with the first chapter: defining what a "definition" itself is, and the second chapter: distinguishing a definition from a description.</meta>

...takes many words and makes them into this single name <note>The author is explaining that a definition acts like a single name for a complex set of concepts</note>. Therefore, the opposite is also said: for if a name is called a concise definition, then a definition is an expanded name. Now, how shall the name be distinguished? For this reason, he calls it a "speech" <note>original: "բան" (ban) — This can mean word, speech, reason, or proposition. In logic, it refers to the "logos" or the verbal expression of a concept</note>. And truly, whatever a name does, a speech does. And since both name and definition reveal the nature of the **underlying thing** <term>underlying thing: "ենթակայ իր" (enthaka ir), the subject or substrate being discussed</term>, why was the definition conceived?

We say it is for the sake of our knowing the **constitutive differences** <term>constitutive differences: "բաղկացուցիչ զանազանութիւն" (baghkats’uts’ich’ zanazanut’iun), the essential traits that make a thing what it is</term>; that is, those things that constitute the underlying thing for us. Often we say "man" alone, and we do not know the constitutive differences. But if we say "man: living, rational, mortal, capable of mind and knowledge," we then know the constitutive differences.

Now, it was called "concise" <note>This refers to a specific formal definition of "definition" as a "concise speech"</note> to distinguish it from the extended speeches of the orators, such as the *Speech on the Crown* <note>original: "յաղագս պսակի" (yaghags psaki) — A reference to the famous oration by Demosthenes</note>, or a historical narrative. For those are also called "speeches," but they are not concise. 

Furthermore, he said it "reveals the nature of the underlying thing" because of those famous "exclamations" <note>original: "բացագանջմանցն" (bats’aganch’mants’n) — Here referring to maxims or proverbs</note>, such as "**Know thyself**" and "**Nothing in excess**" <note>These are the famous Delphic maxims of ancient Greece</note>. For these are called speeches and they are concise, yet they do not reveal the nature of the underlying thing. 

It was also said to distinguish it from **descriptions** <term>description: "ստորագրութիւն" (storagrut'iun), a sketch of a thing based on its appearance rather than its essence</term>. For descriptions do not reveal the nature of the underlying thing, but rather the circumstances of that nature and the things that follow by accident. For example, when we say that "man is upright-walking, capable of laughter, and broad-nailed." For these things reveal the circumstances of the nature, and not the nature itself. Thus, these are the matters of the first chapter.

<column-break/>

Let us come to the second chapter and state how a definition differs from a description. 

It must be known that a definition differs from a description by this: a definition is taken from **substantial** <term>substantial: "գոյացական" (goyats’akan), pertaining to the essence or "ousia" of a thing</term> terms, and it reveals the substance and the essence of the underlying thing. For example: "man is a living, rational, mortal being, capable of mind and genius." 

In contrast, a description is taken from **accidents** <term>accidents: "պատահմունք" (patahmunk'), properties that a thing can lose without ceasing to be what it is, like color or height</term>, and it reveals the circumstances surrounding the underlying thing. For example: "man is upright-walking, broad-nailed, and capable of laughter." 

Since we have said that a definition is taken from substantial words and a description from accidents, let us learn what "substantial" is and what an "accident" is. 

It must be known that the substantial is that which, by being present, constitutes the thing, and by not being present, destroys it. For example: **rationality**. For this, by being present, constitutes the man; but by not being present, it destroys him <note>Meaning that if you remove "rationality" from the definition of a man, the definition no longer describes a human being</note>. 

An accident, however, is that which, by being present, does not constitute the thing, and by being removed, does not destroy it. For example: **whiteness**. For whiteness, by being present, does not constitute the thing, and by being removed, does not destroy it <note>A man remains a man regardless of his skin color</note>. 

Having said these things, let us move further and say how a definition differs from a **definitive description** <note>original: "սահմանադրութիւն" (sahmanadrut'iun) — A technical term for a hybrid logical statement</note>. And it must be known that as...

<summary>This page explains the logical distinction between a "definition" (based on essential characteristics like rationality) and a "description" (based on accidental traits like being broad-nailed). It defines substance as that which constitutes a thing's existence and accidents as non-essential properties.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical curriculum on the art of definition. The previous page established that a definition is a concise speech revealing the nature of an underlying subject. Here, the author distinguishes between the specific "definition" and the more general "term" or "determination," and then begins to explain the difference between a strict "essential definition" and a "descriptive definition."</meta>

Man and animal differ from one another as the universal and the particular. Because "man" is more particular than "animal," while "animal" is more universal. This is because whatever is a man is also an animal—since every man is an animal. But not everything that is an animal is also a man; for not every animal is a man. Indeed, not only is man an animal, but so are the horse and the mule. In the same way, <term>definition</term> <note>original: "սահման" (sahman)</note> and <term>determination</term> <note>original: "սահմանադրութիւն" (sahmanadrut’iun) — This term refers to the "laying down of a boundary" or a "term" in a logical proposition</note> differ from one another as universal and particular. For a definition is more particular, and a determination is more universal. Because whatever is a definition is also a determination, but not every determination is also a definition. For "determination" does not only signify a definition—that is, the concise speech revealing the nature of the underlying thing—but it also refers to the determination of places, villages, and fields. According to Aristotle, it also refers to those parts into which a proposition is analyzed. For Aristotle says that a "term" (determination) is that into which a proposition is analyzed. For example, "Socrates walks." This is a proposition, and it is analyzed into "Socrates" and "walks." And each of these, as Aristotle says, is called a determination; that is, both "Socrates" and "walks."

<column-break/>

Now, as we said, definition and determination differ from one another according to the universal and the particular. But many times, people also call a definition a "determination," having become accustomed to using the universal name. Just as we often call a man by the generic name "animal." Now that we have learned how a definition differs from a determination, let us come to explain how a definition differs from a <term>descriptive definition</term> <note>original: "ստորագրական սահման" (storagrakan sahman) — Also known as a "description." While a definition uses essential traits, a description uses observable qualities or "accidents"</note>. And it is necessary to know that a definition differs from a descriptive definition in this way: a definition is of a single kind, and it is taken from <term>essential</term> <note>original: "գոյացողական" (goyats’oghakan) — Pertaining to the substance or "essence" of what a thing is</note> terms only. For example: "Man: animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and knowledge." But a descriptive definition is mixed, for it is taken from both essentials and <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "պատահմանց" (patahmants’) — Non-essential characteristics that happen to be true of a subject but do not define its species</note>. And one may recognize a composition that is descriptive in this way; for example, when we say: "Man: animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and knowledge, **upright-walking**, **broad-nailed**, and **capable of laughter**." And it is necessary to know that to be a true definition, all the words must be essential. For example: "Man: animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and knowledge." But to be a descriptive definition, it is enough if even one word—

It then distinguishes between strict definitions based on essence and descriptive definitions that include accidental traits like "broad-nailed."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical curriculum on the art of definition, following the previous discussion on how a definition reveals the nature of a subject. It specifically addresses the hierarchical relationship between a definition and a logical "term" or "determination," and then transitions into the distinction between essential and descriptive definitions.</meta>

Man and animal differ from one another as the universal and the particular. Because "man" is more particular than "animal," while "animal" is more universal. This is because whatever is a man is also an animal—since every man is an animal. But not everything that is an animal is also a man; for not every animal is a man. Indeed, not only is man an animal, but so are the horse and the mule. In the same way, <term>definition</term> <note>original: "սահման" (sahman)</note> and <term>determination</term> <note>original: "սահմանադրութիւն" (sahmanadrut’iun) — in logic, this refers to a "term" or the "laying down" of a boundary</note> differ from one another as the universal and the particular. For a definition is more particular, and a determination is more universal. Because whatever is a definition is also a determination, but not every determination is also a definition. For "determination" does not only signify a definition—that is, the concise speech revealing the nature of the underlying thing—but it also refers to the determination of boundaries for places, lands, and fields. According to Aristotle, it also refers to those parts into which a proposition is analyzed. For Aristotle says that a "determination" <note>Aristotle refers to these as 'horos' or 'terms'</note> is that into which a proposition is analyzed. For example, "Socrates walks." This is a proposition, and it is analyzed into "Socrates" and "walks." And each of these, as Aristotle says, is called a determination; that is, both "Socrates" and "walks."

<column-break/>

Now, as we said, according to the more universal and the more particular, definition and determination differ from one another. But many times, people call a definition a "determination," making use of the more universal name. Just as we often call a "man" by the generic name "animal." Now that we have learned how a definition differs from a determination, come, let us stand and state how an [essential] definition differs from a <term>descriptive definition</term> <note>original: "ստորագրական սահման" (stvoragrakan sahman) — equivalent to the Latin 'descriptio' or a definition based on accidental properties rather than essence</note>. It must be known that a definition differs from a descriptive definition in this way: a definition is of a single kind, and it is derived from <term>essential terms</term> <note>original: "գոյացողականաց ձայնիցն" (goyatsoghalakanats dzaynitsn) — literally "substantial sounds," referring to words that describe the core essence</note> only. For example: "Man is an animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and intellect." But a descriptive definition is "mixed," for it is taken from both essentials and <term>accidents</term> <note>original: "պատահմանց" (patahmants) — properties that can change without changing the nature of the thing, like being "tall" or "laughing"</note>. And so that one might verify a [description] composed of both a definition and descriptive elements, consider this: when we say, "Man is an animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and intellect, upright-walking, broad-nailed <note>This refers to the classical Aristotelian description of man as a "featherless biped with broad nails."</note>, and capable of laughter." And it must be known that to be a [strict] definition, every word must be essential, such as: "Man is an animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and intellect." But to be a descriptive definition, even one [accidental] word is enough...

<summary>This page distinguishes between a specific "definition" and a general "term" (determination) in logic, using Aristotle's analysis of propositions. It also explains the difference between an essential definition (based on nature) and a descriptive definition (which includes accidental traits like being "broad-nailed").</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical curriculum on the art of definition, following the previous discussion on how a definition reveals the nature of a subject through essential parts. It uses analogies from music and medicine to explain how a single change can alter a definition.</meta>

...to accept a change by revealing an <term>accident</term> <note>original: "պատաՀումն" (patahum) — in logic, a non-essential property like "white" or "sitting," as opposed to an essential "substance."</note>.
For example: man is an animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and genius, and walking upright. 
Likewise, to perfect the tuning of a lyre, it is necessary for all the strings to be well-adjusted; but to ruin the tuning, it is enough to change [even] one string. In the same way, to have health, there must be a moderation of <term>humors</term> <note>original: "խառնուածոցն" (khaṛnuatsots'n) — referring to the four bodily fluids believed to govern health in medieval medicine</note> and a natural composition of the parts. But to have illness, it is enough to change one humor, one part not being according to nature. 
In the same way, for a <term>definition</term> <note>original: "սահման" (sahman)</note> to exist, all the words must be essential [substantial], as we said; but to be a <term>descriptive definition</term> <note>original: "ստորադրական սահման" (storadrakan sahman) — also known as a "description" (hypographe in Greek), which describes a thing by its accidental properties rather than its essence</note>, it is enough to change just one word. 
And so it is. **B**ut some raise a doubt regarding the first chapter, saying: "You have made the definition circular by explaining a definition through a definition." For we require from you a definition of a definition, and of that definition another definition, and so on until it leads to infinity, and we still will not know what a definition is. 
And some answer, saying: "We did not define it as a definition [in the abstract], but as something definable—that is, as the subject [underlying thing]." To these it should be said that the doubt remains no less; for we say to you: explain the definition of that which is defined, and then the definition of the next definition

<column-break/>

explain its definition as something definable, which similarly would be an endless process. 
**B**ut it should be said thus: just as with measures and numbers, there are some that are self-measuring and self-numbering, and others that are measured or numbered by something else. They are self-numbering and self-measuring according to the <term>units</term> <note>original: "եզականացն" (ezakanats'n) — referring to the 'ones' or individuals that make up a sum</note> within them. For ten numbers itself according to the units within it. 
**B**ut it numbers others, such as twenty and thirty—twenty by two [tens] and thirty by three [tens]. 
In the same way, a definition is both a "self-definition" and an "other-definition." For the same thing defines other definitions, but it is a "self-definition" because it is included among the things defined. 
For example, a man, in defining "man," says that man is an animal, rational, mortal, and capable of mind and knowledge. He defines not only all men, but also himself; for he himself is defined [by that definition]. 
**B**ut some raise a doubt regarding the second chapter, saying: "Since substance is more honorable than accident, and a definition is taken for substances, while a description is for accidents, why do we call it a 'descriptive definition' <note>literally "sub-posed definition"</note>, placing the lesser [description] before the greater [definition], and not a 'definitional description'?" 
**A**nd some answer, saying: "Why, according to the <term>Grammarian</term> <note>original: "քերթողին" (k’ert’oghin) — likely referring to the school of Armenian commentators on Dionysius Thrax</note>, do they say 'half-ass' [mule]?" Because the Grammarian...

<summary>This page explains that a definition requires all its parts to be essential, using analogies from music and medicine. It also defends the logic of "self-definition" against the charge of infinite regress, arguing that a definition can define itself just as the number ten is composed of its own units.</summary>

<keywords>logic, definition, accident, substance, man, health, humors, self-definition, grammarian, analogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical exploration of definitions, transitioning from the grammatical debate over the term "descriptive definition" to the etymological origins of the word "definition" itself, drawing on the works of Aristotle and traditional Armenian logic.</meta>

Homer says *kise* <note>original: "կիսէյ" (kisēy) — an archaic or phonetic reference used in Armenian grammatical tradition to discuss the formation of superlatives</note>: the worse prevails in the composition of names: the worse is better. For by saying *kise* for "bad," and calling it "worst," he terms it *kisame*. For this reason, we also use the term **descriptive definition**, adding the superlative degree to it. This is their view. But it must be said: although this is the traditional defense, those who raised doubts did not doubt correctly. First and foremost, because none of the ancient teachers said that one *must* say "definitional description" instead of "**descriptive definition**." Second, even if we say "descriptive definition" or "definitional description," the intended meaning is not corrupted. For **Aristotle** says that names and verbs, when transposed, signify the same thing. Because they signify the same, whether one says "Socrates walks" or "Walks Socrates." Alongside these, the Gospel stands...

# Lectures. Lectures. Fourth.

<margin>
4
---
3
</margin>
Let us begin the third chapter, which asks: **"From where is the word definition derived?"** One must know that "definition" is a metaphor taken from villages and field boundaries. For the ancients, fleeing from the immeasurability of both sides—

<column-break/>

encroachment and lack—established the setting of boundaries, so that they might enjoy their own property and keep away from that of others. In the same way, a <term>definition</term> <note>original: "սահման" (sahman) — in Armenian, this word literally means "boundary" or "limit," as well as "definition."</note>, by circumscribing the underlying subject, distinguishes it from all others. 

Take, for example, the definition of man: "an animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and knowledge." By saying "animal," it separates him from those that are not animals—that is, the inanimate and the soulless. By saying "rational," it distinguishes him from the irrational. By "mortal," from the immortal. By saying "capable of mind and genius," it distinguishes him from invisible and mortal creatures known as **"eternal nymphs"** <note>In the Neoplatonic tradition followed by Armenian philosophers, these were intermediary beings who possessed innate knowledge without needing to learn.</note>, which possess knowledge by nature rather than by learning; for only man was naturally formed to receive knowledge through the active intellect. 

It is also necessary to know that in a definition, words and <term>extension</term> <note>original: "կիրք" (kirk') — literally "affects" or "passions," but here used in a logical sense to refer to the range of things a term covers.</note> are in inverse proportion to each other. For when a definition increases in words, the scope of things it describes decreases. For example: "man is an animal, rational, mortal, capable of mind and knowledge, and a **poet**." Because "poet" was an addition to the definition, it decreased the scope of the subject; for it defined only the poet and not every man, since not every man is a poet. Behold, it increased...

<note>Vocabulary used: Homer, Aristotle, Socrates, Definition, Lectures, Rational Animal, Mortal, Nature</note>

<summary>This page explains the etymology of the word "definition" as a metaphor for land boundaries and discusses the logical principle that adding specific terms to a definition narrows its scope.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Socrates, definition, etymology, logic, descriptive definition, extension, human nature, philosophy of language</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of definitions. Having discussed the etymology of "definition" as a boundary on the previous page, the author now examines the inverse relationship between the number of words in a definition and the number of things it describes, before transitioning into the fourth chapter on the "sources" of definitions.</meta>

the words are fewer than the things. But when
the words decrease, the things increase. For example:
"rational animal." Here, as the words decrease, the things increase;
for it is not only man who is a "rational animal,"
but also angels and demons. And as **Olympiodorus** <note>original: "Ուլոմպիադորոս" — a 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher and commentator on Aristotle whose works were influential in the Armenian school of logic.</note> the philosopher says:
Nature found a certain wonderful way to be enriched
by poverty, and to feign poverty through richness.
But if someone should doubt, saying: "How
can you say that when a definition increases in
words, the things decrease? For behold, I say:
'Man is a rational, mortal animal,
capable of mind and knowledge by mortality.'"
The words increased, but the things did not decrease,
for it still encompassed every human. We say
that the added words signified nothing more;
for "mortal" and "by mortality" are the same—
since "by mortality" is the addition of a verb
and not a noun, and a <term>definition</term> <note>original: "սահման" (sahman)</note> is recognized by
nouns and not by verbs. This concludes the third chapter.

<margin><insert>4</insert></margin> But let us come to the fourth chapter and let us
speak of from where a definition is taken. And
one must know that definitions are taken either from the <term>subject</term> <note>original: "ենթակայ" (enthakay) — literally "that which lies under," referring to the material or the topic being acted upon.</note>,
or from the <term>completion</term> <note>original: "կատարումն" (katarumn) — referring to the goal, end, or "telos" of a thing.</note>, or from both—
that is, from the subject and the completion—

<column-break/>

But first, let us say what the "subject" is and
what the "completion" is. The **subject** is that in which
something occurs and through which it acts, whether it be an art or a
knowledge. But the **completion** is that toward which it looks
to take shape, according to which it does everything.
For example, in carpentry, the subject is wood; for
carpentry exists for the sake of the wood. But its completion
is to make a chair or to build a temple. And again,
astronomy has the heavenly
bodies as its subject, but its completion is not to make
something like them, but to know their movement.

Similarly, **philosophy** <note>original: "իմաստասիրութիւն" (imastasirut‘iwn)</note> also has a subject
and a completion. Its subject is all beings,
and its completion is the knowledge of them; that is, to
know them and through them to become like God. For
the philosopher is like God, as **Pythagoras** <note>original: "Պիւթակորոս" (Pythagoras)</note>
reveals, speaking to **Lycurgus** <note>original: "Լիկոռգիաս" (Lycurgus) — the legendary lawgiver of Sparta.</note> the lawgiver
and philosopher—for the first of the philosophers
were the lawgivers. "You have come, O Lycurgus,
to my rich temple in doubt. What
shall I call you: a god or a man? But
rather, I shall call you a god, O Lycurgus." <note>This is a famous quote from the Delphic Oracle greeting Lycurgus, used here to illustrate the philosophical goal of "becoming like God."</note>
By saying this, it was not that he did not know what he ought to call
him, but he showed that he was a partner with God by nature
and by philosophy. Hence he also brings up—

<summary>The author explains the logical rule that fewer descriptive words apply to more beings (extension) and then begins Chapter 4, defining the "subject" (material) and "completion" (goal) of various disciplines, culminating in philosophy's goal of becoming like God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of definitions, transitioning from the relationship between words and things to the divine likeness of the philosopher and the sources of definition.</meta>

...but yet, I shall call you a god, O Lycurgus. <note>original: "լիկոյերգես" (Likoyerges) — a reference to the Pythian Oracle's greeting to the Spartan lawgiver Lycurgus, as recorded by Herodotus. The author uses this to show how a human can be likened to a deity.</note> And he likens the perfect philosopher to God. It is clear that God is characterized by those attributes by which He exists; for the same God is characterized by goodness, by knowledge, and by power. Just as poetry also reveals regarding goodness: "The gods are givers of good things." <note>original: "աստուածք տուիչք բարեացեն" — a common Homeric epithet for the gods.</note> And regarding knowledge: "The gods know all things." And regarding power: "The gods can do all things."

In the same manner, the perfect <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: "փիլիսոփոս" (pʻilisopʻos)</note> is characterized by these three: by goodness, by knowledge, and by power. And by goodness: just as God is the provider for all, so also the <term>philosopher</term> <note>original: "իմաստասէրն" (imastasērn) — literally "wisdom-lover," used interchangeably with philosopher.</note> provides for imperfect souls, leading them toward perfect knowledge. And by knowledge: just as God knows all things, so also the perfect philosopher promises to know all things, and he knows those things which are useful. And by power: just as God can do what is most fitting as He wills, so also the perfect philosopher, as much as he is able according to his capacity, he also wills.

But yet, in the case of God, this is reversible; <note>The author is discussing logical reciprocity: if God wills it, He can do it; and if He can do it, He wills it (in the context of perfect providence).</note> for as much as He wills, He is also able, and as much as He is able, He also wills. But in the case of the philosopher, it is not reversible; for as much as he is able, he wills, but not as much as he wills, is he able.

<meta>catchword: For</meta>

<column-break/>

For if he wishes to touch the vault <note>original: "կորնթարթն" (korntʻartʻn) — referring to the arc or rotation of the heavens.</note> of heaven with his finger, he is not able to touch it. Because of this, some answer by saying: "How can you say that the philosopher is characterized by power to be like God, since [this power] is reversible in the case of God, but not in the case of the philosopher, as you said?" To which we say: the perfect philosopher does not wish to desire the unattainable. For even among the Stoics, <note>original: "տոկոսեան" (tokosean) — likely a scribal error for "Ստոիկեան" (Stoikean).</note> the absence of desires is a great sufficiency.

But it must be known that it will be shown later that "goodness," "knowledge," and "power" are understood in one way regarding God, and in another way regarding the philosopher. 

But now it must be known that definitions are taken from the <term>subject</term> <note>original: "ենթակայ" (enthakay) — the underlying material or topic.</note>, or from the <term>completion</term> <note>original: "կատարումն" (katarumn) — the final cause or end goal.</note>, or from both; that is, from the subject and from the completion. 

And from the subject, when we say: that <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "բժշկականութի" (bžškakanutʻi)</note> is an <term>art</term> <note>original: "արհեստ" (arhest) — used here in the sense of a technical craft or 'techne'.</note> concerning the nature of the human body. 

And from the completion: that medicine is the producer of health; for the completion of medicine is either to preserve existing health or to restore that which has been lost. 

And from both, as when we join both these definitions and say: that medicine is an art concerning the human body, the producer of health. And not in this way...

<summary>The author compares the attributes of the perfect philosopher (goodness, knowledge, power) to those of God, while noting human limitations. He then transitions to explaining how definitions are formed from a subject, its completion, or a combination of both, using medicine as an example.</summary>

<keywords>philosopher, divine likeness, Lycurgus, divine attributes, Stoics, definition, medicine, art, subject, completion, logic</keywords>

because we find the definition of natural things
taken from the subject and the fulfillment,
as in the case of <term>anger</term> <note>original: "բարկութիւն" (barkut'iwn) — a standard example in Aristotelian logic.</note>. For anger
is something natural, and yet we define
it from the subject and the fulfillment,
saying that anger is the boiling of the blood around the heart,
for the sake of the desire to return
sorrow <note>That is, the desire for revenge or to grieve the person who caused the anger.</note>. And look: the boiling of the
blood around the heart is from the subject; for
anger has as its <unclear>subject</unclear> the blood around the heart. While
the desire to return sorrow is from the
fulfillment; for the fulfillment of anger is the
desiring to return sorrow to the one
who caused sorrow. But enough regarding these things.
However, it is necessary to say that we correctly say
that every definition is from the genus and the constitutive differ- <note>The text cuts off here at the end of the page; the word would likely complete as "differences."</note>

<summary>This page explores the logical structure of definitions, debating whether they should be derived from a thing's substance (genus and difference) or its purpose and material cause. Using the examples of philosophy, the nature of man, and the emotion of anger, the author argues that natural things can be defined by both their physical components and their final goals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the logic of definitions. It transitions from explaining how definitions are composed of a genus and specific differences to examining the "convertibility" of a definition—the logical test of whether a definition applies only to the thing being defined and nothing else.</meta>

...to take [definitions] <note>The text continues from the previous page's discussion on how to derive a definition from both the subject matter and the final purpose.</note>. For those [definitions] taken from the <term>subject</term> <note>original: "յենթակայէ" (yent’akayē) — the material or topic being addressed.</note> and from the <term>fulfillment</term> <note>original: "ի կատարմանէ" (i katarmanē) — the goal or end result.</note>—from both of these—they possess both a <term>genus</term> <note>original: "սեռ" (seṛ) — the broad category a thing belongs to.</note> and <term>constituent differences</term> <note>original: "բաղկացուցիչ զանազանութիւնս" (bałkats’uts’ich zanazanut’iwns) — the specific traits that distinguish a thing from others in its genus.</note>. For instance: "Medicine is an art concerning the human body." Here, "art" was taken in place of the genus, while the other words were taken in place of the constituent differences. And again: "Medicine is the art that produces health." Here, "art" was taken in place of the genus, while the other words were taken in place of the constituent differences. And again: "Medicine is the art concerning the human body that produces health." Here, "art" was taken in place of the genus, and the other words in place of the constituent differences. And again: "Philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human realities." Here, "knowledge" was taken in place of the genus, while the other words were taken in place of the constituent differences. This concludes the fourth chapter. <margin>† 42</margin> Now, the fifth chapter, in which we ask which is a <term>perfect definition</term> and which is imperfect. And one must know that a perfect definition is one that is <term>convertible</term> <note>original: "անդրադարձի" (andradardzi) — a logical term meaning the definition and the subject can swap places and still be true.</note> with the thing being defined. For example, when we say: "Whatever is a human is a living, rational, mortal being, capable of mind and intellect."

<column-break/>

[And conversely], whatever is a living, rational, mortal being, capable of mind and intellect, that is a human. But an <term>imperfect definition</term> is one that is not convertible. For in the study of definitions, failing to convert happens because of a "fault" in the definition—either through <term>excess</term> <note>original: "յառաւելութենէ" (yaṛawelut’enē) — adding too much detail that doesn't apply to all members of the group.</note> or through <term>deficiency</term> <note>original: "ի նուազութենէ" (i nwuazut’enē) — leaving out essential traits.</note>. For a definition cannot be converted with the thing defined if there is excess or deficiency. For example: "Man is a living, rational, mortal being, capable of mind and knowledge, [and is] a grammarian." Because this [definition] has "excess," it is not convertible. For while whoever is a living, rational, mortal being capable of mind and knowledge and is a grammarian is a man, it is not true that every man is a living, rational, mortal being capable of mind and knowledge *and* a grammarian; for not every man is a grammarian. Furthermore, it is not convertible when there is "deficiency" regarding the thing defined. For example: "Man is a living, rational being." Being deficient, this does not convert. For it is not only man who is a living, rational being, but <insert>as some say</insert> angels are as well. And so it is with these. But one must ask: which of the previously mentioned definitions are the "perfect" ones? And one must know that those are perfect which take from both [aspects]—the subject and the fulfillment. For example: "Carpentry is an art concerning wood for the creation of a chair." But those... <note>The text cuts off, leading to the next page.</note>

<summary>This page explains the logical structure of a "perfect definition," focusing on the principle of convertibility—where a definition must accurately and exclusively describe its subject without being too specific (excess) or too broad (deficiency).</summary>
