<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of definitions within David the Invincible’s "Definitions of Philosophy." The previous page established that a definition is composed of a genus (the category) and constituent differences (the specific traits). Here, the text moves into the concept of "convertibility"—the idea that a perfect definition must apply to the thing defined and *only* to that thing.</meta>

...from the subject alone or from the fulfillment alone, many and most of them are not perfect <note>In this context, a "perfect" definition is "convertible." This means the definition and the term being defined are interchangeable. For example, if "Man is a rational mortal animal," then any "rational mortal animal" must be a "man."</note>. For instance, when we say that "Medicine is an art concerning the human body," it is not convertible <note>original: "ոչ անդրադարձի" (och andradardzi) — literally "does not turn back." A logical term meaning the statement cannot be reversed.</note>. For though everything that is medicine concerns the human body, not everything that concerns the human body is medicine. This is because medicine is not the only thing that exists regarding the human body; there is also the art of <term>ointment-making</term> <note>original: "սպիողականն" (spioghakann) — refers to the preparation of plasters or healing unguents.</note> and the art of <term>anatomy</term> <note>original: "կենդանահատողականն" (kendanahatoghakann) — literally "the cutting of living things."</note>. 

Again, when we speak of the fulfillment, if we say that "Rhetoric is the producer of persuasion," it is not convertible. For while all that is rhetorical is the production of persuasion, not everything that produces persuasion is rhetorical. This is because rhetoric is not the only producer of persuasion; <term>logical philosophy</term> <note>original: "տրամաբանական իմաստասիրութիւն" (tramabanakan imastasirut’iwn) — referring to dialectics or formal logic, which also aims to convince through truth.</note> also produces persuasion. 

And again: "Anger is the boiling of the blood around the heart." This is not convertible. For while everything that is anger is the boiling of the blood around the heart, not everything that is a boiling of blood around the heart is anger. This is because the boiling of blood around the heart can also be caused by a fever <note>original: "ջերմութենէ" (chermut’enē) — heat or fever.</note>. But as we said above, many and most...

<column-break/>

...which [definitions are derived] from the subject alone or from the fulfillment are not perfect. This is why we placed the phrase "many and most" at the beginning. 

For there is indeed an art that is perfect [when defined] from the subject alone, such as <term>glass-making</term> <note>original: "ապակեգործութիւն" (apakēgortsut’iwn).</note>. For that alone is perfect from the subject alone; because everything that is glass-making exists for the sake of glass, and [everything regarding] glass is glass-making.

However, the definitions of philosophy, whether taken from the subject alone or from the fulfillment, are all perfect. And the reason why only the definitions of philosophy are perfect from the subject alone or from the fulfillment is this: because the <term>subject</term> <note>The "subject" (yent’akay) refers to the material or reality that the field of study examines.</note> of philosophy—that is, <gloss>universal</gloss> beings—cannot be the subject of any other art. This is because none of the other arts are able to recognize and know universal beings. 

And the <term>fulfillment</term> <note>The "fulfillment" (katarumn) refers to the end goal or purpose of the discipline.</note> of philosophy—that is, to become like God according to human capacity—is not the fulfillment of any other art. For no other art is able to make a human like God according to human capacity.

**Vocabulary:**
Medicine, Rhetoric, Philosophy, Logical, From the Subject, From the Fulfillment, Glass-making, David the Invincible, Definitions.

Along with all these teachings, having completed the fifth chapter, let us
->Lecture: Lecture: Five<-
seek the sixth, in which we state
how many and of what kind the definitions of philosophy are.
First, it must be known that there are six definitions of
philosophy, and they are as follows. First and foremost, those
who say that philosophy is the knowledge of beings
as they are. Second: the knowledge
of divine and human things.
Third, which says that philosophy is the meditation
on death <note>original: "խոկումն մահու" (khokumn mahu). This is a famous Platonic definition of philosophy as a preparation for the soul’s separation from the body.</note>. Fourth: those who say
that philosophy is becoming like God according to the
capacity of humans. Fifth: those who say that
philosophy is the art of arts and the
science of sciences <note>original: "արհեստ արհեստից և մակացութիւն մակացութեանց" (arhest arhestits yev makatsut’iwn makatsut’yants). This defines philosophy as the foundational discipline upon which all other practical skills and theoretical knowledge depend.</note>. Sixth: in addition
to these, those who say that philosophy is the love
of wisdom. Thus concludes the sixth
chapter. And in the seventh chapter, in which we state
why there were this many and these kinds of definitions of philosophy,
and why they are neither too many nor too few. For this, we provide
two reasons: one from division, and
the other from enumeration. And from division
[the argument is] as follows: There are some things that exist as substrates but are
not named, such as those that exist in the depths.

<column-break/>

For there are some things in the depths that have existence but
do not happen to have a name assigned to them because
they are not seen. And again, there are some things
named but without a substrate [existence], such as
myths and the <term>stag-goat</term> <note>original: "եղջերուաքաղն" (eghjeruakaghn). A classic example in ancient logic (equivalent to the Greek *tragelaphos*) of a creature that can be described and named but does not exist in reality.</note>, which are named but
do not exist as substrates. And again, there are some
which are both named and have a substrate, such as
man and eagle and so on, both defined and existing
as substrates. Now, philosophy is both
named and exists as a substrate; and why it has this [nature],
it has it according to the first category. Because philosophy
is the mother of wisdoms and arts and universal
sciences. For from it, all arts and
sciences take their premises, principles, and completion.
Just as the <term>geometer</term> <note>original: "երկրաչափն" (yerkrach’ap’n) — literally "earth-measurer."</note> accepts by
confession that a "point" is that which has no parts,
the cause of which the <term>natural philosopher</term> <note>original: "բնական իմաստասէրն" (bnakan imastasern). This refers to a philosopher who studies the physical world (physics), who provides the theoretical justification for the axioms used by practical scientists.</note> knows.
And again, the physician accepts by confession
that the body is composed of four elements, the
cause of which the natural philosopher knows.
And again, the <term>grammarian</term> <note>original: "քերթողն" (k’yert’oghn) — in this context, it refers to a scholar of literature or grammar who understands the rules of meter and language.</note> accepts by confession
that there are syllables long by nature; but the cause
the <term>musical philosopher</term> <note>original: "երաժշտական իմաստասէրն" (yerazhshtakan imastasern). Music was considered a branch of mathematics and philosophy involving the study of ratios and harmony.</note> knows. Therefore,
philosophy, as we have said, is both named
and has existence; and it possesses existence according to the first category.

<meta>The text continues the discussion of the definitions of philosophy, likely following a section on the etymology of the word. The catchword from the previous page is "form" or "shape."</meta>

...because it is named, and the name comes from the naming—that is, from the <term>etymology</term><note>original: "ստուգաբանութիւն" (stugabanut’yun), literally "true-word-telling" or etymology</note>—of that definition which says that **philosophy is the love of wisdom.** And because the primary tradition possesses existence, it possesses that definition which it has from its preeminence and its "outer-naming" <note>likely referring to a title or extrinsic definition</note> which says: **philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences.**

And because it has a subject, it has both a <term>subject matter</term><note>original: "ենթակայ" (ent’aka), literally "that which lies under," the Greek *hypokeimenon*</note> and a goal. For every art and every science has a subject matter and a goal. And each of these is twofold: for it has both a *proximate* subject and a *remote* one, and a *proximate* goal and a *remote* one.

So that what has been said may be certain to us, let us practice this argument upon the example of **shipbuilding**. It is necessary to know that shipbuilding has a subject matter and a goal, and each is twofold. It has a proximate subject and a remote one, and a proximate goal and a remote one.

The **proximate subject** is wood in general. But the **remote subject** is this specific kind of wood and its suitability for becoming the keel of the ship. For first and foremost, the shipbuilder takes wood in general, and then he seeks out which wood is suitable to become the keel, and he brings that which is suitable. And again, the **proximate goal** is to make a ship; but the **remote goal** is to make it for the sailors in such a way—perhaps in a "pointed" shape or a "lamp-like" shape.

<column-break/>

In the same way, **philosophy** also has a subject matter and a goal, and each is twofold: for it has a proximate subject and a remote subject, a proximate goal and a remote goal.

Now, the **proximate subject** is "beings" in general <note>all existing things</note>. But the **remote subject** is the extent of things—both **divine and human affairs**.

Again, the **proximate goal** is the **meditation on death** <note>original: "խոկումն մահու" (khokumn mahu), translating the Platonic *melete thanatou*</note>; that is, the deadening of the passions. But the **remote goal**, by means of the deadening of the passions, is **becoming like God** according to the capacity of human beings.

And now, because philosophy has a subject matter and a goal, and each is twofold as we have said; for this reason, and because its name comes from its preeminence, there are four more definitions:
1. Two from the **subject matter**: one from the proximate subject, which says that **philosophy is the knowledge of beings as they are beings.**
2. And the other from the remote subject, which they say is: **philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human affairs.**
3. And again, two from the **goal**: one from the proximate goal, which they say is: **philosophy is the meditation on death.**
4. And the other from the remote goal, which they say is...

It introduces four major definitions of philosophy based on its subject (beings, divine/human affairs) and its goals (meditation on death, becoming like God).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the definitions of philosophy, moving from the specific example of shipbuilding to the broader categorization of the six traditional definitions. It specifically focuses on why the number six was chosen, using Neopythagorean number theory as a justification, and begins to order the definitions based on their derivation (subject, goal, or etymology).</meta>

<margin>
(A small cross-like symbol is drawn here)
</margin>

**Philosophy is the likeness to God according to the ability of humans.** <note>This is one of the six classic definitions of philosophy used by David the Invincible, derived from Plato's *Theaetetus*.</note> And now this is the reason for the division: why there are six definitions of philosophy. But regarding the nature of the number, it is like this: among numbers, some are **perfect**, some are **imperfect**, and some are **superabundant**. 

A **perfect number** is one whose parts, when added together, are equal to the whole, such as **six**. For the parts of six, when added together, produce six; for it has a half, which is three; a third, which is two; and a sixth, which is one; which equals six. <note>In ancient arithmetic, "parts" refer to proper divisors. For 6, the divisors are 3, 2, and 1. 3+2+1=6.</note> For it does not take a fourth; because if it took a fourth, it would be necessary to divide the unit, since a fourth of six is one and a half. But arithmetic does not divide the unit, but rather proceeds only down to the unit. 

But **superabundance** in numbers is when the added parts are found to exceed the whole, such as **twelve**. For the parts of twelve added together result in the number sixteen; for it has a half, which is six; a third, which is four; and a fourth, which is three; which makes thirteen; and a sixth, which is two; and a twelfth, which is one; which equals sixteen. 

But **imperfection** is when the parts added together are found to be less than the whole: such as **eight**. For the parts of eight added together [make] the number seven

<column-break/>

result; for it has a half, which is four; a fourth, which is two; and an eighth, which is one; which equals seven. 

But now, since things are thus, it is fitting for the first perfect number, **six**—for the sages knew no other perfect number before it. For this reason, there are six definitions of philosophy. For it is necessary that every part of the nature of all arts, being most perfect, be adorned by a most perfect number. 

And these things remain with their abundance. 

**Lecture Sixth.**

(Small ornamental marks)
</margin>

Let us move to the eighth chapter and speak of the **ordering of the definitions** of philosophy. And it must be known that among the six definitions of philosophy, the very last is the one from <term>etymology</term><note>original: "ստուգաբանութենէն" (stugabanut’enēn)</note> which says that **philosophy is the love of wisdom.** 

But the last of the other five is the definition from <term>excellence</term><note>original: "յաղտաունութենէ" (yaght’aut’unene), referring to a definition based on the supremacy of the field</note> which says that **philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences.** 

But of the other four definitions, the first are from the <term>subject matter</term><note>original: "յենթակայէ" (yent’akaye), the Greek *hypokeimenon*</note> and the last are from the <term>goal</term><note>original: "ի կատարմանէ" (i katatmanē), the Greek *telos* or completion</note>. And of each of these, that which is from the **proximate subject** is the first, and that which is from the **remote subject** is the last. Likewise also those which are from the goal: the first is from the **proximate goal**, and the last is from the **remote [goal]**...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the six definitions of philosophy. Having previously established why the number six is "perfect" according to mathematical theory, the text now enumerates each definition and begins to explain their hierarchical arrangement. This curriculum is attributed to David the Invincible, a 6th-century Armenian Neoplatonist who studied in Alexandria.</meta>

...manner, of which the arrangement is as follows and according to 
<margin>1</margin> this model: The first definition of philosophy is from the proximate subject, <note>In late antique logic, a "subject" is the matter or "stuff" a discipline deals with. The "proximate" subject is what philosophy is immediately concerned with: beings.</note> which says that **philosophy is the knowledge of beings as they are.**

<margin>2</margin> The second definition is from the remote subject, which says that **philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human things.** <note>This definition is traditionally attributed to the Stoics and Pythagoras.</note>

<margin>3</margin> The third definition of philosophy is from the proximate end, <note>The "end" (Greek: *telos*) refers to the goal or purpose of the practice.</note> which says that **philosophy is the meditation on death.** <note>original: "khokumn mahu." This definition is derived from Plato's *Phaedo*.</note>

<margin>4</margin> The fourth definition is from the remote end, which says that **philosophy is the likeness to God according to the ability of humans.**

<margin>5</margin> The fifth definition is from pre-eminence, which says that **philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences.** <note>original: "arhest arhestits yev makatsutyun makatsutyants." This highlights philosophy's role as the foundation for all other disciplines.</note>

<margin>6</margin> The sixth definition of philosophy is from etymology, <note>The study of the origins and meanings of words.</note> which says that **philosophy is the love of wisdom.**

Having said this, let us come and speak of the reasons for the order of the definitions of philosophy. And it must be known that the definition of philosophy from etymology, which says that "philosophy is the love of wisdom," 

<column-break/>

was placed last in the order, and rightly so. Because for every thing, it is necessary first to exist, and then to meet with a name; just as one is first born, and then a name is given. In the same way, it is necessary first and foremost for philosophy to exist, and then to meet with a name and be called "philosophy." Since the name is naturally suited to hold the final place, for this reason the definition from etymology received the last rank, just like the name "philosophy" itself. 

But the definition from pre-eminence, which says that "philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences," was ranked where it was <note>The Armenian text implies it follows a specific logic of "generality."</note> for these reasons: every thing that is general precedes the specific that exists within someone particularly. For example: "animal," being a general thing, precedes "rational" and "mortal," because "animal" is more inclusive than "rational" or "mortal." <note>This is a classic example from the "Porphyrian Tree," a logical tool used to categorize things from the most general (animal) to the most specific (individual human).</note> And again, "rational" and "mortal" precede being a "receptacle of mind and sciences." For "rational"...

<summary>This page lists the six traditional definitions of philosophy used in the medieval Armenian curriculum and begins explaining their logical order. It argues that the etymological definition ("love of wisdom") comes last because an object must exist before it can be named.</summary>
<keywords>David the Invincible, definitions of philosophy, art of arts, science of sciences, etymology, proximate subject, remote end, Neoplatonism, Porphyrian tree</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical justification for the ordering of the six definitions of philosophy. The author explains why definitions based on the "subject" (the material or topic) must precede those based on the "end" (the goal or purpose), and why general concepts are prioritized over specific ones. This text belongs to the "Prolegomena to Philosophy" tradition of David the Invincible.</meta>

...and "mortal" is more general than that which is capable of mind and knowledge. For the capacity for mind and knowledge is unique to humans alone. Thus, in this way, the more general things are placed before those things that belong specifically to something. Now, since the more general precedes those that exist specifically, for that reason, those definitions that are derived from the **subject** <note>In this context, the "subject" (ent‘akay) refers to the underlying matter or the field of study that a discipline works upon.</note> and from the **completion** <note>The "completion" (katarumn) refers to the end goal or final product of a craft or science.</note> are placed before the definition from **pre-eminence**.

For the **subject** is the basis for the **completion**, and it is within the subject that other arts and sciences exist. However, **pre-eminence** <note>original: "artanut‘iun." This refers to the definition of philosophy as the "art of arts."</note> is not appropriate to any but **philosophy** alone, because philosophy alone is supreme. And for this reason, the definition from pre-eminence is ranked higher, as it specifically suits philosophy alone.

However, the definitions from the **subject** are prior to those from the **completion**. This is because the subject exists first, and then the completion. For if the wood for carpentry does not exist first as a subject, one cannot make a chair, which is the completion. Furthermore, the definition from the **proximate subject** is placed before

<column-break/>

<page-num>24</page-num>

that which is from the **remote subject**. For the general is placed before the particular. If the general "wood" does not exist, then the particular cannot exist either—namely, walnut or plane tree <note>original: "sosakh." The Oriental plane tree was highly significant in ancient Armenian culture, often associated with divination and sacred groves.</note>. 

Following the same principle, the definition from the **proximate subject** is placed first, which says: **philosophy is the knowledge of beings as they are.** And the second definition is placed next, which is from the **remote subject**, saying: **philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human things.** This is because "beings" in general are placed before particular existences. 

Similarly, after this, the definition from the **proximate completion** is placed first, which says: **philosophy is the meditation on death.** And after this comes the one from the **remote completion**, which says that **philosophy is the likeness to God according to the ability of humans.**
<margin><image-desc>Decorative cross mark with abbreviations</image-desc></margin>
For the meditation on death comes before the likeness to God. For if one does not first meditate on death and clearly distance oneself from passions <note>The "passions" here refer to worldly desires or emotional disturbances that a philosopher must overcome.</note>, one cannot become like God. This concludes the **eighth chapter**. Let us proceed to the **ninth chapter** and discuss who the discoverers of these definitions were. And it is necessary to know that...

<summary>This page explains the logical hierarchy of the definitions of philosophy, arguing that the subject matter must be defined before the end goal, and general existence must be understood before specific divine or human attributes. It concludes the eighth chapter by emphasizing that moral purification (meditation on death) is a prerequisite for divine likeness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the six definitions of philosophy. The previous page established that definitions based on the "subject" (what philosophy studies) and the "completion" (what philosophy achieves) are primary. This page identifies the specific sources of these definitions, attributing them to Pythagoras and Plato.</meta>

of the two definitions that are derived from the **subject** <note>The "subject" (ent‘akay) refers to the realities that philosophy examines.</note>, and the one derived from **etymology** <note>original: "stugabanut‘enē." This refers to the definition of philosophy as the "love of wisdom."</note>, we say that **Pythagoras** was the discoverer. Although we cannot demonstrate this directly from the writings of Pythagoras; for Pythagoras did not wish to leave behind any compositions, saying, "I do not wish to leave my teachings in **soulless things**, but in **living things**." By "soulless things," he meant **books**, and by "living things," his **disciples**. For if someone should ask a question or be in doubt, the disciples are able to respond and resolve it; whereas books, always saying one and the same thing, cannot resolve doubts that arise outside of their own text. 

And so, as we said, we cannot demonstrate this from the compositions of Pythagoras himself. However, we can demonstrate it from the **Pythagoreans**. For **Nicomachus** the Pythagorean <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa (c. 60–120 AD), a mathematician known for his "Introduction to Arithmetic."</note>, who discovered the theoretical study of arithmetic, stated that Pythagoras defined **philosophy** in this way: 

**First**, that philosophy is the knowledge of **beings** as they are <note>original: "gitut‘iun ēakats‘n." This is the first definition of philosophy.</note>. **And again**, that philosophy is the knowledge of **divine and human things**. **And again**, that philosophy is the **love of wisdom**. 

But the [remaining] two definitions pertain

<column-break/>

to **Plato**, which are derived from the **completion** <note>The "completion" (katarumn) refers to the end goal or final result of practicing philosophy.</note>. These are the ones where they say that philosophy is the **meditation on death**, and where they say that philosophy is **becoming like God** according to human capacity. 

For in the ***Phaedo*** dialogue, Plato is seen to say that philosophy is the meditation on death; wherein he says that those who strive and rightly approach philosophy are in a state of distress—they forget the things of this world, and regarding themselves and others, they contemplate nothing else but **dying** and **mortality** <note>A reference to Plato's *Phaedo* 67e, where Socrates discusses the practice of death as the purification of the soul.</note>. 

In the ***Theaetetus*** dialogue, it appears he speaks of philosophy to **Theodorus the Geometer**, saying it is **becoming like God according to human capacity**. Through this, he says: "Because, O Theodorus, it is not possible to destroy evil, for it is necessary that it always exists as the opposite of the good, and it cannot be located among the higher things; but it must of necessity linger around this **mortal nature** and this earthly place. For which reason, one must **flee** from here to there as quickly as possible." And what then is this flight? It is **becoming like God according to human capacity** <note>A reference to Plato's *Theaetetus* 176a-b. This "flight" is not a physical movement, but a moral and intellectual transformation.</note>.

It explains Pythagoras's preference for oral teaching over writing and cites Plato's "Phaedo" and "Theaetetus" as the sources for defining philosophy as the meditation on death and becoming like God.</summary>

Having learned how many the **Neoplatonic** <note>original: "norap‘ilisop‘ean," literally "new-philosophical." The first of these definitions says that **philosophy is the knowledge of beings as they are**. 

And one must know that **knowledge** <note>original: "gitut'iun."</note> is taken in place of the **genus** <note>term: "genus" (ser). In formal logic, the genus is the broad category a thing belongs to.</note> as something general. Whereas "**of beings**" is taken in place of the **constitutive differences** <note>term: "constitutive differences" (baghkats‘uts‘ich' zanazanut‘iun). These are the specific characteristics that distinguish one thing from others in the same genus.</note> which are not philosophical but belong to other arts. For philosophy is the knowledge of **all existents**, 

<column-break/>

whereas other arts and sciences are not of all existents, but only of some **particulars**.

And some doubt why it was said with the **article** <note>term: "article" (yad). In Armenian grammar, this refers to the definite article suffix (-ն/-n). The author is debating the nuance between "knowledge of beings" and "knowledge of *the* beings."</note>—that is, "knowledge of **the** beings"—and why it was not said without the article, as "knowledge of beings." 

There are some who say that he might show, through the article, the **communion of beings** which is captured from above by **creative causes** and from above by **material causes**. Regarding this, we say that you do not speak well. For the article does not signify the "communion of beings," but rather **foreknowledge**. For the article signifies **reference** and a **recollection** of that which one knew before. For when we say without the article, "**A man** came," we speak as if regarding an **unknown thing**. But when we say, "**The man** came," it signifies things **known beforehand**. 

However, in this place, this article signifies nothing of that sort; rather, it was placed to make manifest the **universality of the beings**. For we have yet to learn and know in the following sections that propositions without the article are equivalent to **particulars** <note>Small, specific instances.</note>. But with the article, they are equivalent to **universals** <note>General, all-encompassing truths.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of the six definitions of philosophy. Having previously established the "knowledge of beings" as the first definition, the author now clarifies why the phrase "as they are" is essential and transitions to the second definition: "the knowledge of divine and human things."</meta>

propositions; now, so that it might be revealed that philosophy is the most comprehensive knowledge. For this reason, he added as a supplement: "it is the knowledge of beings; **as they are**." He added this to show how philosophy knows these things; that is, it does not know them according to **quantity**, but according to **nature**. For it is not possible to know the quantity of individual things <note>Since individuals are infinite in number, they cannot be counted or known individually by science.</note>, but rather their nature. Therefore, by declaring this—that it knows according to nature and not according to quantity—he said "as they are."

Now, regarding the confirmation of the first definition, this is enough. Let us come to the second definition and confirm it: that **philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human things**. And one must know that here, "knowledge" is taken as something general in place of the <term>genus</term> <note>The broad category under which philosophy falls.</note>. While "of divine and human things" stands in place of the <term>constitutive differences</term> <note>The specific qualities that distinguish philosophy from other types of knowledge.</note> which constitute philosophy. For only philosophy is concerned with both divine and human things; it does not despise the divine for the sake of

<column-break/>

human things, nor does it neglect human things by being preoccupied with the divine. Rather, it follows and apprentices itself to the divine, while it orders and brings to perfection the human.

From this, some raise a doubt, saying: "How can you say that philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human things? For there is a knowledge of other beings: of the heavens, of the earth, and of all existents—and those are neither divine nor human. Furthermore, philosophy is said to be about the nature of the soul, which is neither divine nor human." 

Solving this doubt, we say: among beings, some are <term>intelligible</term> <note>Things understood by the mind alone, like mathematics or God.</note> and some are <term>sensory</term> <note>Things perceived through the five senses.</note>. Now, the highest peaks of the intelligible are the **divine**, and the peaks of the sensory are the **human**. Thus, by naming the extremes, he included the intermediate and sensory things through them. With these points, this section is concluded. Let us come to the forthcoming Practice.

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the definition of philosophy as a "meditation on death." Having established that this does not mean seeking physical death, the author now addresses a potential contradiction in the writings of Plato and provides a systematic breakdown of the different types of "life" and "death."</meta>

What do we have to say concerning **Plato**, who sometimes 
says that one "must meditate on death," and at other times says 
that "it is not right to cast oneself out"? <note>Original: "արտահանել" (artahanel) – literally "to lead out" or "to cast out," a common euphemism for suicide in Neoplatonic commentaries.</note> Was he 
perhaps contradicting himself, or not? We say he was not. 
Rather, let us demonstrate that he did not contradict himself 
by saying a few things. It is necessary to 
know that **life** is a state of being and a 
cause of existence. But **death** is a deprivation and a 
cause of non-existence. And each of these 
is said to be twofold. For indeed, there is a 
twofold **life**: that which is *natural*, and that which is 
*voluntary*. Now, the *natural* is called the union 
of soul and body, according to which the soul is 
said to give sensation and movement to the body; 
it is in this sense that we all say we "live," and 
in this sense the body is said to "sustain" the soul. 
Therefore, **Homer** <note>The famous Greek epic poet.</note> calls the body a "shackle" <note>Original: կապարան (kaparan). Neoplatonic commentators often cited Homer’s descriptions of the body as a prison or shackle for the soul.</note>; for this reason 
it is called "body" [*marmin*], which means "numbed" [*ndarm*], 
because it numbs the soul, and it becomes numb 
in the grave. <note>The author is using a traditional Armenian etymological pun: *marmin* (body) is being linked to *ndarm* (numb/paralyzed), suggesting the physical body dulls the soul's spiritual faculties.</note> But **voluntary life** 
is when the worse overcomes the better; 
that is, when the soul is defeated by bodily 
pleasures, which is also called a "dissolute 
life"—that is, one that is not temperate. But 
**natural death** is the separation of the soul from the body,

<column-break/>

according to which we all pass away. But 
**voluntary death** is a way of life according 
to virtue; that is, to live while 
meditating on death for the sake of 
putting the <term>passions: irrational or selfish impulses that cloud the intellect</term> to death. Now, since 
these things are so, four things are 
revealed: being, non-being, being good, and 
being evil. Now, "being" is natural life, 
while "non-being" is natural death. 
And "being good" is **voluntary death**. 
In the beginning, [voluntary life is] a 
pleasurable way of living. Now, when 
**Plato** says that one must "meditate on death," 
he is speaking of **voluntary death**; 
that is, that one must live according to virtue 
and put the passions to death. But when he 
says that "it is not right to cast oneself out," 
he signifies **natural death**, 
according to which we all die; 
and these things are so. And it must be 
known that a certain **Cleombrotus of Ambracia**, 
thinking that Plato was commanding the 
philosopher to meditate on **natural death**, 
threw himself from a wall and died. 
Regarding this, **Callimachus** <note>A noted Greek scholar and poet from Alexandria.</note> says: "Farewell, O Sun!" cried **Cleombrotus of Ambracia**, and then leapt from the high 
wall into Hades—not because he had suffered 
anything worthy of death <note>original: "արժանի ինչ ոչ ուտեալ մահու" – a literal translation of the Greek "having seen nothing evil worthy of death," meaning he wasn't fleeing misfortune, but was misguided by his reading.</note>, but because of the writings of Plato.

<summary>The author clarifies Plato's dual use of the term "death," distinguishing between natural death (suicide) and voluntary death (the virtuous suppression of passions), while citing the tragic case of Cleombrotus as a warning against misinterpretation.</summary>

<margin>It was</margin>

->### Ninth Lecture<-

Now, because **Plato** defined philosophy as

oneself out." For they even say that

Cyrnus, plunge into the sunlit abyss." 
But one must know that he <note>Theognis.</note> 
does not mean that the destitute person 
should remove himself; rather, he means 
that the destitute should sow in rugged

possible, even in the sea, so that he 
might be saved from famine. According 
to the second way, the feast is 
dissolved because the food is 
corrupted or poisoned <note>Original: դեղսամակարդացեալս (deghsamakardatseals) – literally "curdled with drugs" or "poisoned."</note>. 
For when such foods appear, the guests 
rightly rise and the feast is dissolved. 
Likewise, when someone's body 
becomes corrupted and unfit to 
receive the soul's impulses, he 
rightly "leads himself out," abandoning 
himself from the pain.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Stoic "rational departure" (suicide). The previous page began listing the conditions under which the Stoics believed a person might rightly choose to end their own life, using the metaphor of a banquet being dissolved. This page details the third through sixth justifications.</meta>

...just as a certain Cynic philosopher <note>The Cynics were a school of ancient Greek philosophers who lived in accordance with nature and rejected social conventions.</note> who, being "half-man" <note>Armenian: *kisadamas*. This likely refers to someone who is partially paralyzed or "half-dead."</note>, came before **King Julian** <note>Julian the Apostate (r. 361–363 AD), the Roman Emperor known for his interest in philosophy.</note> saying, "Half of my body is dead, and half sees the light of day. O King, I live a life of blind misery <margin>grave, light of the sun</margin> like a dog; therefore, command either that I be healed or that I be killed." To which the King replied that he was deprived of both the fruit and the sensation, lacking in everything while still perceiving something.

**A**ccording to the third manner, the banquet <term>banquet (erakhan): a central metaphor in this text where "the banquet" represents life, and "leaving the banquet" represents death</term> is dissolved when there is a private distress affecting the host <note>Armenian: *kochnatearn* – literally "lord of the calling" or master of the feast.</note>. For the host is said to "dissolve the banquet" if he suddenly falls ill or hears of the loss of a loved one; the guests then rise, and the banquet ends. **A**nd so, if such a private distress reaches someone, they rightly remove themselves <note>i.e., commit suicide.</note>, as did **Timycha**, the wife of the Pythagorean. <margin>natural instinct</margin> She was so named because, having fallen into the hands of the Sicilian tyrant, and being asked why the Pythagoreans would not eat the "great bean" <note>The Pythagoreans famously had a taboo against eating beans, though the exact reason was a mystery even in antiquity.</note>, she said, "I would rather eat them than tell you." But when the tyrant commanded, "Then eat!" she replied, "I would rather tell you than eat!" And so, biting off her own tongue, she passed away <note>A famous story of philosophical endurance: she bit off her tongue so she could not be forced to reveal the sect's secrets under torture.</note>.

**A**ccording to the fourth manner, the banquet is dissolved

<column-break/>

on account of drunkenness. For when the guests fall into a state of drunkenness, the banquet is rightly dissolved. **I**n this same way, a person rightly removes themselves when they reach extreme old age and senility, and they begin to lose their mind and babble incoherently.

**A**ccording to the fifth manner, the banquet is dissolved when the guests begin to fight and behave unjustly toward one another. Likewise, one rightly removes themselves when, having been captured by enemies, they are commanded to do something unjust, or to fall into disgrace, or to eat unseemly things <note>Specifically, things forbidden by one's religion or philosophical code.</note>.

**A**ccording to the sixth manner, the banquet is said to be dissolved because of a public distress. For when a public distress arrives—such as a fire, an earthquake, or an invasion of barbarians—the guests rise and the banquet is dissolved. **T**hus, a person rightly removes themselves when a public distress arrives, such as when enemies are about to seize and plunder the city, and the person is overcome by that fear.

**N**ow, these are the views of the **Stoics**. However, others join only two manners together according to which it is right...

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical discussion on the justifications for "rational departure" (suicide) before transitioning into a new section (Praxis 31) which defines the ultimate goal of philosophy: becoming like God. It specifically critiques the Stoic view by citing Platonic and Christian-aligned arguments regarding the soul's duty to remain in the body.</meta>

...one who removes himself <note>In this context, "removing oneself" refers to the act of suicide.</note>; for they say that regarding the conduct of this life, it may be good, bad, or indifferent. Now, when someone who is in a good or indifferent state sees themselves plunged into a bad state, they rightly remove themselves. And again, when being in the worst of states, if they see themselves never being able to take wing toward the better, they rightly remove themselves. And so, these things are as stated. For which reason we say that neither by right nor by unrighteousness should anyone remove themselves. For those who wish or think in this way oppose the Creator, wishing with obstinacy to loosen what He has bound. And it is necessary to agree with **Plato** who says:
> that we are as if in a certain guardhouse <note original="պահարանում - paharanum">This is a reference to Plato's *Phaedo* (62b), where he uses the word *phroura* (prison or guardhouse) to describe the body's relationship to the soul.</note>, and one must not extract oneself from it or flee away, but rather remain in that which is bound until He <note>Meaning God or the Creator.</note> shall loosen it.

But if trials <note original="փորձութիք - p’ordzut’ik’">This term can mean "trials," "temptations," or "tests."</note> should happen to someone, it is not for the sake of removing oneself, but for the testing of the soul. For just as a good pilot is tested not in the calmness of the sea, but in the agitation of the waves, so too is the noble soul tested in

<column-break/>

the trial of the champion <term>champion (nahatak): a term often used in Armenian for martyrs or those who struggle spiritually, like athletes in an arena</term>. Hence, the **Peripatetics** <note original="Ճեմականքն - Chemakank’">Literally "the strollers," referring to the followers of Aristotle who taught while walking in the Lyceum.</note>, wishing to demonstrate the endurance of the soul, used to pray, saying:
> "Send us trial."

Along with these things, there is also the upcoming **Praxis** <term>Praxis (Prak): a lecture, study, or chapter in a philosophical commentary</term>. In **Praxis 31**, the tenth point, **Plato** ascends from here to the point of perfection; and not simply to any point, but to that which is furthest and even beyond. For he defines **philosophy** as "likeness to God according to human capacity" <note original="նմանութիւն Աստուծոյ ըստ կարողութեան մարդկան">This is the famous Platonic definition of philosophy from the *Theaetetus* (176b).</note>. For what could be as blessed and fitting for the diligent as to become like God according to human capacity? For this reason, some say that the philosopher cannot, as a human, be like God, because the essence <note original="էութիւն - eut’iwn">Greek: *ousia*.</note> is different. The essence of God and man differ, as the poet <note original="բեթուղն - Bet’ughn">Likely a corruption of the name Theognis or a reference to a specific poet; the quote following is a common sentiment in Greek gnomic poetry.</note> makes clear, saying:
> "For nothing of the earth is like the immortal gods; nor are the ground-treading <margin>light, sun</margin> race of men."

But if their essences differ, then their perfections must also differ. For example, the essence of a human is one thing, and that of a horse is another; thus the perfection of a human is one thing, and that of a horse is another. Now, if the essence of God and man differ, and if those whose essences differ are distinct, then their perfections are also distinct.

<summary>This page argues that humans should not commit suicide because they are placed in life by a Creator, comparing trials to a storm that tests a pilot. It then defines philosophy as the effort to become like God, while acknowledging the inherent difference between divine and human nature.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Peripatetics, suicide, trials, Likeness to God, essence, human capacity, soul, philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the definition of philosophy. Having previously addressed why one should not prematurely end their life, the text now focuses on the Platonic definition of philosophy as "becoming like God as far as is possible for man." The author clarifies the specific nature of this "likeness."</meta>

and shows that the perfection of God is one thing and that of man another; for which reason the philosopher cannot be identical <note>The text suggests that while a philosopher strives for likeness, they never achieve the same essence as God.</note> to God. Now, we shall hasten to resolve this doubt by stating in what sense we use the term "likeness" here; but first, let us state in how many ways "likeness" is spoken of. It is necessary to know that likeness is spoken of in four ways:

<margin>1</margin> Likeness is said to exist when the same quality is seen throughout an entire species. For example, when we say all Ethiopians are like each other in respect to their blackness, or that all swans are like each other in respect to their whiteness.

<margin>2</margin> Again, likeness is said to exist when the same quality is seen in different species, but according to "more" and "less." For example, we say that black pepper and white pepper are like each other in respect to their heat; for both are hot, but they differ in degree; for the black is more hot, while the white is less so.

<margin>3</margin> Again, likeness is said to exist when various qualities are found in various species. For example, we say the pelican and the pigeon are like each other; for just as the pelican has various qualities—such as white,

<column-break/>

reddish-brown, and black—likewise we say the pigeon possesses those same qualities.

<margin>4</margin> Again, things are said to be like each other as the **image** and the **paradigm** <term>paradigm (yarats’uyts’): the original model or blueprint of which something else is a copy</term>. We know the image of **Socrates** to be like **Socrates**. And it is necessary to know that the "paradigm" is also called the **prototype** <note original="նախատիպն - nakhatipn">literally "first-type" or "original."</note>, according to which the image was made; as when, by holding **Socrates** as the paradigm, we say the image of **Socrates** is like **Socrates**.

<margin>5</margin> Now, it is in this sense that we say the philosopher is like God: just as the image of **Socrates** is like **Socrates**. Although the image of **Socrates** is one thing and **Socrates** is another—for one is animate <note>Living/breathing</note> and the other is inanimate—in the same manner and sense, we say the philosopher is like God, even though the essence of God is one thing and the essence of man is another.

<margin>6</margin> And that the philosopher resembles God shall be made manifest from this: for by whatever forms the Divine is characterized, by those same forms is the perfect philosopher characterized.

<margin>7</margin> For the Divine is characterized by these three:

<margin>8</margin> by **Goodness**, by **Knowledge**, and by **Power**.

<margin>9</margin> As poetry <note>Likely referring to the works of Homer or Hesiod, commonly cited by Neoplatonists.</note> makes clear by saying:
Regarding **Goodness**, that "the gods are the givers of good things." Regarding the **Knowledgeable**, that "the gods know all things." And regarding **Power**, that...

<summary>This page explains the fourth definition of philosophy—becoming like God—by distinguishing between four types of "likeness." It concludes that a philosopher is "like God" in the same way a portrait (image) is like its subject (paradigm), specifically through the shared traits of goodness, knowledge, and power.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the definition of philosophy as "becoming like God as far as is possible for man." The previous page established different types of likeness (identity, degree, variety, and image-paradigm). This page applies those categories to the attributes of the philosopher compared to the Divine.</meta>

Are the gods all-powerful? Likewise, the perfect philosopher is formed by these three attributes: goodness, knowledge, and capability. 

Regarding **goodness**: just as the Divine is the provider for all things, likewise the perfect philosopher cares for imperfect souls, transforming the imperfect and ignorant soul into perfect knowledge through the means of wisdom. 

Regarding the **knowledgeable** form: just as the Divine knows all things, so too the perfect philosopher promises to know all things. 

And regarding **capability**: just as the Divine can do and wills all things, so too the philosopher does as much as is possible and willed. 

Now, these things being so, some might ask: "How can you say the philosopher is like God in goodness, knowledge, and power?" For as the poet <note>original: "ert'ołin" — a reference to the poet Homer. The quote is from the Iliad (5.441): "Since the race of immortal gods and of men who walk upon the earth is in no way the same."</note> says, the race of immortal gods is in no way like that of men who tread the earth. And how can you say that this [likeness] was established in the definition "according to human capacity"? <term>human capacity (karołut'iwn mardkan): the inherent limitations of being human, which prevent a person from being identical to God</term> For as much as human capacity and understanding allow, the philosopher is thought to be like God. This is because goodness, knowledge, and power are not the same in God as they are in 

<column-break/>

man. For goodness is spoken of in one way regarding God, and in another regarding man. Goodness is essential <term>essential (ēakats'eal): belonging to the very nature or being of a thing, rather than being an added quality</term> to God, and the essence of God is goodness. Therefore, He is incapable of receiving evil because of the abundance of His goodness. Just as the sun is said to be incapable of receiving darkness because of the abundance of its light. 

But man possesses goodness by habit <note>original: "unakut'iamb" — a state or disposition acquired through effort or practice, rather than being one's nature.</note>; therefore, he is capable of receiving evil. Just as the air possesses white light by disposition—for when the sun rises, the air is enlightened—it is also said to receive darkness when the sun sets. 

And again, knowledge is different in God than it is in man. For God knows all things always and uniformly; there is no time when He does not know, but He knows all things always and uniformly. But man does not know all things always or uniformly. For a newborn does not yet know anything by actual impulse, but is said to know only by potentiality <term>potentiality (zawrutyun): the capacity to acquire knowledge in the future, even if it is not currently present.</term> 

And again, it is possible for someone to know something today and forget it tomorrow, or not to know everything, but only in part. For at one time he knows this, and at another time he knows that; as Plato reveals by saying that it is desirable for one who, at least in old age... <note>Likely a reference to Plato's *Theaetetus* 176b or *Laws*, discussing how wisdom is a difficult, life-long pursuit.</note>

<summary>This page explains that a philosopher's "likeness to God" is not an identity of essence but an imitation of divine attributes—goodness, knowledge, and power—within the limits of human nature. It uses the analogy of light to show that while God is goodness itself, humans only participate in goodness as a temporary disposition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the likeness between the philosopher and the Divine, specifically focusing on the attribute of "capability" or "power," and providing the textual source for the definition of philosophy in Plato's writings.</meta>

philosophy and prudence together.
**A**nd again, he speaks of the power <note>original: "karoghut'iun" — refers to the attribute of being able to act or create</note> of God in one way and that of the philosopher in another. For in God, power is reciprocal: for as much as He wills, He also can; and as much as He can, He also wills. No "incapacity" ever occurs in God. But for the philosopher, it is not reciprocal. For while he wills as much as he is able, he is not able to do everything he might will. 
For if he should wish to seize the sun with his hand, or to create the heavens, he cannot. If he does not do these things, it is not because he does not want to, but because it is an impossible thing. The perfect philosopher does not desire those things; he longs only for those things which he is capable of attaining. For to desire such [impossible] things is a sign of great small-mindedness and an unfulfilled longing. We say, like
<margin>5</margin>Job, that by knowing the weakness of his nature, he does not long for the unattainable, but longs for those things which he is capable of reaching. So much for these matters. 
**B**ut some raise a doubt, asking: "Where did Plato define philosophy as 'becoming like God according to human capacity'?" 
And we say: in the *Theaetetus* <note>original: "T'evdit" — David is referencing Plato's dialogue, the *Theaetetus* (specifically 176a-b)</note> dialogue. For there, in a conversation with a certain geometrician named Theodorus, he says the following: "But since, O Theodorus, it is not possible for evils to 

<column-break/>

perish—for there must always be something contrary to the good—and since they cannot have a place among the gods, they necessarily haunt this mortal nature and this region by necessity. Therefore, one must try to flee from here to there as quickly as possible." 
Now, what is this flight? It is "likeness to God according to human capacity." And this likeness consists in "being holy and just, along with prudence."
**A**gain, some might say that he was not defining philosophy here, but rather the "flight from evils." 
**T**o them we shall say that the flight from evils is nothing other than philosophy. For philosophy, which brings about the "death of the passions"—as it was defined many times above—is the "meditation on death." <note>This refers to the Platonic definition of philosophy as *melete thanatou* (meditation or practice of death), meaning the separation of the soul from bodily passions.</note> And the death of the passions is nothing other than the flight from evils. It is therefore evident that he was indeed defining philosophy. 
And furthermore, regarding the phrase "along with prudence," it is clear that justice and prudence are virtues. Now, the place of philosophy is virtue, for through virtue we acquire philosophy. Thus, it is clear that by saying one must "be just along with prudence," he was defining philosophy.
**A**nd further, regarding the statement "along with prudence,"

he defined the theoretical [aspect]; for prudence is the

justice is seen as passing through all of them. 
For it is seen not only in one

...and good order might be preserved. And as we see in all things that some are only rulers, such as the divine; and some are both rulers and subject to rule, such as human [affairs]—for human things are under the rule of the divine, and are rulers over irrational animals; but others are under rule alone, as are those irrational animals. In the same manner also in man, as in a "small world" <note>original: "p'ok'r ashkharh" — the Armenian equivalent of the Greek *mikrokosmos*, the idea that a human being mirrors the structure of the universe.</note>:

Such a person is also prudent, because they know the cause of why it is necessary to be prudent. Such a person is also just, because injustices arise from bodily pleasures. 

But "acquired" virtues were spoken of in contrast to "natural" ones; and natural [virtues] are those which exist according to the temperament of the body. These do not follow one another; for one who is temperate by the temperament of the body is not by all rights also courageous and prudent and just. These are called "slavish" and "irrational." They are called "slavish" as they necessarily follow the temperament of the body—indeed, such a person is temperate by necessity. But they are called "irrational" for not knowing the cause of why it is necessary to be temperate; and again they are called "irrational" because such virtue is naturally seen even in irrational animals. For instance, when we say the turtle-dove is temperate by nature, and the fox is prudent, and the lion is courageous, and the stork is just—because it feeds its aged father <note>The belief that storks cared for their elderly parents (the "Lex Ciconiaria") was a popular ancient and medieval illustration of natural justice and filial piety.</note>.

And again, **acquired virtues** <note>original: "makats'ats'akan" — virtues learned through study and reason, rather than innate habit.</note> were discussed in relation to **moral virtues**, which come from tradition and instruction. This occurs when someone becomes **temperate** <note>original: "oghjakhōh" — also translated as chaste or sober; here referring to the control of passions.</note> by following a command without knowing the underlying reason. **Lecture Eleven**

He gave the reason for **Pythagoras** defining philosophy in two ways: one from the subject matter and one from the ultimate goal.

->**The Stagirite**<-
<note>original: "Stagirats'in" — a common epithet for Aristotle, who was born in the city of Stagira.</note>
that is, **Aristotle**, also defines philosophy. He does not consider it a small matter, even if he defines it more humbly than the others did in their two-fold definitions; rather, he thinks of it as something great and higher, defining it by its excellence.

He states that **philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences.** But one must ask what he intends by each of these titles—that is, "art of arts" and "science of sciences."

<margin>+</margin> For it would have been enough to say that philosophy is 

<column-break/>

an **art** and a **science**. Now, we must ask: why did he propose "of arts" and "of sciences"? It must be said that through the first doubling—that is, "art of arts"—he likened **philosophy** to a **King**. But through the second doubling—that is, "science of sciences"—he likened philosophy to **God**.

<margin>+</margin> For when we say "ruler of rulers," we indicate a King. In the same way, when we say "art of arts," we liken philosophy to a King. But when we say "**King of kings**," we indicate God. In the same way, by calling philosophy the "science of sciences," we liken it to God.

<margin>+</margin> Because science <note>original: "makats'ut'iwn" — often used to translate the Greek "episteme," meaning certain, theoretical knowledge.</note> is superior to art <note>original: "arhest" — translating the Greek "techne," meaning practical craft or skill.</note>. This is because science provides the principles for art. Even the stonemason and the poet <note>original: "k'ert'awghn" — can mean grammarian or poet; here likely referring to the creator of literary works.</note> know what things are and what their nature is, even if they do not know the underlying causes; instead, they refer these back to the **musical philosopher** <note>In the Pythagorean tradition, the "musician" or "harmonicist" is the philosopher who understands the mathematical laws governing all arts.</note>. And just as a King does not defile himself by involving himself directly with the bloodthirsty crowd, but appoints governors over them, from whom...

<summary>This page discusses the definition of philosophy as the "Art of Arts" and "Science of Sciences," comparing its hierarchy to that of a King and God. It explains that philosophy provides the foundational principles for all other practical crafts and sciences.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle (The Stagirite), Pythagoras, definitions of philosophy, Art of Arts, Science of Sciences, acquired virtue, moral virtue, hierarchy of knowledge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Aristotelian definition of philosophy as the "Art of Arts and Science of Sciences." It explains how philosophy acts as a foundation for all other specific disciplines by providing them with their principles and defining their subjects.</meta>

through the higher and the lower, He provides care; in the same manner, **Philosophy** brings forth **arts** <note>original: "arhest" — refers to a craft or technical skill (Greek: techne).</note> through their **subjects** <note>original: "ent'akay" — literally "that which lies under," referring to the specific material or topic a science studies.</note> which it knows. And again, just as the divine has certain invisible powers through which He cares for the stars, so too Philosophy brings forth **sciences** <note>original: "makats'ut'iun" — systematic knowledge or science (Greek: episteme).</note> so that through them it may know their subjects. And further, for this reason, he defines philosophy as the **art of arts and the science of sciences**, so that he might show that arts and sciences have the same relationship to philosophy as their own subjects have to them. For the subject of **medicine** is the human body, but for philosophy, medicine itself is the subject. And again, the subject of **astronomy** is the heavenly body, but for philosophy, astronomy itself is the subject. And again, for this reason, he called philosophy the art of arts and the science of sciences, because philosophy knows

<column-break/>

the **nature of beings**. But it leaves the things outside of nature and the secondary consequences to the arts and sciences. For philosophy itself, by employing knowledge, distinguishes **matter**, **form**, and **principles**, and fully accounts for the **four elements**. From these, it accounts for the
<margin>What the natural philosophers <note>original: "Yerkirgushaks" — literally "earth-diviners," likely referring here to those who study the physical world or geologists/naturalists.</note> require, etc.</margin>
**homoeomeries** <note>original: "nmanamasneayn" — a term from Aristotelian biology meaning "similar-parts," referring to tissues like bone or skin that are the same throughout.</note>. From these "similar-parts," it defines the **instrumental organs**, and from the instrumental organs,
<margin>blood, bile, phlegm, etc. <note>These are the "humors" of ancient medicine; their balance was thought to determine health.</note></margin>
the **human body**. All other **accidents** <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are properties that can change without changing the essential nature of a thing.</note> that occur in the body, philosophy leaves to the field of medicine to know—namely, **pain** and **health**. It is not as if philosophy does not know these things, but rather it does not wish to make itself unseemly by descending into the most minute and final details. And again, philosophy knows the nature of **sound**, but it leaves the attributes of sound to the **grammarian**—namely, the accents and breathings. And again, philosophy knows the nature of the **line**, but it allows the rest to be known by **geometry**. And again, for this reason, he called philosophy the art of arts and the science of sciences, because it is that which gives the **principles** to all arts and sciences.

<summary>This page explains philosophy's role as the "Art of Arts," arguing that while specific sciences like medicine or geometry study particular subjects, philosophy provides the underlying principles and studies those sciences themselves. It illustrates a hierarchy of knowledge where philosophy focuses on essential natures (like matter and form) while leaving specific details (like health or grammar) to specialized disciplines.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Art of Arts, Science of Sciences, medicine, astronomy, elements, humors, nature of beings, principles, geometry, grammar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Philosophy as the foundation of all other disciplines. It provides specific examples from Grammar, Medicine, and Rhetoric, and introduces Pythagorean classifications of the self and its possessions.</meta>

For **Grammar** <note>original: "k'erakanut'iun"</note>, taking from others, recognizes that the letters **eta** and **omega** <note>original: "zēn ev zovn" — referring to the Greek vowels η (eta) and ω (omega), which are long by nature. Philosophy provides the underlying reason for why these sounds exist as they do.</note> are long by nature; however, the reason why this is so is known to **Philosophy**. But grammar, admittedly taking these principles from Philosophy, organizes its own art.

Again, **Medicine** knows that the human body is composed of **four elements**, yet it does not know the reasons why there are four elements; instead, it admittedly takes this from Philosophy to organize its own craft.

And again, **Rhetoric** <note>original: "chartasanut'iun"</note> is useful for **Justice**, that is to say, for **Politics** <note>original: "k'aghak'akanut'iun"</note>. Yet rhetoric is ignorant of the nature of justice itself; it admittedly takes this from Philosophy to organize its own art. Because rhetoricians are ignorant of the nature of justice, they choose to commit a wrong rather than <gloss>to die</gloss> to suffer a wrong. 

But the **Philosopher**, who knows the nature of justice, chooses neither of these; however, if he must choose, he chooses to suffer a wrong rather than to commit one. For they even say that no one can harm another’s **soul**, unless that person harms their own soul by performing unjust and improper deeds. For if someone harms another,

<column-break/>

they either harm the **body** or **external things** by seizing possessions. But they cannot harm the soul, only its instrument, which is the body. As the **Pythagoreans** used to say: **"I," "mine," and "that which belongs to mine."** 

By **"I,"** they meant the **soul**, for it is through the soul that we possess perception. By **"mine,"** they meant the **body**, as it is the instrument of the soul. And by **"that which belongs to mine,"** they meant **external things**, that is, one's possessions. How then could someone harm another? They can only harm their own self; they cannot harm another's soul.

And it should be known that Philosophy provides principles not only to the **rational arts** <note>original: "banakan arhestits'" — disciplines involving speech and logic, like rhetoric or grammar.</note> and sciences, but also to the **irrational** <note>original: "anbanits'" — literally "speechless" or "non-verbal" arts, referring to manual crafts.</note> ones, such as the cord for the carpenter and fire for the blacksmith. These are called "irrational" arts not because they lack a rational cause—for there is no art that lacks a cause, even if the craftsman is ignorant of it—but they are called "irrational" because it is possible to learn and practice them without speaking. For it is possible to complete the making of a chair without uttering a word. 

And again, it should be known that Philosophy is able to give principles not only to all arts

<meta>pencil note '40' at bottom right margin</meta>

<summary>This page argues that philosophy provides the foundational principles for all disciplines, from grammar and medicine to manual crafts. It also details the Pythagorean view that the soul is the true 'self,' which remains unharmed by external misfortune or bodily injury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical defense of Philosophy as the "Art of Arts and Science of Sciences." It moves from the examples of Rhetoric and Medicine to the fundamental rules of Logic and Definition, specifically addressing how Philosophy corrects the imprecise definitions used by other disciplines.</meta>

...and of sciences, but also to correct again the errors within them. For **grammarians** <note>original: "k'ert'awghk'" — literally "poets," but often used in this context for those who study the structure of language.</note>, in defining **"voice"** <note>original: "dzayn"</note>, called it a **"wounding of the air."** The philosopher corrects this again, saying that this definition is poor, because "voice" is not **reciprocal** <note>original: "zugadardzi" — a technical term in logic meaning that the definition and the thing defined must be perfectly interchangeable (e.g., all humans are rational animals, and all rational animals are humans).</note> with "wounding of the air." Indeed, not every "wounding of air" is a voice. For as he says, if one does not strike the air in a specific way, no voice is completed.

But **perfect definitions** are those which are reciprocal with the things being defined. For instance: whatever is a "living, rational, mortal being, capable of mind and intellect," that is a **human**. And whatever is a **human**, is "a living, rational, mortal being, capable of mind and intellect." For this reason, they define "voice" in this way:

**Voice** is the emission of air through the throat, processed through the roughness of the windpipe, and shaped by the tongue and the **epiglottis** <note>original: "makalezuaw" — literally "the thing upon the tongue."</note>.

Again, it must be said that for this reason he called philosophy the **"art of arts and science of sciences,"** because all rational arts have need of divisions, definitions, and demonstrations, of which **Philosophy** is the mother.

<column-break/>

Alongside these matters, we shall begin...

### Lecture. Lecture Twelve.

Since we have gone through the entire definition as a whole, let us henceforth come to his <note>Referring to the author being studied, likely David the Invincible or Porphyry.</note> wisdom and examine each point individually, bringing them forward for discussion.

For some say that you spoke very foolishly when you said that philosophy is the "art of arts." For if it is an "art," it is evident that it is an art; but if it is an art, it is evident that it is **fallible** <note>original: "skhalakan" — prone to error.</note>, which is unfitting <note>The argument is: Arts like carpentry can make mistakes; if Philosophy is just an "art," it must also be imperfect, which the critics find unacceptable.</note>. 

The solution must be stated: not everything that is said together as a compound can also be said separately. For behold, we say **"dead man,"** but we cannot say **"man"** separately, because he does not possess the properties of a man; for he is neither animate nor sentient. Again, we say **"stone ship,"** but it is not called a **"ship"** <note>original: "naw"</note>, because it does not float upon the waters like a ship. Again, as the riddle says: **"A man and not a man"** <note>The "Eunuch" riddle: A man who is not a man (a eunuch) saw and did not see (with one eye) a bird and not a bird (a bat) perched on a wood and not a wood (a reed) and hit and did not hit it (with a pumice stone).</note>, but nevertheless a man. **"A bird and not a bird,"** but nevertheless a bird... on a piece of wood and not...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 43 regarding the definitions of "art" and "science" (philosophy). The previous page established that while philosophy is called the "Art of Arts," it differs from specific crafts. This page delves into the levels of knowledge, distinguishing between mere experience, empirical skill, art, and science based on whether one understands the "cause" (the "why") of a thing.</meta>

...confessedly, if it is a sign that lacks a specific part, the **cause** <note>original: "pacharn"</note> of which he does not know, that is the concern of the **natural philosopher** <note>This refers to someone who observes the natural world without necessarily having the formal tools of logic.</note>. However, regarding **indemonstrable science** <term>Indemonstrable science: "annerstoragrakan makats'ut'iwn," referring to knowledge based on self-evident axioms that do not require logical proof.</term>, it takes as its principles **common thoughts** <note>original: "hasarakats' mtatsut'is"</note> which have no need of proof. For example, when we say that **"God is good,"** this is a common thought because everyone confesses that God is good, and it requires no proof. 

Now, let these matters be so. And since we have made mention of **art** and **science**, we shall speak of them in equal measure. But before discussing them, we shall speak regarding **knowledge** <note>original: "gitut'iwn"</note> in general. 

Now, one must know that knowledge is either **universal** or **particular**; it is either with a **cause** or without a **cause**. From these, four categories arise:
1. **Skill** <note>original: "hmtut'iwn" — simple experience or acquaintance with a thing.</note>
2. **Empiricism** <note>original: "nerhmtut'iwn" — advanced experience or practical skill.</note>
3. **Art** <term>Art: "arhest," implying a technical craft or discipline (Greek: techne).</term>
4. **Science** <term>Science: "makats'ut'iwn," implying systematic, theoretical knowledge (Greek: episteme).</term>

Now, **skill** is the knowledge of a single thing without its cause. For instance, when someone knows one specific medicine and it is useful for that one thing only, but they do not know the **cause**—that is, why or how it must be used. On the other hand, **empiricism** is more universal than mere skill, but it is still knowledge without a cause. This is like those who serve under 

<column-break/>

**skillful physicians** <note>The text distinguishes between the "empiricist" (who knows what works from experience) and the "theoretical physician" (who knows why it works).</note>. They know many medicines, but they remain ignorant of the **cause** of the medicine's effect. 

Furthermore, **empiricism** is a memory of human observations that have appeared many times and in similar ways. For the empirical physician, by remembering and preserving many instances where the same effect appeared from certain medicines, finds them useful. 

In contrast, **art** is universal knowledge accompanied by a **cause**. Or, art is a **habit** <term>Habit: "unakut'iwn" (Greek: hexis), a stable quality or disposition of the mind.</term> that proceeds along a path together with **imagination** <note>original: "erevakayut'iamb" — here meaning a mental blueprint or conceptualization.</note>. For art is a certain habit of knowledge, but it also "proceeds along a path," meaning it performs its tasks in an orderly way. 

The phrase "together with imagination" was said because of **nature** <note>original: "bnut'iwn"</note>. For nature is also a habit—since it possesses the qualities of the things it inhabits, such as in a human, or in a stone, and in others—and it acts according to a prior order. However, nature does not act according to **imagination** <note>mental planning</note> in the way that art does. For the **artisan**, making use of **reason** <note>original: "bani"</note> when he wishes to create something (that is, the cause), first stamps the image within himself and only then completes the work. But nature never stamps an image within itself beforehand...

<summary>This page defines and categorizes four levels of knowledge—Skill, Empiricism, Art, and Science—distinguishing them by their use of "cause" and "imagination." It specifically explains that while both Art and Nature follow an orderly "habit," Art is unique because the artisan mentally plans (imagines) the work before executing it.</summary>
