<meta>This page continues the technical classification of knowledge begun on previous pages, specifically focusing on the definitions of "Art" and "Science" as understood in the Armenian Neoplatonic tradition (likely following David the Invincible). It distinguishes between useful, vain, and evil arts.</meta>

And again, **art** <term>Art: "aruest" or "arhest," corresponding to the Greek *techne*, meaning a systematic craft or discipline.</term> is a composition of perceptions, practiced through **expertise** <note>original: "nerhmtut'eamb" — advanced practical skill or empiricism.</note> and prior training, directed toward a useful completion for this life. Furthermore, **possession** <note>original: "kalwatsut'iwn" — likely referring to the "grasping" or mastery of a subject.</note> is defined again in this way:

**Art is a composition**; that is, a sum total resulting from production. For art is brought to completion by some people knowing one thing and others knowing another. **Practiced through expertise**; that is, tested through many skills. For many experiences, having tested the various types of an art, recognize them through that very same art.

And as for why it was said to have a **useful completion for this life**, this is to distinguish it from **vain arts** and **evil arts**, which do not have a useful completion in this life.

**Vain arts** include **tightrope-walking** and **pole-balancing** <note>original: "laraxaghats'ut'iwn" and "dzoghaxaghats'ut'iwn" — traditional forms of acrobatics.</note>, which neither benefit nor harm our way of life.

However, **evil arts** include **sorcery** and **divination**, which not only fail to benefit our way of life but actually cause harm.

On the other hand, **science** <term>Science: "makats'ut'iwn," corresponding to the Greek *episteme*, meaning certain, theoretical knowledge.</term> is universal, unerring, and unchangeable knowledge; for it knows the object of knowledge without error. And again,

<column-break/>

**science** is the unerring knowledge of things that are the **underlying subjects** <term>Subject: "ent'akay," the Greek *hypokeimenon*, referring to the actual matter or reality being studied.</term> according to the nature of those things. For as the underlying things possess their nature, so too does science know them.

Now, let these things be so. But some people raise a doubt, saying: "If **art** knows with a **cause** <note>original: "pacharn" — the reason or 'why' behind a thing.</note> and **science** also knows with a cause, how do they differ from one another?"

And some say that it follows that **science** knows unerringly and is without error, while **art** errs. To these people we say: You do not speak correctly. For art, according to its own principles, is also unerring; but it only *appears* to err because of the instability of the **underlying subject**.

Thus, **art** and **science** differ from one another because the subjects of **science** are unerring, such as geometry and astronomy <note>original: "astghagitut'iwn" — literally "star-knowledge."</note>, and the stars themselves. For astronomy is unerring, and so is its subject—namely, the stars.

But while **art** is unerring according to its own principles, it appears to be errant because its subject is **fluid** <note>original: "hosanut" — literally "flowing" or "in flux."</note>. For example, [the study of] illness <note>The text likely refers here to the art of Medicine, which studies the subject of disease.</note> and wood are subjects; now, [the study of] illness is unerring according to its own principles, but wood...

<summary>This page defines "Art" as a useful composition of skills and "Science" as unerring, universal knowledge. It defends the precision of Art by explaining that "errors" in crafts like medicine or carpentry arise from the unstable nature of the physical materials (subjects), not from the principles of the art itself.</summary>
<keywords>Art (arhest), Science (makats'ut'iwn), tightrope-walking, sorcery, astronomy, geometry, causality, subject matter (ent'akay), David the Invincible</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical distinction between "Art" (techne) and "Science" (episteme), likely following the Prolegomena to Philosophy by David the Invincible. It transitions from the end of the 12th lecture into the 13th, focusing on the Pythagorean definitions of philosophy.</meta>

...because the subject matter is corruptible and in flux, and can suddenly break the clarity of the work and cause the **art** to fail. But as we said more fully above, these differ from one another—**art** <term>Art: *arhest*, referring to practical or applied knowledge (Greek: *techne*).</term> and **science** <term>Science: *makats'ut'iwn*, referring to certain, theoretical knowledge (Greek: *episteme*).</term>. For science, according to its own reason, is unerring both in itself and in its subject. But art is unerring in its theory, while in its subject it appears to be fallible. Now, these matters are concluded. And here begins the:

### Thirteenth Lecture
Let **Pythagoras** be for us the beginning and the end. For it is fitting that for such a man, the beginning and the end should meet as in a circle. For in a circle, the beginning is joined to the end. Thus, he was the beginning and the end for us. He is the **beginning**, because his are the definitions that were stated at the start; that is, that **philosophy** <term>Philosophy: *imastasirut'iwn*, literally "love of wisdom."</term> is the knowledge of beings, insofar as they are beings. And again, philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human things. But he is also the **end**,

<column-break/>

<header>46</header>

because the definition of that same man was explained at the conclusion, which says: **Philosophy is the love of wisdom**. 

And one must know that some criticize the explanation of this definition, saying that every definition must be reciprocal with its defined object <note>In formal logic, a definition is "reciprocal" or "convertible" if the definition and the term defined refer to the exact same set of things (e.g., "Man is a rational mortal animal").</note>; but the present definition is not reciprocal. For while philosophy is the love of wisdom, not every love of wisdom is necessarily philosophy. For every art loves and desires its own subject. 

Therefore, it must be said that the present definition is good, for it belongs to **Pythagoras**. For first and foremost, Pythagoras discovered the definition of philosophy, which previously had been used loosely and applied even to manual crafts. As the **Poet** <note>original: "ch'ert'oghn" — referring to Homer, who was often called "The Poet" in classical education.</note> reveals; for he says the "wise man" fitted the ship together, calling the builder "wise." 

But Pythagoras set the "wise man" apart and placed him above, toward the true knowledge of being. For he was the first to name "wisdom" as the truest knowledge—that is, the knowledge of **God**. And it must be known that philosophy is useful for many levels, just as there are many levels of philosophy.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of epistemology (the theory of knowledge) found in the Neoplatonic commentaries attributed to David the Invincible. It follows the comparison of Art and Science from page 46, now categorizing the specific mental faculties that lead to different types of understanding.</meta>

<margin>
D.
Clearly 
explains the 
proposition 
and the 
conclusion.
</margin>

† For it is necessary to know the five cognitive powers: **sensation**, **imagination**, **opinion**, **discursive reasoning**, and **mind** <note>These correspond to the Greek terms: *aisthesis*, *phantasia*, *doxa*, *dianoia*, and *nous*.</note>.

And **sensation** <term>Sensation: *zgayut'iwn*, the most basic level of awareness through the five senses.</term> is the knowledge of a particular present thing; because every sensation knows partial knowledge. But **imagination** is the partial knowledge of absent things, and it is distinguished from mere thought by this: because imagination, as we have said, is the memory of absent things. But "mere thought" is the disposition of non-existent things, as when someone creates a "stag-goat" <note>original: *ełjeruap’ałn*; the Greek *tragelaphos*, a mythical creature used in logic to illustrate a concept that can be thought of but has no existence in reality.</note> in their mind for the sake of definition.

But **opinions** are said to be twofold: there is that which is with cause (reason), and that which is without cause. "Without cause" is like when someone opines that the **rational soul** is immortal without knowing the reason. And "with cause" is when one knows the supportive argument for its immortality. 

And one must know that **opinion with cause** is the conclusion of propositions <note>In formal logic, a proposition (*arajarkut'iwn*) is a statement that leads to a conclusion.</note>. For example:
The soul is self-moving.
The self-moving is always-moving.
The always-moving is immortal.
Behold the conclusion of the propositions: that is, "the soul is immortal," which is called "opinion with cause."

<column-break/>

<header>47</header>

But **discursive reasoning** <term>Discursive reasoning: *tramakhohut'iwn*, the process of logical step-by-step thinking (Greek: *dianoia*).</term> is the knowledge of universals together with their cause. Because of this, some are in doubt, saying: if "opinion with cause" is universal knowledge and "discursive reasoning" is universal knowledge with cause, how do they differ from one another? 

To which it must be said that "opinion with cause" is the conclusion of the propositions; but "discursive reasoning" is the finder of propositions from common admissions, as well as the weaver and the concluder of them.

Now, these things being so, one must know that from **sensations** is born **experience** <term>Experience: *hmtut'iwn*, or empirical skill (Greek: *empeiria*).</term>; and from **opinion without cause** is born **intuition** <note>original: *nerhmtut'iwn*, literally "inner-skill" or insight.</note>. Furthermore, from both sensation and imagination combined, intuition is born. 

But from discursive reasoning and "opinion with cause" is born **art** <term>Art: *arhest*, referring to practical or applied knowledge (Greek: *techne*).</term>. 

And from discursive reasoning and the mind, **science** <term>Science: *makats'ut'iwn*, certain and theoretical knowledge (Greek: *episteme*).</term> is born. For when it takes principles that are capable of demonstration, that is where science is born from discursive reasoning. But when they take principles that are not suitable for demonstration, that which belongs to the mind... <note>The text ends mid-sentence, likely continuing on the next page to describe the "mind" (*nous*) as the faculty of immediate, non-demonstrable principles.</note>

<meta>The catchword at the bottom right refers to the previous numbering or folio sequence, though the page is marked 47.</meta>

<summary>This page defines the five cognitive powers—sensation, imagination, opinion, reasoning, and mind—and explains how they combine to form different levels of human knowledge, such as experience, art, and science.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic discussion of the definitions of philosophy, moving from the internal mental faculties to the external classification of philosophy itself. It draws heavily on the tradition of David the Invincible, a 6th-century Armenian philosopher who studied in Alexandria. This page transitions from the six classic definitions of philosophy to a discussion of Pythagorean numerology.</meta>

...we say give birth to **science** <term>Science: *makats'ut'iwn*, referring to stable, organized knowledge as opposed to mere opinion.</term>. And now, as we have already said, it has been shown through many steps that **philosophy** is of great utility, and that philosophy is the best of all arts and sciences. As **Plato** makes clear in the case of **Theodorus the Geometer**, saying:
> If such a thing, O Theodorus, never came among men and never shall come as a gift from God... <note>original: "Եթէ այսպիսին ինչ ով Թէոդորէ..." This is a reference to Plato's *Theaetetus* (176b), suggesting that philosophy/wisdom is the greatest gift of the gods to mortals.</note>

Now, these things being so, we have recognized that there are **six definitions of philosophy**, and we have understood the reasons for them. And not only the definitions, but also that there are four distinctions <note>The "four ways" or categories under which the definitions fall.</note> among the definitions of philosophy.

For some are derived from the **subject matter**, such as "knowledge of beings as they are beings," and "knowledge of divine and human things."

And some are from the **goal**, such as "philosophy is the meditation on death," and "becoming like God according to human capacity."

And from **excellence**, such as "the art of arts and the science of sciences."

Finally, from **etymology**, such as "the love of wisdom."

And for this reason, there are four distinctions of the definitions of philosophy. Because...

<column-break/>

...the number **four** was highly honored among the **Pythagoreans**. Just as they themselves, when taking oaths by Pythagoras, would say: "I swear by him who handed down to us the **Tetrad**, the ever-flowing fountain of nature." <note>The "Tetrad" or Tetractys (1, 2, 3, 4) was a mystical symbol for Pythagoreans, representing the organization of space and the universe.</note> They called the four "ever-flowing" because of the **four elements**. And for this reason, the number four was highly honored among the Pythagoreans: either because of the four elements, or because of the four virtues of the soul <note>The four cardinal virtues: Wisdom, Justice, Fortitude, and Temperance.</note>, or because the number four, when added together with its predecessors, completes the number **ten**.

For taking [the number four] and adding three, it completes seven; and again adding two, it completes nine; and adding one, it completes ten. <note>1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10. In Pythagorean thought, the number ten was considered the "perfect number" (Decad) containing all other numbers.</note> And the number ten is perfection; for every individual unit exists by its own name up to ten, and after this, it cycles back in the same circle.

[The section] concluded with these matters.

### The Fourteenth Lecture

Because in the previous discussion we stated the numerical reasons why there are six definitions of philosophy, it is now necessary to know that there are two types of **Arithmetic**: even and odd. <term>Even and Odd: *dar* and *kočat*. In early Armenian mathematical vocabulary, *dar* refers to "even" and *kočat* (literally "truncated" or "incomplete") refers to "odd" numbers.</term>

<meta>This page continues the Neoplatonic introduction to philosophy, likely following the Prolegomena of David the Invincible. Having previously discussed the six definitions of philosophy, the text now transitions into the symbolic and arithmetic properties of numbers, specifically focusing on the classification of even and odd numbers and the mystical properties of the number seven.</meta>

And the **even** <term>Even: *zar*, a term for paired or matching numbers.</term>, which is able to be divided into two equal pairs; such as four, such as six, such as eight, such as ten. But the **odd** <term>Odd: *kochat*, literally meaning "truncated" or "cut short," referring to the remainder left when dividing by two.</term>, which is not able to be divided into two equal pairs; such as three, such as five, such as seven, such as nine. Now, the common beginning of both the even and the odd is the **monad** <note>original: *miak*, referring to the "One."</note>; but the first of the even is the **dyad** <note>original: *erkeak*, referring to the "Two."</note>.

And some wonder how the dyad, being a number, can be a beginning. To these we say that it is not truly a number; for it does not possess within itself the property of numbers. For every number, when multiplied, produces a larger number than when added. For example: three times three is nine, but three and three is six. Behold, by multiplying, it made a larger number, nine, than by adding, which made six. 

But the dyad is not like this; for by multiplying and by adding, it produces the same number, four. Two times two is four; two and two is four. Therefore, it is evident that the dyad is not a number, but only the **beginning of numbers**. 

These things being so, one must know that the number **seven** is called a **virgin**, just like **Athena** <note>The Greek goddess of wisdom.</note> and like **Time**. This is because every number within the first ten either begets someone <note>i.e., acts as a factor to produce another number.</note> or is begotten by someone <note>i.e., is a product of other factors.</note>. But the number seven neither begets any number within the first ten, nor is it begotten 

<column-break/>

by any; for example: Two begets four by means of multiplication, and three begets nine. Furthermore, four is begotten by two, and it begets eight through multiplication by two. Likewise, five begets ten by means of multiplication by two. 

And six is begotten by three, according to that same multiplication. And eight is from four, according to that same multiplication. And nine is from three, according to three-fold multiplication. And ten is from five, according to two-fold multiplication. But the number seven, as we said before, begets no one and is begotten by no one within the first ten. 

Now, in this same way, **Athena** is called a virgin. For the **outsiders** <note>original: *artak'ink'n*, a standard term used by Armenian Christian scholars to refer to "external" or pagan Greek philosophers.</note> used to say of Athena that she was born from the head of **Aramazd** <note>original: *Aramazd*, the supreme deity of the Armenian pantheon, used here to translate the Greek **Zeus**.</note>. 

And it is called **Time** because infants born at six months or eight months do not survive, but those born at seven months do survive. And again, because it is natural for children to sprout teeth in the seventh month, and to change them in the seventh year.

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion on the properties of numbers and the dyad as the beginning of multiplicity, the text now transitions into the formal methodology of logic—specifically the method of division (diaeresis)—to categorize the branches of philosophy. This follows the structure of David the Invincible’s "Definitions of Philosophy."</meta>

Let us now come, as philosophy is a multifaceted thing, to subject it to **division**; for through the act of division, we accurately understand its parts, and through the parts, we accurately know the whole. And again, by knowing its parts, we are able to accurately discern which philosophical compositions belong to which specific branches. This is so that we do not, out of ignorance, mistake those belonging to the **theoretical** for the **practical**, or those belonging to the **practical** for the **theoretical**.

Now, since we have mentioned division, let us first learn what **division**, **epi-division** <note>original: *makabazhanumn*; a secondary division of the same subject using a different criteria.</note>, and **sub-division** <note>original: *storabazhanumn*; the division of a part that has already been divided from a whole.</note> are. Then we must know that **division** is the first cutting of an underlying subject. For example, when we say that among animals, some are **rational** and some are **irrational**. 

But **epi-division** is a second cutting of that same underlying subject, performed according to a different method; such as when we divide "animal" again and say that among animals, some are **mortal** and some are **immortal**. But **sub-**

<column-break/>

**division** is the cutting of a part that has already been divided; such as having divided "animal" into rational and irrational, we then **sub-divide** the "rational" into **mortal** and **immortal**. Again, we divide the "mortal" into **man** and **horse**, and such things as these.

In the same way, **philosophy** <term>Philosophy: *Imastasirutiun*, literally "love of wisdom."</term> is divided into the **theoretical** and the **practical**. Through the theoretical, we may know the truth; while through the practical, we may achieve the rectification of our morals. 

Next, it is worth asking why philosophy is divided into **two**—I mean the theoretical and the practical—and not into one, or into many. To this, one must say that a division cannot consist of only one; for division is never naturally inclined to be about a single thing, but rather about many. Indeed, division "loves duality"; that is, to distinguish one thing from another by choice, and not to be scattered into a confusing multitude. 

However, philosophy was divided into two for **three reasons**. First and foremost is this: philosophy was divided into the theoretical and the practical because the theoretical and the practical are essential to our being <note>The text cuts off here, beginning to explain how these two branches correspond to the human faculties of intellect and action.</note>...

It specifically applies these methods to philosophy, splitting it into Theoretical (knowledge of truth) and Practical (ethics and behavior) branches.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the discussion on the method of division, David the Invincible now explores the specific reasons why philosophy is divided into two primary branches: Theoretical and Practical. This page provides three reasons based on human nature, the imitation of the Divine, and the structure of the human soul.</meta>

[practical] <note>This completes the word from the previous page: "sub-division."</note> branch. And the **theoretical** branch is innate to us in this way: as all children show by being fond of asking questions in conversation—that is, by being naturally curious—for each of us finds pleasure in hearing something new.

In the same way, the **practical** branch is also substantial within us, as shown by the soul never being truly at rest. Even during sleep, it never stops moving as it wanders through dreams. And even when we are not sleeping and have nothing specific to do, we still move; we might twirl a hair from our heads or fiddle with a piece of straw. Now, because both the theoretical and the practical are innate to us, philosophy is divided into these two parts.

The second reason for this division into two is this: as we learned previously, **philosophy** <term>philosophy; defined here as "the likeness to God as far as is possible for human beings."</term> is a likeness to the Divine, which possesses two virtues: the theoretical and the practical. The theoretical is that by which He knows all beings, and the practical is that by which He is the Creator. 

Therefore, philosophy is divided into two—into the theoretical and the practical—so that through the theoretical branch, one may imprint within oneself the theoretical virtue of Divinity, and through the practical branch, the practical virtue, by caring for human souls and leading them from ignorance to true knowledge.

<column-break/>

The third reason is this: philosophy has the intention of adorning human souls. Human souls are naturally endowed with two powers: the **theoretical** <note>original: *tesakan*; relating to vision, contemplation, and knowledge.</note> and the **vital** <note>original: *kendanakan*; often translated as "animate" or "active," relating to the life-force and emotions.</note>. 

The theoretical power consists of: **discourse** <note>original: *tramakhohut‘iwn*; rational reasoning or dialectic.</note>, **opinion**, **sensation**, **imagination**, and the **mind**; these are the things we spoke of earlier. 

The vital power, however, consists of these: **will**, **choice**, **inclination**, **anger**, and **desire**.

For this reason, philosophy was divided into two: the theoretical and the practical. This was done so that through the theoretical branch, the theoretical powers of the soul might be adorned, and through the practical branch, the vital powers might be adorned. For the practical branch teaches us not to act out of anger and not to desire anything improper. 

Now, these are the reasons why philosophy was divided into two: the theoretical and the practical. 

One must also know that each of these two branches is both overcome by the other and overcomes the other. For the theoretical overcomes the practical in terms of its **subject matter** <term>subject matter; the underlying material or topic that a branch of study examines.</term>; this is because all existing things are the subject matter of the theoretical for the sake of knowledge. But for the practical, only human souls are the subject matter; for it concerns only human souls...

However, the **practical** power overcomes the theoretical in its fulfillment. This is because the fulfillment of the theoretical is **truth**, whereas the fulfillment of the practical is **the good**. 

Through the practical branch, one adorns the heart by removing the passions and performing good deeds. But through the theoretical branch, one attains truth; for it is the theoretical that teaches us the truth of all existing things. 

Furthermore, "the good" is more honorable than "the true," as it is more universal and all-encompassing. For that which is true is also good, but not every good thing is necessarily true. Indeed, we sometimes find what is called a "good lie," such as in this example: Suppose someone gave a sword to another to keep in trust. Later, in a moment of rage, the owner comes to demand it back so that he might commit a murder. But the keeper of the sword denied it, saying, "You gave me nothing." And behold:

### Lecture Sixteen
**Because philosophy has branched into the theoretical and the practical, it is necessary to know that each of these is further subdivided into two parts.**

<column-break/>

And first and foremost is the **theoretical** branch, for it is more honorable than the **practical**. This is because in the theoretical, all existing things are the subjects of study; but in the practical, only human souls are the concern. 

One must know that **Plato** <note>original: "Platovn"</note> subdivides the theoretical in one way, and **Aristotle** in another. Plato subdivides the theoretical into the **natural** <term>pnabanakan; the study of physical nature</term> and the **theological** <term>astvatsabanakan; the study of divine things</term>. He does not wish for the **mathematical** <term>usumnakan; literally 'of the studies,' referring here to the mathematical sciences</term> branch to be considered a part of philosophy itself; rather, he sees it as a preliminary training <note>propaedeutic</note>, much like grammar and rhetoric. 

For this reason, he inscribed above the entrance to his university: "Let no one ignorant of geometry enter." He wrote this because Plato treats many things theologically and uses mathematics as a preparation for theology; the mathematical sciences act as a collaborator in order to know theology, of which geometry is a part.

**Aristotle**, however, subdivides the theoretical into three: the **natural**, the **mathematical**, and the **theological**. He classifies the mathematical branch as a formal part of philosophy, treating it as a distinct field of knowledge. 

And so, these matters stand thus. But it is necessary to ask: why is the theoretical divided into three parts? To which we say, that the theoretical...

<meta>Continuing from the discussion on the division of philosophy into theoretical and practical branches, this page explores the three subdivisions of theoretical philosophy based on the relationship between matter and thought, as taught by David the Invincible.</meta>

...all things are subject to being known. Furthermore, existing things are threefold: they exist either through their **essence** <note>literally "subject-matter" (Armenian: ենթակայ)</note> or through **contemplation**.

First, there are things that are **material** <term>nyut'avor (նիւթաւոր) — composed of physical matter</term> both in their essence and in our contemplation of them, such as wood, stone, and all other visible things. For these things are material in their underlying substance and in how we think about them; because it is impossible to conceive of a stone <unclear>existing</unclear> without matter.

Second, there are things that are **immaterial** <term>annyut' (աննիւթ) — without physical matter</term> both in essence and in contemplation, such as an angel, God, the mind, and the soul. For these are immaterial in their essence and in our conceptualization of them; because it is not possible to conceptualize God, an angel, or the mind as being material.

Third, there are things that are material in essence but immaterial in contemplation, such as **shapes**. For shapes are material in their essence; a triangle, a square, or any other shape cannot exist without matter; rather, they have their existence in a stone, in wood, or in some other material. However, they are immaterial in our contemplation. For when someone imagines a shape through **reasoning** <note>Armenian: տրամախոհութիւն</note>, they impress that very shape upon their contemplation by itself. Just as a piece of wax <unclear>taking</unclear> the impression of a signet ring receives the image of the seal alone into itself, and does not take any of the other material <note>original: "իմաստնոյն" (of the wise/skilful), though likely a scribe's error for "iron" or "gold"</note>. In the same 

<column-break/>

way, when reasoning imagines shapes within itself, it takes nothing from the physical matter. Instead, it holds the very essence of the shape and the logic of it within itself.

Now, because all things are subject to being known by the **theoretical** branch, and as we have said that these things are threefold, the theoretical branch is therefore divided into three parts:
1. **Natural Science** <term>Physics (բնաբանական)</term>
2. **Mathematics** <term>The Study Branch (ուսումնական)</term>
3. **Theology** <term>Divine Science (ածաբանական)</term>

And it must be known that **Natural Science** is ordered toward those things which are material in both essence and contemplation. **Theology** is concerned with those things which are immaterial in both essence and contemplation. And **Mathematics** is ordered toward those things which are material in essence but immaterial in contemplation.

Now, these things being so, let us speak of their arrangement. One must know that **Natural Science** holds the first rank because it is familiar to us and connected to our life, being entirely material. **Mathematics** holds the middle rank—that is, between Natural Science and Theology—because it shares in both: it is material like Natural Science, yet immaterial like Theology. Finally, **Theology**, of necessity...

### Page 54

...received the higher order. For it is not possible to ascend from **physics** <term>bnabanakan (բնաբանական) — the study of the natural, material world</term> to **theology** <term>astvatsabanakan (աստուածաբանական) — the study of divine, immaterial things</term> immediately. For one must first move from that which is entirely material toward that which is entirely immaterial. Otherwise, we suffer the same fate as those who, after staying in a dark place for a long time, suddenly attempt to gaze at the sun and are immediately blinded.

Now, so it is with those who attempt to move from the entirely material directly to the entirely immaterial. Poetry allegorized this in the story of Otus and Ephialtes <note>original: "ատոսի և Փիլոտոսի" — giants from Greek mythology</note>, who dared to place Mt. Ossa upon Mt. Olympus, and upon Ossa, the leaf-shaking Mt. Pelion, so that heaven might become a walkway. This signifies nothing else than that those acting as philosophers attempted to go from the entirely material to the entirely immaterial without the **mathematical sciences** <term>usumnakan (ուսումնական) — literally "the study-able" sciences; the middle step of the curriculum</term>. Therefore, one must ascend from physics to theology [through this middle step].

And that it is necessary to employ the mathematical sciences after physics was commanded by the wonderful Platonist <note>original: "պղատոնիոս" (Platonius); likely referring to a Neoplatonic teacher or commentator</note>, saying: "The mathematical sciences should be handed down to the beginners to familiarize them with **incorporeal nature**..."

<column-break/>

...nature; through which the knowledge of the existence of incorporeal nature is gained. 

But some are perplexed, saying: "Since you possess not only the mathematical from these, but also the physical and the theological, why is only this one called 'mathematical' <note>meaning "that which is learned"</note>?" To which certain **Pythagoreans** <note>followers of Pythagoras, who emphasized the role of numbers in the universe</note> answer that it is right for this alone to be called "mathematical." This is because it possesses its perception through **reasoning** <term>tramakhohut'yun (տրամախոհութիւն) — discursive or logical thought</term>. Whereas that which is learned is naturally suited to reasoning alone, for the mind simply grasps existing things.

...they spoke regarding these realities. But what we are about to say is certain and reliable, which we must make known through five chapters:

First: who and how many are the types of the **mathematical sciences** <term>usumnakan (ուսումնական) — literally "the sciences to be learned," referring to the Quadrivium</term>.
Second: in which we state the two reasons for their names.
Third: in which we discuss the arrangement of these types.
Fourth: in which we state who were the discoverers of the types of mathematical sciences.
Fifth: in which we state which types are associated with these types.

Now, let us come to the first chapter and say who and how many are the types of mathematical sciences. It must be known that there are four types: **Arithmetic**, **Music**, **Geometry**, and **Astronomy**.

Let us come to the second chapter and state the reasons for the names of the types of mathematical sciences. It must be known that the mathematical exists through **quantity** <term>k’anak (քանակ) — the category of size, number, or "how much"</term>, and is defined by these: either it is **Arithmetic**, which is quantity itself; or it is the composition of sounds, like **Music**, for this too is quantity. For it <note>Music</note> seeks to know which sound has a **double ratio** <note>original: "երկպատիկ" (2:1 ratio)</note>, and which has a **hemiolic ratio** <note>original: "կիսաբոլոր" (3:2 ratio)</note>, and which an **epitritic ratio** <note>original: "մակեռանդ" (4:3 ratio)</note>, and which an **epioctave ratio** <note>original: "մակուքառասն" (9:8 ratio)</note>.

<column-break/>

Or it is the extension of the earth, like **Geometry**, for this too is quantity; or by the movement of the celestial bodies, like **Astronomy**, for this too is quantity, as they have movements and extensions according to which they move.

Now, these things being so, and it being known that the mathematical exists through quantity, it must be known that quantity is twofold: either **continuous** or **discrete**.

**Continuous quantity** <term>sharunak (շարունակ) — also meaning "ongoing" or "connected"</term> is that whose parts are joined together at a single common boundary, like the stone of a wall, which is continuous quantity. For if you were to cut and divide all its parts **potentially** <term>zorut’yamb (զաւրութբ) — in the mind or as a possibility, rather than physically</term> in many places, the cut and divided parts would still be joined at a single boundary.

For this segment is understood as joined to that segment when you take a conceptual line to be the division. By taking the divisions through the power of a conceptual line, and that conceptual line being the midpoint between the potential divisions, this part is found to be joined to that by the conceptual line, and that one to another by a conceptual line; and through the conceptual line they are joined to one another.

However, we said that the continuous is divided **potentially** and not **in actuality** <term>azdmamb (ազդմամբ) — also "by effect" or "operationally"</term>; for that which is divided in actuality becomes **discrete** <term>tarorosh (տարորոշ) — separated or distinct</term> and is cast out from the nature of the continuous. But...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Mathematical Sciences" (the Quadrivium), specifically defining the nature of quantity as the foundation for the four disciplines: Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, and Astronomy. It follows the previous page's introduction of the five chapters of this study.</meta>

...the discrete quantity is defined, having nothing in between by which it can be joined to another, as is number. For ten is discrete; even if one takes it as composed of two fives, there is no shared number in the middle of the fives by which they are joined to one another. For if one takes a single unit in the middle of the fives, it ceases to be ten <note>The author is explaining that discrete numbers have no common boundary; if you try to find a "middle" point that belongs to both halves, you change the total count.</note>.

**A**nd now, these things being so. **B**ut it must be known that each of these types of quantity is spoken of in two ways: that which is said *per se* <note>original: "ըստ ինքեան" — meaning "by itself" or absolute</note> and that which is said by comparison <note>original: "ըստ բաղդատութե" — meaning relative to something else</note>.

And *per se* is when we take a number by itself, as when we take the number ten on its own, not comparing it to another number. But "by comparison" is when we compare one of the numbers to another, as ten to five, saying that ten is the double of five.

Furthermore, continuous quantity is twofold: for there is that which is immobile, and that which is mobile. Immobile is like the earth, for it does not move from this place to another place. But mobile is like the heavens, which are ever-moving.

**N**ow, these things being thus: since the mathematical <term>usumnakan (ուսումնական) — the sciences of the Quadrivium</term>, as we said before, is composed of

<column-break/>

quantity. And we said quantity is twofold: discrete and continuous. And again, each of these is twofold: the discrete is *per se* and by comparison; and the continuous is immobile and mobile, which together make four types.

**N**ow, according to the correspondence of these four types of quantity, the four mathematical sciences exist: **Arithmetic**, **Music**, **Geometry**, and **Astronomy**.

**O**f these, **Arithmetic** is composed of discrete quantity *per se*. But **Music** is from discrete quantity by comparison. **Geometry** is from immobile continuous quantity. **Astronomy** is from mobile continuous quantity.

**W**ith this, the second chapter also ends. Let us come to the third chapter and speak of the order of the arrangement of the types of mathematical science.

**A**nd it must be known that **Arithmetic** and **Music** received the first rank in the arrangement, rather than **Geometry** and **Astronomy**, because **Arithmetic** and **Music** are composed of discrete quantity. But **Geometry** and **Astronomy** are composed of continuous quantity. And discrete quantity is the cause... <note>Scholastic philosophy generally held that "discrete" number is simpler and more fundamental than "continuous" magnitude, and therefore must be taught first.</note>

<summary>This page defines the four branches of the Quadrivium based on the division of quantity into discrete (number/sound) and continuous (space/motion). It establishes a hierarchy of the sciences, placing Arithmetic and Music before Geometry and Astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Quadrivium, Quantity, Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, Astronomy, David the Invincible, Discrete Quantity, Continuous Quantity</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical differentiation between discrete and continuous quantities, establishing a hierarchy for the four mathematical sciences (the Quadrivium): Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, and Astronomy. It follows from the previous page's definition of quantity.</meta>

...is more excellent than the continuous. This is because **discrete quantity** is capable of receiving various species distinctly and without confusion. For example, twenty-five and five, being discrete quantities, received distinct species without confusion, being both "circular" and "square." It is "circular" because, just as in a circle the end is joined back to the beginning, the same point can be seen as both the start and the completion. Likewise here, having begun from five and multiplying, it ended in a circular fashion at twenty-five; and that same five—as five times five is twenty-five—behold, having begun from five, it ended in five <note>The author is describing "automorphic" or "circular" numbers in ancient mathematics, where the power of a number ends in the same digit as the number itself (e.g., 5² = 25).</note>. 

Now, as for it being a "square": every number multiplied by itself completes a square number. As three times three is nine; four times four is sixteen; likewise five times five is twenty-five. 

But **continuous quantity** cannot receive various species without confusion. For wax, which is a continuous quantity, if it is not molded according to the shape of a figure, it cannot receive other figures unless the previous one is first destroyed; otherwise, there is confusion. Now, since discrete quantity is more excellent than the continuous,

<column-break/>

for this reason, the science of numbers, <term>Arithmetic (թուական) — the study of absolute numbers</term>, being composed of discrete quantity, received the first rank, ahead of Geometry and Astronomy. 

Furthermore, Arithmetic is ranked before <term>Music (երաժշտական) — here meaning the study of ratios and proportions</term>. For Arithmetic, as we said before, consists of discrete quantity *per se* <note>original: "ըստ ինքեանն" — absolute or by itself</note>. But Music consists of discrete quantity in relation to harmony <note>original: "ըստ բաղազանութե" — meaning the relationship or ratio between two sounds</note>. That which exists "by itself" is prior to that which exists "in relation"; for simply being is prior to being "in a certain way." 

Likewise, <term>Geometry (երկրաչափական)</term> is prior to <term>Astronomy (աստեղաբաշխական)</term>. This is because Geometry consists of immobile continuous quantity, while Astronomy consists of mobile continuous quantity. And the immobile is prior to the mobile, because immobility is the beginning of motion. For everything that is destined to move has its beginning of motion from rest and immobility. These things concern the ordering of the branches of the mathematical sciences.

Let us come now to the fourth chapter and discuss who were the discoverers of these branches. It must be known that the **Phoenicians** discovered Arithmetic, as they were merchants and had a need for calculation for the purpose of counting.

<summary>This page explains why Arithmetic is the primary mathematical science, arguing that discrete numbers are more "excellent" than continuous forms like wax. It establishes a hierarchy of the Quadrivium based on whether a quantity is absolute or relative, and immobile or mobile.</summary>

<keywords>Arithmetic, Music, Geometry, Astronomy, discrete quantity, continuous quantity, circular numbers, square numbers, Phoenicians, Quadrivium</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four mathematical sciences, moving from their definitions to their historical origins and their practical versus theoretical branches.</meta>

As for **Music**, the Thracians [discovered it], because Orpheus was from there, of whom they say he first discovered music. As for **Astronomy**, the Chaldeans [discovered it], because since they were always in a state of tranquility there <note>The author refers to the clear, calm skies of Mesopotamia</note>, they were easily able to grasp the movements of the stars. As for **Geometry**, the Egyptians found it out of necessity; for when the Nile River flooded over Egypt and confused the boundaries of the fields, quarrels and killings occurred among the Egyptians over each boundary—until they devised a measure called the <term>skhen <note>original: "սխէնն" (sxēn), referring to the Greek "schoenus," an ancient unit of land measurement</note></term>. By applying this, they found the boundaries of each field. From this, Geometry was discovered. 

And let us now proceed to the fifth chapter, and let us state what kinds [of sciences] are associated with these types. And it must be known that with **Arithmetic**, <term>Calculation (համարողական) — the practical art of counting</term> is associated. But they differ from one another, because Arithmetic seeks and examines the nature of number itself; according to its subtle understanding, number is composed of units and does not divide the unit. But Calculation is composed of perceptible counting and divides the unit into halves, thirds, and fourths <note>In ancient theory, Arithmetic dealt with the properties of integers, while "Calculation" or "Logistic" dealt with practical fractions and trade</note>. And with Music is associated <insert>material</insert>

<column-break/>

music—that is, it is composed of **lutes**, **pipes**, and **cymbals**. But **True Music**, being useful through reason alone, constitutes itself. And with **Geometry** is associated <term>Land-surveying (երկրաբաժանական) — the practical division of earth</term>. For Geometry is composed of reason, but Land-surveying is composed of physical work. And with **Astronomy** is associated <term>Spherics (գնդական) — the study of spherical bodies</term>; and they differ from one another. For Astronomy is composed of the knowledge of the movement of the heavenly bodies, but Spherics deals with the spherical body itself and its properties; for it speaks of various sections of spherical things. 

And it must be known that the power of Music is great, grasping and disposing the soul through various emotions, as is shown by hums and laments, which dispose the soul according to their own nature. As some tell the story concerning **Alexander [the Great]**: when he was at a banquet, the musician played a warlike tune; [Alexander] immediately armed himself and rushed out. But then, the musician struck a joyful tune, and he returned again to the feast.

<meta>This page transitions from the historical origins of the mathematical sciences (the quadrivium) to a formal logical lecture on the classification of philosophy and the various methods of conceptual division, common in late antique philosophical commentaries like those of David the Invincible or Elias.</meta>

With these things [now] prepared. And here begins:

### **Lecture 10**

And it is handed down that **Philosophy** is divided into the **Theoretical** and the **Practical**. Furthermore, the **Theoretical** is divided into the **Natural** <note>original: "բնաբանական" (physical), the study of nature</note>, the **Mathematical** <note>original: "յուսումնական" (pertaining to learning), specifically the four sciences of arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy</note>, and the **Theological**. 

Henceforth, let us pause and state in what manner the division of these subjects occurred. For we shall speak of the **Practical** and the matters of the Practical later on. But let us first learn how many modes of division there are.

Thus, it must be known that some say there are **eight modes of division**, and they are as follows:

1. From **Genus** to **Species**.
2. From **Species** to **Individuals**.
3. From the **Whole** to the **Parts**. (And this is twofold: either it occurs in **homogeneous** <note>original: "նմանամասնեայս" (parts-like-the-whole)</note> parts or in **heterogeneous** <note>original: "աննմանամասնեայս" (parts-unlike-the-whole)</note> parts).
4. From a **Homonymous** <note>original: "հոմանուն" (words with the same sound but different meanings)</note> term to its various significations.
5. From **Essence** to **Accident**.
6. From **Accident** to **Essence**.
7. From **Accident** to **Accident**.
8. From one thing [as a source] and toward one [as a goal]. 

Now, <term>as</term> <gloss>as</gloss> an example of **Genus to Species**: when we divide "Animal" into "Rational" and "Irrational." 

From **Species to Individuals**: when we divide the general category of "Human" into "Socrates," "Plato," and "Alcibiades,"

<column-break/>

and into each particular individual. 

From the **Whole to Parts** in a **homogeneous** manner: as when we divide a whole vein into smaller veins, which are similar in nature to the whole, because they possess the same nature as the whole. 

From the **Whole to Parts** in a **heterogeneous** manner: as when we divide the whole head into eyes, ears, and nostrils, which are unlike the whole and are not even like each other. 

From a **Homonymous term** to various significations: as when we divide the word "dog" <note>original: "շուն" (shun)</note> into the celestial [the Dog Star], the terrestrial [the animal], and the marine [the dogfish]. 

From **Essence to Accident**: as when we say that among humans, some are white and some are black. 

From **Accident to Essence**: as when we say that among white things, some are snow and some are wool. 

From **Accident to Accident**: as when we say that among white things, some are hot and some are cold. "Hot" as in food, or a hand. 

From **one source**: as when we say "medical book," "medical drug," or "medical tool." 

Toward **one goal**: when we say "healthy book," "healthy house," or "healthy drugs," because we call them healthy in order to reveal their relation to [the goal of] health. 

And these things are so. But it must be known that from these aforementioned modes...

<meta>The previous page listed eight traditional modes of division used in logic. This page begins a critical analysis, arguing that only three of these are "strictly" or "truly" valid modes of division, while explaining the logical flaws in the others.</meta>

There are strictly three modes of division: 
as from **Genus** to **Species**; as from the **Whole** 
to **Parts**; and as from a **Homonymous** sound to its 
various significations <note>original: "Հոմանուն" (homanun) – terms that sound the same but have different meanings, like "bank" (of a river) and "bank" (financial institution)</note>. As for the other modes, 
they cannot be [properly] constituted, as 
we shall demonstrate. For a division 
cannot occur from **Species** to **Individuals**; 
for individuals are infinite and indefinite in number <note>The author argues that because specific individuals (like a single horse or a specific person) are born and die, they cannot be fixed in a formal logical system</note>, since some 
perish and others are born, which we cannot 
include in a [fixed] number. Furthermore, division 
from **Accident** to **Essence** <note>original: "պատաՀմանէ յէութիւն" – "Accident" here means a non-essential property, like color, while "Essence" is the nature of the thing itself</note> is not strictly 
a mode of division. 
For when we say, "of white things, some are snow 
and some are wool," it is not truly a mode of division. 
For it is not the "whiteness" itself, which exists 
independently of a body, that is being divided; rather, it is the body 
in which the whiteness exists that is divided. But a body is an 
**Essence**; therefore, **Essence** is dividing **Essence**, 
and it is not the **Accident** dividing the **Essence**. And again, 
everything that is divided, is divided by its like and not by its 
unlike. For when **Animal** is divided into **Rational** 
and **Irrational**, the **Rational** is divided from the **Animal**, 
and the **Irrational** is divided from the **Animal**; it is not 
divided from that which is "not-animal," which is its unlike. But 
in dividing from **Accident** to **Essence**, it is divided 
by its unlike. Therefore, according to this example, it is not 

<column-break/>

strictly called a mode of division when **Essence** is divided 
into **Accidents**, nor when an **Accident** 
is divided into **Accidents**. For to say that 
"of white things, some are hot and some are cold," does not 
divide the **Accident** itself according 
to its own properties; rather, it divides the bodies 
in which the accidents reside. This also cannot be 
called a mode of division "from a single source" <note>referring to the 8th mode mentioned on the previous page</note>, 
as it seems to me. 
For from a single source they are indefinite and countless; according to the same reasoning 
regarding the source. 
Therefore, there are three strictly true 
modes of division: as from **Genus** to **Species**; 
as from the **Whole** to **Parts**; and as from a **Homonymous** 
sound to its various significations. <margin>†</margin> But 
it is also necessary to state the causes of the modes of division— 
those of beings, and those pertaining to beings—to judge 
them from every [type of] division. 
In this way and according to this example: 
everything that is at once divisible, is divided 
either according to **Essence** itself, or 
according to **Accident**. And if it is divided 
according to [Essence] itself, it is divided either as a **Thing** 
or as a **Sound** <note>original: "ձայն" (dzayn) – voice or phonetic word</note>. If it is divided as a 
**Thing**, these modes of division occur: 
as from **Genus** to **Species**; as from **Species** to **Individuals**; 
as from the **Whole** to **Parts**; as from a single source. 
For these were naturally constituted to be divided as **Things**. 
But if the mode of division occurs as a **Sound**...

<summary>This page analyzes the logic of "division," arguing that only three types (Genus to Species, Whole to Parts, and Homonyms) are strictly valid, while rejecting divisions based on "accidents" (temporary properties) or "individuals" as being logically unstable.</summary>

...as a **homonymous** sound <note>original: "հոմանուն ձայն" (homanun dzayn) — a word that sounds the same but has different meanings</note> into its various significations. 
But if the thing being divided is naturally

Along with these things, the following 
Lectures—the Nineteenth Lecture—
having discussed the modes of division: 
The sixteenth [point]: according to which 
mode is **Philosophy** <term>Philosophy: իմաստասիրութիւն (imastasirut’yun), literally 'love of wisdom'</term> divided into the 
**theoretical** and the **practical**? First, it must be known 
that it cannot be divided as a **genus** into **species**. This is because 
in a division of opposite species, there is 
no "last" or "first"—as when an **animal** is 
divided into **rational** and **irrational**. One cannot say 
that the rational is prior to the irrational, 
nor the irrational prior to the rational; rather, 
they are equal and simultaneous. But in the 
theoretical and the practical, there is a first and a last. 
For the theoretical is first, because by means 
of the theoretical and of reason 
comes the practical; so that the 
practical might not be irrational and without cause. 
For this reason, the division into the 
theoretical and the practical cannot be 
as from a **genus** to **species**. Secondly, because 

opposite species do not have 
need of one another in order to exist. But in the 
theoretical and the practical, it is not so; 
rather, the practical has need of the theoretical, 
by which it is constituted and ordered, so that it 
might not be irrational and without 
cause. Instead, one must first examine 
by nature that which is "good" through the **theoretical**, 
and thus by it constitute the **practical**. 
Therefore, philosophy is not divided into 
theoretical and practical as a **genus** into **species**. 
Nor is it as a **species** into **individuals**; because individuals 
are infinite and cannot be contained [in a system]. 
But these are not infinite; they are only two: the 
theoretical and the practical. Furthermore, individuals 
are shown as parts. But these are not; it is 
evident that philosophy cannot be divided into the 
theoretical and the practical as a **species** into **individuals**. 
Nor is it as a **homonymous** sound into its various 
significations. For in homonyms, there is no 
first and last; for no one says that the 
celestial dog <note>The constellation Canis Major</note> is prior to the terrestrial one <note>A literal dog</note>, 
or the terrestrial prior to the marine one <note>A dogfish or shark</note>. 
But here, as we previously demonstrated, 
the theoretical is prior to the practical. 
Therefore, philosophy cannot 
be divided into the theoretical and the practical as...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of logical divisions from page 61. Having previously argued that Philosophy is not divided like a genus into species, the author now examines other logical modes, such as essence and accident, before settling on the relationship between a whole and its parts.</meta>

...**homonymously** <note>original: "հոմանունաձայն" (homanunadzayn) — words that sound the same but have different meanings</note> into various signified things.
Furthermore, it is not as an **essence** <note>original: "ութիւն" — likely a scribal error for "էութիւն" (ēut’yun), meaning substance or essence</note> into an **accident** <term>accident: պատահումն (patahum), a logical term for a property that is not essential to a thing's nature</term>; nor as an accident into an essence. This is because **Philosophy** is not an essence, nor is the **theoretical**, nor the **practical**; rather, they are accidents. Furthermore, it is not as an accident into an accident. For an accident [divided] into an accident is divided either as a **genus** into **species**, or as an essence into essence. Now, we have shown that it is not divided as a genus into species, nor as an essence into essence, because it is an accident. 
Furthermore, it is not as a **whole** into **parts**, neither in the manner of **similar parts** <note>original: "նմանամասնաբար" (nmanamasnabar) — homogeneous parts, like water where every drop is still water</note> nor in the manner of **dissimilar parts** <note>original: "աննմանամասնաբար" (annmanamasnabar) — heterogeneous parts, like a human body made of hands and eyes</note>. This is because similar parts are called homonymously [the same] as each other and as the whole. 
Whereas the theoretical and the practical, although they are called homonymously [Philosophy] like the whole, they are not called so of each other. 
Furthermore, it is not [a division into] dissimilar parts. This is because those things divided into dissimilar parts are called homonymously neither of the whole, nor of each other.
Whereas the theoretical and the practical: although they are not called homonymously of each other, they are called so of the whole; for that [the theoretical] is Philosophy, and this [the practical] is too. 
But now, let us understand from what has been said how the division of Philosophy occurred as if from a single genus; for from a certain single Philosophy, both the theoretical and the practical were named "Philosophy."

<column-break/>

<page-num>62</page-num>

2, but however **Aristotle** says that it was divided like the **flute** <note>original: "ըստրինջն" (ustrinjn), likely a corruption of "սրինգն" (sringn) — the Greek *syrinx* or pan-pipe. Neoplatonic commentators used the flute as an example of a whole composed of integral parts.</note> into its parts; and he says this well. 
Because the theoretical and the practical are parts of Philosophy. And just as when one part is missing, the whole must be incomplete, in the same way, if the practical is not present, Philosophy is incomplete. 
For it is necessary for the perfect philosopher to be adorned not only by the theoretical, but also by the practical. 
And if someone should ask: into what did the division occur—into similar parts or into dissimilar parts? 
We say: into similar parts. Because they are called homonymously both of the whole and of each other, which is a specific property of similar parts: to be called homonymously of the whole and of themselves. 
It is similar here: for just as Philosophy is called Philosophy, so too the theoretical and the practical are called Philosophy. 
Only this, and even more importantly: the theoretical is called practical, and the practical theoretical. 
The theoretical is called practical because it is the "work" of the mind. While the practical is called theoretical because the practical is the offspring of the theoretical. For having first found through the theoretical that **temperance** <term>temperance: ողջախոհութիւն (oghjakhonut’yun), also translated as prudence or sound-mindedness; a primary virtue</term> is good, one establishes the same through action. 
Now we have learned how the division in Philosophy occurred as [a division] of a whole...

<meta>This page continues the logical investigation from page 62 regarding how Philosophy is divided. The previous page ended by discussing the division of a whole into similar parts (homogeneous) and dissimilar parts (heterogeneous), specifically noting that the branches of Philosophy are not called by each other's names.</meta>

...into parts, in the manner of similar parts. Let us now come and speak of how the **theoretical** branch was divided into the **natural**, the **mathematical**, and the **theological**. <note>original: "բնաբանականն, ուսումնականն, և յածաբանականն" — these represent the three traditional divisions of theoretical philosophy in the Aristotelian tradition: Physics, Mathematics, and Metaphysics/Theology.</note> 

It must then be understood that this was not a division of a **genus** <term>genus: սեռ (ser), a general category that can be divided into specific types</term> into **species** <term>species: տեսակ (tesak), the specific sub-types that fall under a genus</term>. For a division of opposite species does not have a "first" and a "last" <note>In formal logic, species of the same genus are considered equal and simultaneous, not ranked in a sequence</note>. However, these are ordered: first and foremost the **natural**, then the **mathematical**, and then the **theological**. 

Furthermore, a genus is never naturally divided into three, while the **theoretical** is divided into three: the natural, the mathematical, and the theological. And it is necessary to know why this was not a division of a genus into species. 

Because of this, some say: "How is it that the **animate** <note>original: "շնչականն" (shnchakan) — literally 'possessing breath' or 'having a soul'</note>, being a genus, was divided into three: the animal, the zoophyte <note>original: "կենդանաբոյսն" (kendanabuysn) — organisms like sponges or corals thought to be between animals and plants</note>, and the plant?" But to this we shall say that we intend to show that such a division is not proper. 

Again, in opposition, they say: "Behold, **Rhetoric**, being a genus, is divided into three species: the **judicial**, the **deliberative**, and the **panegyric**." <note>original: "յատենականն, ի բարեխոհականն, ի կացրդականն" — the three classical branches of Greek rhetoric (forensic, deliberative, and epideictic)</note>. But to this we shall say that this division is also not proper. For Rhetoric is not said to be divided into these as a genus into species, because among opposite species, there is no distinction of time. For example, the **rational** and the

<column-break/>

**irrational**, when divided, have no temporal division. For we cannot say that the rational exists in the past time and the irrational in the present or the future. 

But the so-called species of Rhetoric do have temporal distinctions from one another. For the **deliberative** branch exists for the sake of future time; because when someone comes to counsel another, they deliberate concerning what is to come. But the **judicial** exists for the sake of past time; for the accuser judges those things which have already been done. And the **panegyric** <note>original: "կացրդականն" (katsrdakan) — also known as epideictic or demonstrative rhetoric, used for praise or blame</note> exists regarding the present; for it possesses within itself the presentation of the virtues present in the subjects. 

Therefore, it is evident that these are not truly species, nor is the genus [properly] divided; thus, the division of Rhetoric into these mentioned species is not proper.

Furthermore, the **theoretical** is not said to be divided as a **whole** into **parts**, neither into **similar parts** <note>homogeneous parts</note> nor into **dissimilar parts** <note>heterogeneous parts</note>. Not into similar parts because, although they are not called by each other's names **homonymously** <note>original: "հոմանունաբար" (homanunabar) — meaning 'sharing the same name'</note>, they are all called by the name of the whole. For the natural is called a "species" [of the theoretical], as are the mathematical and the theological. 

For as we have said many times, it is a property of similar parts to be called by the name of the whole and of each other homonymously. Furthermore, it is not [divided into] dissimilar parts...

<meta>catchword: նա- (na-)</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the division of Theoretical Philosophy into Natural, Mathematical, and Theological branches is not a simple genus-to-species division, because these branches follow a specific hierarchical order and temporal distinctions that true logical species do not possess.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of how Philosophy is divided into its constituent parts. The previous page established that the branches of philosophy are not "similar parts" (like water from water) but "dissimilar parts" (like hands or feet of a body), and it began discussing why these divisions do not follow the standard rules of a "genus" dividing into "species."</meta>

...-ly <note>Continuing from the previous page: "homonymously"</note>, for it is characteristic of dissimilar parts that the whole is not called homonymously by the names of its parts. But these [branches of philosophy], although they are not called homonymously of one another, they are called so of the whole. Furthermore, a theoretical branch is not said to be divided into individuals **as** a species. And **not** as an essence into an accident <term>accident: պատահումն (patahum), a property that a thing can lose without ceasing to be itself, like being tan or tall</term>. And **not** as an accident into an essence. And **not** as an accident into another accident. And **not** as a homonymous term into various meanings—**because of** the reasons mentioned previously—but they are [related] **as** "from one" and "toward one." <note>original: "իմիովն և ասամի" (i miovn ev ar mi) — This is a translation of the Greek philosophical concept *aph’ enos kai pros hen*. It describes things that aren't exactly the same, but all relate back to a single source or goal.</note> **Because** the natural, the mathematical, and the **theological** <note>original: "ածաբանականն" (astvatsabanakann) — literally 'God-speaking'</note>, **as** if from a single species, were each called "theoretical."

And so it is regarding these things. Let us now come and speak of how the mathematical branch is divided into arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy. And it must be said that this genus does not divide into species. And **not** **as** a species into individuals. And **not** **as** a whole into parts. And **not** **as** a homonymous term into various meanings. And **not** **as** essence into accident. And **not** **as** accident into essence, and **not** **as** accident into accident—**because of** the previously stated reasons. But [they are related] **as** "from one" and "toward one." **Because** arithmetic, music, geometry, and astronomy—**as** from the single [source] of the mathematical—were each called "mathematical."

### With these things, it is concluded. And next 
### is the Lesson: The Twentieth Lesson.
### Ending the division of the theoretical branch.

**Let us come** also to the division of the **practical** branch <term>practical philosophy: գործական (gortsakan), the branch of philosophy dealing with action, ethics, and politics</term>. For although it is prepared within the theoretical—**because** through the theoretical and through reason it adorns the moral and subdues the passions—one should not, however, despise it. **Because**, **as** Plato says: "I call a philosopher not the one who knows much, nor the one who is able to grasp many things in the mind, but the one who leads a pure and spotless life." **According** to this, the truly perfect philosopher is not the one who knows much, but the one who can subdue the passions. But now, let us come and state the division of the practical branch. 

And it is necessary to know that the Aristotelians divide the practical branch in one way, and the Platonists in another. For the Aristotelians divide it into the political, the economic, and the moral. But perhaps it is divided into three **because** when someone adorns character—when they adorn the character of a single individual—it becomes the "moral."

...[management] of the house, which becomes **domestic**; or of the city, which becomes **political**. And that these are divided into three, Aristotle shows in his own writings. 1.  The sincerity of a husband and wife toward each other.
2.  The love for children.
3.  The fear <note>Meaning respect or discipline</note> of servants.
4.  The moderation of income and expenses.

**Because** the immoderation of either [income or expense] is of a worse nature. Yet regarding the "Politics" [as a subject], the intention, the composition, and the title differ. <column-break/>

-tle titled his in the plural <note>Refers to the *Politics* or *Politika*</note>. Therefore the intention is also different, because Aristotle says how the ancients lived—that is, the Argeians, the Boeotians, and all other **Greeks**. From this, one must know that the Platonists <note>original: "պղատոնականքն" (pghatonakankn)</note> speak against the Aristotelians <note>original: "արիստոտէլականացն" (aristotelakanatsn)</note> regarding the division of the **practical** branch [of philosophy]. First, because a **genus** <term>genus: սեռ (serr), a general category that can be divided into specific types</term> never divides into three, but into two; just as "animal" divides into **rational** and **irrational**, or as "color" divides into white and black. 

Second, because nowhere in a divided species is one seen within the other; for the rational is never seen in the irrational, nor is the irrational seen in the rational. But here, the **moral** is seen within the **domestic**, and the **domestic** is seen within the **political**. 

Third, these [three parts] are never equal to the genus individually. For the rational alone cannot be equal to "animal" (which is the genus), nor can the irrational alone; only both together [equal the genus]. Yet here, the **political** alone is [said to be] equal to the **practical**. For as the Aristotelians say, the practical branch is divided into the moral, the domestic, and the political—which contains...

After the undertaking <note>The "undertaking" or "argument" presented by a specific school of thought.</note> of the **Platonists** against the **Aristotelians** regarding the division of the **practical** into the **ethical**, the **economical** <note>original: "տնտեսականն" (tntesakann) — referring to household management rather than modern finance.</note>, and the **political**: they themselves henceforth undertake to divide the **practical** into two only: the **legislative** and the **judicial**.

a city, for the sake of legislating according to how each ought to conduct their life. And the judge either judges the individual, or the house, or the city.

This also became clear through the things said previously; for we learned it through the **definitions** and through the **division**. Through the division, we learned that it is **theoretical** and **practical**; for it is divided into these alone.

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the nature of philosophy. Having previously discussed the division of "practical" philosophy into legislative and judicial branches (a Platonist view), the author now argues for the superiority of philosophy over all other arts and sciences. The text uses the famous "six definitions of philosophy" standard in the medieval Armenian curriculum, derived from the Neoplatonic school of Alexandria (specifically the works of David the Invincible).</meta>

... [philosophy] alone is truly so. For other arts do not know all beings, but only one specifically determined thing; for they do not concern themselves with **theology**. But **philosophy** <note>original: "իմաստասիրութիւն" (imastasirut’iwn) — literally "love of wisdom."</note> is concerned with all beings and with divine things. Furthermore, philosophy alone is truly called **practical** <note>original: "գործական" (gortsakan)</note>. For other arts are either about the body—as is the **medical** art—or about those things outside the body—as are **carpentry**, **construction**, and **blacksmithing**.

But philosophy is concerned with the **soul**. For the soul, corrupted by bodily passions and pleasures and left incomplete, is naturally disposed to be raised up and enlightened by means of philosophy. Now, just as the soul is more precious than the body, so too that which adorns the soul—philosophy—is more precious than other arts which adorn the body. For this reason, it is called "practical" more than other arts, because it adorns the impulses <note>or "movements" of the mind.</note> of the soul.

Now, having learned through division what philosophy is—that it is **theoretical** <note>original: "տեսական" (tesakan)</note> and **practical**—we have also come to know through the **definitions** <note>original: "սահմանաց" (sahmanats). These refer to the six canonical definitions of philosophy used in medieval Armenian education.</note> what kind of thing philosophy is. For through the definitions based on the **subject matter**—that is, the one which says: "Philosophy is the knowledge of beings as they are beings," and "Philosophy is the knowledge...

<column-break/>

<page-num>67</page-num>
...of divine and human things"—it follows that philosophy is **theoretical**.

And through the definition based on **excellence** <note>or "pre-eminence" over other fields.</note>, which says: "Philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences," we learned that philosophy is **sovereign**.

And through the definition based on the **proximate end** <note>The "proximate end" refers to the immediate goal or result.</note>, which says: "Philosophy is the meditation on death," <note>original: "կանխամահումն" (kanxamahumn). This is a Platonic concept suggesting that the philosopher prepares for the soul's release from the body.</note> we learned that philosophy is the cleanser of passions.

And through the definition based on the **remote end** <note>The "remote end" refers to the ultimate or final goal.</note>, which says: "Philosophy is the likeness to God according to the power <note>or capacity</note> of humans," we learned that philosophy is for the sake of ultimate things. For the political philosopher desires both right knowledge and good deeds, as much as it is possible for a human to resemble God.

And if it is necessary to speak briefly: philosophy consists of two types—the theoretical and the practical—and of six definitions. Some of these refer to theoretical philosophy, some to the practical, and some to both.

For the two definitions based on the **subject matter**—the one saying "Philosophy is the knowledge of beings as they are beings," and the one saying "Philosophy is the knowledge of divine and human things"—belong to the theoretical.

And the one based on **excellence**, which says "Philosophy is the art of arts and the science of sciences," [belongs] to the theoretical part of philosophy...

It introduces the six classic definitions of philosophy, categorizing them as theoretical, practical, or sovereign based on their subject matter and goals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of philosophy, specifically focusing on the famous "six definitions" of the Neoplatonic tradition.
