...pertain to [philosophy]. And the other [definition], which concerns the final end, states that **philosophy is the meditation on death.** <note>original: "խոկումն մահու" (khokumn mahu). This is a Platonic definition suggesting that philosophy prepares the soul for separation from the body.</note> And the one derived from etymology, which states that **philosophy is the love of wisdom**, pertains to the **practical** <term>practical: the branch of philosophy concerned with ethics, politics, and the regulation of behavior</term> part. Whereas the one concerning the distant goal, which states that **philosophy is becoming like God according to human capacity**, pertains to both [the theoretical and practical].

Now we must learn what kind of thing philosophy is. We have said that because of appearances, philosophy is spoken of figuratively. As the noble saying of **Theodorus** <note>Likely referring to a Neoplatonic commentator or a character in a Platonic dialogue</note> makes clear in his own words: "Such a good thing, O Theodore, has never appeared among men from God, nor shall it ever appear."

And it must be known that the purpose of this is for philosophy to figuratively lead and adorn human souls, transforming them from this material and "flesh-stifling" life into a life similar to the divine. It must also be understood that the **soul** <note>original: "հոգի" (hogi)</note> possesses two powers: the **cognitive** <note>relating to knowledge and understanding</note> and the **vital** <note>relating to life-force and impulses</note>. 

The cognitive powers are these:
1. **Intellect** <note>original: "միտք" (mitk)</note>
2. **Discursive reasoning** <note>original: "տրամախոհութիւն" (tramakhohut'iwn)</note>
3. **Sensation**
4. **Opinion**
5. **Imagination**

The vital powers are these:
1. **Will**
2. **Choice**
3. **Inclination**
4. **Desire**
5. **Anger**

In each of these categories, some are **rational** and some are **irrational**. Within the cognitive powers of the soul, the rational ones are intellect, discursive reasoning, and opinion; whereas the irrational ones are sensation and imagination. Likewise, within the vital powers, some are rational and some are irrational. The rational ones are called will, choice, and inclination; the irrational ones are anger and desire. 

<margin>5</margin>

Now, let these matters be so. God granted philosophy to early on adorn the human soul. It adorns the cognitive powers through the **theoretical** <term>theoretical: the branch of philosophy concerned with the nature of reality and God</term> part, and the vital powers through the **practical** part. This is so that we might gain foresight from our opinions and first do nothing evil.

Every **evil** is punishable.
No one who is punished is **incorruptible**.
No one who is corruptible is **uncreated** <note>meaning, they have a beginning/cause</note>.
Evil is corruptible; therefore, evil is not uncreated.
No one is evil by nature.

No "made thing" is a **substance** <note>original: "գոյացութիւն" (goyatsut'iwn)</note>.
Therefore, evil is not a substance. 
All opposites are the destroyers of one another.
Opposites, therefore, are not incorruptible.
Nothing uncreated is changeable.

Goodness is not uncreated.

<meta>This page transitions from the general definitions of philosophy discussed on the previous page into a specific logical and theological defense regarding the nature of evil. It addresses the ontological status of evil, arguing that it is not "uncreated" (eternal/divine), likely as part of a commentary by David the Invincible or a similar Neoplatonic-influenced Armenian philosopher.</meta>

...[It] is not **uncreated** | in the Holy Divine Books they deliver [evil ones] to torments. They say that not only other evils, but even the **Slanderer** <note>original: "բանսարկուն" (bansarkun), a traditional Armenian term for the Devil or Satan</note> himself is delivered to torments and is **altered** <note>In this philosophical context, "alteration" or "change" implies that a being is not divine, as the Divine/Uncreated is considered immutable and unchanging.</note>. No one who is uncreated is altered; therefore, nothing among evils is uncreated.

Those things which are completely opposite do not have equality; for by every necessity, that which is formed as an **inclination** <term>inclination: "միտումն" (mitumn), referring here to the ontological direction or essential tendency of a being</term> in one [nature] is not formed in the other. And while **Goodness** is formed as uncreated, evil is not formed from any of these uncreated things, but is only evil. Everything that rightfully desires something has its desire adapted [to its nature]. If one were to long for things according to their nature, then evil would belong to the **corruptible** <note>things that are subject to decay and death</note>. If it belongs to the corruptible, it corrupts and perishes; and that which is corrupted is not uncreated. 

If evil were uncreated, it would be evil according to its nature. But no one sins by acting according to their nature. Therefore, the evil one sins [by choice]. And he who sins is not beyond reproach; yet the **Satan** is under reproach. Therefore, he is not uncreated. Whoever predicates "uncreated" [to evil] truly is and becomes evil. <note>decorative flourish</note>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry from the previous page, shifting from the nature of evil to the foundational principles of logic used to define divinity and existence. # PREFACE

->On Genus and Species<-

Since, O Christopher, it is necessary <gloss>for the knowledge</gloss> of the categories <gloss>of theology</gloss> to know what is genus, what is difference, what is species, what is property, and what is accident; and for the explanation of definitions and entirely for the sake of the division and knowledge of causes; I will attempt to hand these over to you briefly by observing them through these few words, as an **Introduction** <term>Introduction: "ներածութի" (neradsuti), the Armenian title for Porphyry’s *Isagoge*, a foundational logic textbook used to prepare students for Aristotle’s philosophy</term> to compose this for your use, passing through it with discourse. I encounter these simpler inquiries moderately, while longing for the deeper things.

And care must be taken immediately regarding the naming of the genus: whether these [the five universals] are **subjects** <note>original: "ենթակայ" (enthaka), meaning things that have a real, independent existence</note> or whether they exist only as essential thoughts; and if they are substantiated as bodies or are incorporeal; and whether they are perhaps separate from things, or exist within sensible things and are substantiated within them. 

<summary>This page introduces the five basic concepts of Aristotelian logic—genus, species, difference, property, and accident—as essential tools for theological study. It poses the classic philosophical "Problem of Universals," questioning whether these categories exist in reality or only in the mind.</summary>

<keywords>logic, theology, Christopher, genus, species, universals, Isagoge, Porphyry, categories, Aristotelianism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Porphyry's "Introduction" (Isagoge), specifically focusing on the first of the five universals: Genus. It moves from the general philosophical questions of the previous page to specific definitions and examples used in the medieval classroom.</meta>

for it is a division, however. But the lineage of **Heracles** <note>The author uses Heracles as a classic example of a 'genus' meaning a family line or ancestry</note> 
<margin>from Nicomachus</margin>
descending as a genus; and those close to the philosopher **Nicomachus**. The genus was held similar to this; and it shows it as the beginning of the seed, and after this the multitude of the groups which are distinguished from Heracles, being explained by others; and we obtained the whole collection [to mean] the genus is a division there. 

And in another way, again, **genus** is said to be that under which the **species** is ranked. In this sense, it is said that perhaps these [generations] are spoken of because they are the beginning and causes of the genus according to given births, and they are the cause containing everything under them.

Behold the triple [meanings] of the genus spoken of; regarding the third meaning, it is the discourse <gloss>definition</gloss> among the philosophers, which the definitions also explained, saying: 

->**"A genus is that which is predicated of many things differing in species, in response to the question 'What is it?'"**<-

Such as **animal**. For among those things being predicated according to "what it is," some are said of one thing alone, such as **individuals** <term>Individuals: "անհատք" (anhatk), meaning something indivisible, a specific person or thing</term>, such as **Socrates**, and "this person," and so on. But others are said of many, like **genera**, **species**, **differences**, **properties**, and **accidents**, which are common.

Commonly, some [are predicated] of others specifically. This is the genus, such as **animal**; and the species, while the "what it is" remains a commonality, such as **rational** <note>In traditional logic, "rational" is the specific difference that makes a human a human within the genus of animals</note>; and the accidents, such as being tall or being **risible** <term>Risible: "ծիծաղելին" (tsitsaghelin), the unique human capacity to laugh, used as a standard example of a 'property'</term>.

<summary>This page defines the three ways the term "genus" is used: as a family lineage (like the descendants of Heracles), as the source of a birth, and as a logical category that groups different species together under a common "what is it?" question.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of Porphyry's *Isagoge*, moving deeper into the technical distinctions between the "Five Universals." Specifically, it clarifies how a "Genus" (the general category) differs from a "Species" (the sub-category), a "Difference" (the distinguishing trait), and an "Accident" (a random quality).</meta>

...bound, as in the definition of the genus. This is meant to distinguish the genus from those things predicated of only one individual and from things that are different. For while the "differences" <note>The "difference" (differentia) describes the quality that sets one species apart from another within the same genus, such as "rationality" for humans.</note> explain **of what sort** a thing is, the genus is predicated of **many species**. Even if species are also predicated of many, they do not differ by species, but only by number. 

For instance, the species "man" is predicated of **James** and **John** as species <note>The text likely refers to individuals belonging to the species.</note>. These individuals do not differ from each other in species, but only in number <note>In Aristotelian logic, two humans are the same "kind" of thing, but they are two separate "numbered" entities.</note>.

But "animal," being the **genus** of "man" and "horse" and others, is predicated of subjects that differ in species, and not just in number.

The genus also differs from the **property** <term>Property: 'յատուկ' (yatuk), a characteristic that is not part of the essence but belongs to every member of a species and only that species, like the ability to laugh.</term>, because a property is assigned to only one species and is defined by that one species alone. Examples include being **"capable of laughter"** for man alone, or **"having fingernails"** <note>original: "զկա մատունն" - likely referring to the flat nails characteristic of primates/humans in classical logic</note> for man. But the genus is predicated of all its various species, and specifically of many things that differ in kind. 

The genus also differs from the **accident** <term>Accident: 'պատահումն' (patahum), a quality that can come or go without the thing itself being destroyed, like being seated or having a certain hair color.</term>. For although accidents may be predicated of many things that differ in species, they are predicated in the sense of "what sort of thing" <note>original: "որպիսի ինչ" - corresponding to the Latin 'quale'</note> it is, or how it happens to be, rather than "what" it is.

When we look at that which is being predicated, we see that some things are more related to "of what sort" they are. For when asking "of what sort" 

<column-break/>

a man is, we say he is **rational** and **mortal**. We say these things merely as an accidental <note>The scribe here refers to the 'difference' or 'quality' as a type of predication distinct from the essential 'what'</note> "rational" predication. 

But if we must bring to light "what" a man is, and we ask the question **"What is it?"**, we answer: **"An animal."** Thus, the genus of man is "animal." 

Furthermore, the phrase "predicated of many" in the definition separates the genus from those terms that apply to only one thing, such as the "finger of a man" <note>The text uses "finger" as an example of a specific part or individual trait rather than a universal category.</note>. The phrase "differing in species" separates the genus from those things that are predicated of only one species or are the species themselves. 

And finally, the phrase **"in response to 'What is it?'"** <note>original: "զինչն" - corresponding to the Latin 'in quid', the category of essence</note> distinguishes the genus from "differences" and from "accidents." For an accident does not explain the essence <gloss>the 'what' of a thing</gloss>, but rather "of what sort" things are, or how they happen to be. 

This genus contains even those things placed under the definition of a subject. For the time being, this is the answer regarding species. 

And there is a command over each **animal**, according to which we say it is "nearest" <note>The "proximate genus" is the category immediately above the species in the hierarchy of being.</note> and worthy of priority. Regarding the species and the genus, we have become accustomed to such a composition, saying that **"man is a species of the genus animal,"** and so on.

<summary>This page defines the philosophical concept of "Genus" by distinguishing it from other logical categories like species, properties, and accidents, using the classic example of "Animal" vs "Man."</summary>
<keywords>Genus, Species, Predication, Accident, Property, Rationality, Animal, Logic, Porphyry, Isagoge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of logical categories, specifically focusing on the definition of 'Species' and its hierarchical relationship to the 'Genus' as found in Porphyry’s introduction to Aristotle's logic.</meta>

...and the capacity for species, and the manual <note>original: "ձեռանդուննիկ" — likely referring to the specific 'form' or 'shape' that defines a category</note> of the form of species. And if one reverses the species through negation, one finds that those which are many and different in species are predicated through the one. It is necessary to know the species which is maintained by the negation of the genus; for the genus provides the limit <note>or "definition"</note> for each species. <margin>each</margin> It is necessary to present for each <unclear>one</unclear> <unclear>... ...</unclear> and the definition is revealed as such:

**A species is that which is under the genus, or that of which the genus is predicated.** Or perhaps in this way: **A species is that which is predicated of many things that differ in number.** In this way, the most specific <note>original: "յատկագուննիկ" — the 'most special' or 'lowest' species</note> revelation will be that which belongs only to the species itself. Would these then not be the most specific? And the following shall be stated in a firm manner: each through its own subsistence.

There are some things that are **most generic** <note>The 'Genus Generalissimum' or the highest category like 'Substance'</note> and others that are **most specific** <note>The 'Species Specialissima' or the lowest category like 'Man'</note>, and between the most generic and the most specific, there are others. A genus is "more general" than that which is below it; this is called the **transcendent genus**. And the most specific is the **"most species-like"** <note>the 'infima species'</note>, after which there will be no other lower species. And between the most generic and the most species-like are those which

<column-break/>

signify the same things, but in relation to one another <note>meaning a category can be a species to the one above it and a genus to the one below it</note>. 

And the question of predication shall be: Is "substance" the genus, and is "body" the species of substance? Then the genus of "body" is "animate body," through which comes "animal." This "animal" contains "rational animality," through which "man" exists. And "man" is predicated of the few parts <note>referring to individual humans</note> of mankind. 

Not all genus and species are inherent to these substances, but there is a **most specific** and **most numerous** species. "Body" is a species of <term>Substance: 'գոյացութիւն' (goyats'ut'iun), the primary category of that which exists in itself.</term> and a genus of "animate body." Furthermore, "animate body" is a species of "body" and a genus of "animal." 

And in turn, "animal" is a species of "animate body" and a genus of "rational animal." And "rational animal" is a species of "animal" and a genus of "man." And "man" is a species of "rational animal." But "man" is **no longer a genus** for all humans, but only a species. And this is called the species only because it is most closely related to the first individuals <note>like Socrates or Plato</note>. 

And the genus will be <unclear>... ...</unclear> the highest of substances, before which there is no other genus, because it is the **most generic**. But "man," being a species, after which there is no other species...

from the revelation of these newly established things, which exist as self-governing **individuals** <note>original: "անհատիցն" — things that cannot be divided further, like a specific person</note>. For the individual is at the bottom and is short-lived, and is called only a **species**, and specifically the **final species** <note>The 'infima species', the lowest level of classification before reaching specific individuals</note>, being the most specific. 

However, those in the middle, which come before it, shall be species; yet relative to what follows them, they are **genera** <note>plural of genus</note>. Therefore, these middle terms possess two roles: toward those things above them, they are said to be species, but toward those things below them, they are said to be their genera.

But the **extremes** <note>the very top and the very bottom of the hierarchy</note> have only a single role. For the **most generic** <note>The 'Genus Generalissimum', the highest category such as 'Substance'</note>, by its very nature, has the role of being the supreme genus over all. Since there is nothing prior to it, it no longer has a higher genus and is a unique beginning. 

In contrast, the **most specific** <note>The lowest species, like 'Human'</note> has a single role as a species relative to the genus above it. As for itself, it has no others below it <note>except individuals</note>. Furthermore, it is called the species of individuals, as it encompasses them, and by being a <margin>species</margin> it contains them all, for they are contained by it. 

But that which is the **most generic** is not a species of anything, and stands outside

<column-break/>

whatever might be above it. And the **most specific**, being a species, is no longer called a genus, for it is not divided into further species; rather, it is unique and perfect in number. It is that which is **predicated** <note>original: "ստորոգի" — a logical term for what is stated or 'categorized' about a subject</note> of the individuals.

The middle terms between the extremes are called species and genera relative to one another. Each is placed as a species or a genus depending on whether they are taken in relation to one thing or another. 

And for those things from the most particular up to the most specific, they are called species as we ascend, and relative to each other, they are genera. This is as it was with **Achilles** and **Peleus** and **Tantalus** <note>original: "աստիղէս... պեղոմ պիղէս... տան տաղիդոս" — the scribe has adapted Greek mythological names often used in logic exercises</note>, and finally **Agamemnon**. Even in the diverse mythologies <note>original: "աղաբանութիցն" — literally 'fables' or 'discourses'</note>, they have brought down and traced back to **Aramazd** <note>original: "յարա մագղ" — the Armenian equivalent of Zeus/Jupiter</note> multiple times. 

But concerning the highest genera and species, it is not so; for there is not one single common genus for all beings. They are called <term>Homonymous</term> <note>terms with the same name but different essence</note> according to one supreme <term>genus</term>, as <term>Aristotle</term> demonstrates. Instead, in that consideration, there are the **ten principles** <note>The Ten Categories of Aristotle</note>, the **ten genera** which are the primary principles. And although everything that is seen may be called 'homonymously,' it is said to be called so and not interchangeably.

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of the Porphyrian Tree, focusing on the relationship between Genus and Species. ...in two ways. For if there were one common genus for all beings, and that were the highest of all, it is unclear if all beings could be predicated of it.
| According to the ten primary categories <note>Refers to Aristotle’s ten categories: Substance, Quantity, Quality, Relation, Place, Time, Position, State, Action, and Affection.</note>, there is an equality of names. Now, the most generic and the most specific names are a certain inclusion of others within themselves. But the genus follows the rule of being placed after the more specific names. Therefore, as we descend from the most generic to the most specific, we are commanded to stop freely and descend by means of dividing through specific differences <note>original: "տեսակարար տարբերութիւն" — the "differentia" that distinguishes one species from another within a genus</note>, leaving behind the infinite multitude. For a certain knowledge arises from these; and in descending to the most specific, it is necessary to divide it always by the multitude of particulars. And in ascending to the most generic, it is necessary to unify the multitude; for the essence of the species, and even more so the genus, gathers the individuals into one place. But the individuals and the opposites according to each person are always divided into a multitude. For by the participation in the species, 

<column-break/>

there are many men. But the individual is one, and the common is many. For the dividing principle always sets each apart, and the collecting principle creates a classroom of sorts.
| Now, concerning the things revealed about the genus and the species, what is the nature of the genus for the things that exist? And for the species, which are many? For the species is always a division <note>original: "կոտորումն" — literally a fragment or piece, referring to how a species is a sub-section of a genus</note> of the genus. The genus is always predicated of the species, and all higher things are predicated of the lower. However, the species is not predicated of its nearby genus, nor of the higher ones; for it does not work in reverse. For it is always proper and right to predicate the encompassing terms of the things they encompass. Just as "neighing" <note>original: "խրխճնական" — the specific difference of a horse in medieval logic</note> is predicated of a horse, or "noble" is predicated of man; but the lower is no longer predicated of the higher. For you will no longer say that "animal is man," but you will see that "man is an animal." But you shall speak of the underlying state, and it is necessary that the genus of the species be predicated, and the genus of the genus, until the most generic genus is reached. For if it is true to say that a particular man is a "man," and "man" is an "animal," and "animal" is "substance" <term>Substance (Goyac‘ut‘iun): the most basic category of being that exists in itself.</term>, then it is also true to say the particular man is an animal...

<summary>This page discusses the logical hierarchy of Genus and Species, explaining that while Genus is predicated of Species (e.g., all men are animals), the reverse is not true. It also describes the "descent" from general categories to specific individuals and the "ascent" from individuals back to a unifying genus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical exposition of Porphyry’s Isagoge, specifically the "Porphyrian Tree." Having previously discussed how a "Genus" (a broad category) is divided into "Species" (more specific categories), the text now explains how we reach the level of the "Individual"—the single, unique being that cannot be divided further. It then transitions into a definition of "Difference," which is the quality that distinguishes one thing from another.</meta>

...perception and sensation.
It is evident when, descending from the higher categories to the lower ones, you subordinate the lowest species to the **individual**, while you move upward from the individual to the species and then to the genus. And those which are generic, or belong to the genus, are the intermediate levels between the most general and the most specific. And by means of these, both the species and the individual are defined. For the genus is the "most generic" of all, following the sequence of genera, species, and individuals. Thus, the genus exists prior to the more specific levels, encompassing all specific differences and individuals. Only the species encompasses all individuals, and the individual is predicated of only one thing <note>In logic, "predicated of" means a quality or name that is stated about a subject. For example, in "Socrates is a man," "man" is predicated of Socrates.</note> which bears that name uniquely and individually in its form—for example, "white" <note>original: "սպիտակ" — a common example in logic for an "accident" or quality that can change without changing the essence of the thing.</note>. And the accident that comes to us is called the "indwelling spirit," such as the spirit of **Socrates** alone. Now, such means are described because an individual is composed of unique properties; the collection of qualities in one person will not be found in any other. For the most specific properties of species will no longer be found together in the mind in the same way. But concerning "Man," I say this: the same specific property will exist across many individuals, even if he is more...

<column-break/>

...to all and to each single one. But when those things enter the mind, the individual, the species, and the genus are held together. For the name of the "whole" is similar to the species; the species is a part of the whole, but also a part of others <note>A species like "Man" is a "whole" encompassing all individual humans, but it is also a "part" of the larger genus "Animal."</note>, while the "whole" itself is not of others, but consists of others. For the parts are the "whole" of the others in terms of genus and species—what is the most generic, what is the most specific, and what <margin>the species</margin> are. In a small way, by that same individual and by the definition, the species preserves both the genus and the individual.

-> ### Concerning Difference <-

**Difference** <note>original: "տարբերութիւն" (tarberut'yun) — the Greek 'diaphora'. This refers to the characteristic that distinguishes one species from another within the same genus, like 'rationality' distinguishing humans from other animals.</note> is spoken of in a "common" sense and in a "proper" sense. In a **common** sense, one thing is said to differ from another by a change or "otherness," whether in the disposition of the soul, or in some relation, or in something else that is subordinated through a change of place. It is also categorized by otherness in the natural states of childhood and maturity, or in action, or in rest. These always involve a change in one's own state. But a thing is said to differ **properly** from another <margin>un-</margin> when it occurs through an inseparable accident <note>An "inseparable accident" is a feature that stays with a person for life, like a scar or the color of their eyes, even though it isn't part of their essential definition as a human.</note> or an alteration. Examples of such inseparable accidents are the "keenness" of the hawk's eye, or the "deadness" <note>possibly referring to a permanent state or a specific physical trait</note> in a person. It is in this proper sense that they are said to differ...

<summary>This page explains the relationship between the "most generic" categories and the "individual" (using Socrates as an example), then begins defining the concept of "Difference" (Differentia), distinguishing between common changes and permanent "inseparable accidents."</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, logic, Genus, Species, Individual, Socrates, Difference, Accident, Aristotle, Categories</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical exploration of Porphyry’s Isagoge. Following the discussion of how individuals are formed by a unique collection of properties, the text now transitions into a formal definition of "Difference" (differentia). It distinguishes between differences that create a new species and those that merely describe a temporary state or accidental quality of an object.</meta>

### On Difference

When an irrational creature, through **mental reflection**,
is distinguished—just as man, as a species, is
<margin>different</margin> distinguished from irrational animals by a **difference**
in quality. And it is written that in 
these cases, all the lower levels of being <note>The text uses a phrase that suggests "subordinate natures" or "hypostases."</note> 
are taken to have a common 
signification of "alteration." 
But specific properties are another matter. For among **differences**, 
there are some that make a thing "altered" and some that make it "another." 
Those that make it "another" are called 
**species-making** <note>original: "տեսակ ար արք" — a translation of the Greek *eidopoios*, referring to differences that define a new species, like "rationality" defining "man."</note>. 
But those that merely "alter" it are simply 
called **differences**. For the 
difference of "animal" is said to be 
"rational"; this makes the species "man" 
something "other" than "animal." 
However, "moving" creates only an 
**alteration** in relation to "slowness." 
One [rationality] makes it a different kind, 
while the other [movement] is only an 
alteration. Now, from these **alterations**, 
divisions arise from the **genera** into 
the **rational** parts. 
And the definitions are explained by 
the genera through such **differences**. 
But that which only "alters" results 
only in an **alteration**, such as 
changes in posture or form. Now, 
beginning again from the top, we must 
speak of **differences**. 
Some are **separable**, 
and some are **inseparable**. For—

<column-break/>

<page-num>77</page-num>

—moving, walking, and 
the like, as well as falling ill or 
recovering, are **separable accidents** 
of the subjects. But 
being [in a state] is an accident. 
<margin>the movement of the will</margin>
These are the separable ones. And there is a 
genus of separable things. 
There are those that are **essential** <note>original: "ինքեան" (in themselves) — corresponding to the Latin *per se*.</note> and those that 
are **by accident**. 
For "rational" is essential 
to man, as are "mortal" and 
being capable of "wisdom." 
But being **snub-nosed** <note>original: "ծոռնելն" — a classic Aristotelian example used to describe a physical trait that is inseparable from a specific person but not part of the definition of 'human'.</note> or 
**flat-nosed** is by accident 
to the same individual. 
Now, those which are essential are 
taken into the **substance** <note>original: "գոյացութիւն" — the core essence or "being" of a thing.</note> 
and the definitions. 
But those by accident are not 
taken into the substance, nor 
do they make a thing "another," but rather 
"altered." And those which are essential 
do not admit of "more" or "less." 
But those by accident, even if they 
are **inseparable**, admit of 
**intensity** and **diminishment**. 
For a **genus** is not 
predicated "more" or "less" of 
its members, 
nor is the difference of a genus, 
by which it was divided and determined. 
For each one [of these differences] 
completes the definition of the being. 
For the "being" of each 
thing is one and the same; 
it does not admit of intensity 
or diminishment. 
But being snub-nosed, or having a 
flat nose, or other...

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Porphyry’s Isagoge, specifically focusing on the role of "Difference" (differentia) in logic. It explores how specific qualities—like being rational or mortal—serve to both divide a broad "genus" into smaller parts and "constitute" (build up) the definition of a specific "species."</meta>

That example and the response brought to it, and then if he brings [it] forth: among the long types of differences belonging to the **genera**, some are **separable**, while some are **separating** and externally unlimited in themselves, and some are according to **accidents** <note>original: "ըստ պատահմանն" — referring to non-essential qualities that can change, like being seated or having a tan.</note>.

But if we look at the differences in themselves: some are those by which the **genus** is divided into opposites, and some are those by which the things divided become **species** of the world. In the essential difference of all things, there is the **animal** <note>original: "կենդանի" — used here in the broad sense of any "living being" or "organism."</note> which has become **animate**, **sensitive**, **rational**, **irrational**, **mortal**, and **immortal**. 

For the difference of being "animate" and "sensitive" is **substantial** <note>original: "թակայականն" — relating to the underlying essence or "hypostasis" of the being.</note> for the substance of the animal. Because an animal is a substance that is animate and sensitive. However, the distinction between **mortal** and **immortal**, and between **rational** and **irrational**, are the **dividing** factors of the differences of animal; for through these, we divide the **genus** and the **species**. These are the other **dividing** differences. 

For the **genera**, they are **connective**, and for the **species**, they are **constitutive** <note>original: "բաղկացականք" — from the root for "to compose." These are the differences that "make up" the definition of a species, such as "rationality" constituting "man."</note>. Thus, the "animal" is completed by the differences of "rational" and "immortal." Likewise, "mortal" and "immortal" are differences in themselves, but the differences of "rational" and "mortal" are **constitutive** of **man**. And the [differences] of rational and immortal are another kind [than] irrational...

<column-break/>

...the same foundation of the individual and the animal. In this way, the higher **substance** is also divided by the difference of "animate," and likewise "sensitive" and "insensitive." "Animate" and "sensitive," joined according to substance, complete the **animal**. 

Now, since these same things are judged as differences, how are they called **constitutive** or how are they called **divisive** in the formation of species? These are even more necessary for the division of genera and for the definitions of **separable accidents**, no matter how much they separate. 

This is what they define as **Difference**: that by which the **species** exceeds the **genus**. For **man** exceeds **animal** by the addition of **rationality** and **mortality**. For the "animal" itself is not any one of these [on its own]. Otherwise,
<margin>it joins</margin>
the species are formed by these distinctions. Not every kind of knowledge of the destructive will form the opposite. But as it is worthy to perform by power <note>original: "զաւրութբ" — likely referring to potentiality (*dynamis*).</note> all the distinctions in itself, yet in **actuality** <note>original: "ներգործութբ" — referring to the Greek *energeia*, or the realized state of a thing.</note> it exists as one uniquely and not from anything else. Nor are the opposites made substantial. And they define it, and distinguish the many and those things that differ by species, according to how the **predicate** <note>original: "ստորոգեալն" — the logical term for what is stated or "predicated" about a subject.</note> is situated within.

<margin>2</margin>
2. Since "rational" and "mortal" have constituted **man**. In what way for man? And for others, what [is it]? For what will those who are asked about other **animals** <note>original: "կենդանի" — used here to mean any living being with a soul, whether human or beast.</note> say, if we distinguish them by the familiar terms of "rational" and "mortal"?

<margin>J</margin> **F**rom things composed of **matter** and **form** <note>original: "ի նիւթոյ և ի տեսակէ" — this refers to the Aristotelian theory of hylomorphism, where everything is a combination of underlying stuff (matter) and a defining shape or essence (form).</note>, or from those having a composition from a specific form and matter. Just as a **bronze statue** is made from the matter of bronze and the form of the shape. In the same way, "**man**"—both the general concept and the specific individual—was composed proportionately of the **genus** and the incoming **difference**. But for the whole, the man is called "**animal, rational, mortal**," just as the bronze statue is called [a statue] there. 

But if one divides such differences in this way: the differences which explain are familiar to the same genus. For "rational" and "irrational" distinguish man and horse, both of which are under the same genus of "**animal**." And they compose it thus: by whatever differences each one differs, for according to the genus, nothing was distinguished. Because we and the irrational beasts are all **animals**; but "rational," being added, distinguished us from them. And by rationality, or [for] the horse, yet "mortal," being added, distinguished

<column-break/>

us from them. And this, by putting forward the matter concerning **differences**—the one that exists for them and those that distinguish under the same genus—but another difference which is composed of the **soul**; these are called parts of the **substance** <note>original: "գոյացութիւնն" — equivalent to the Greek 'ousia', referring to the core essence of what a thing is.</note>. 

For if it is natural to **sail** <note>original: "նաւել" — a standard example in Armenian logic texts of an acquired skill used as a non-essential difference.</note> as a difference of man, even if it is specific to man. For we shall say of animals that some are more naturally disposed and some are not distinguished from others, but the natural disposition to sail does not establish a connective **substance**. Nor is it a part of it, but only [a part] of mortality, because it does not exist as an internal **species**, but rather as what are called external **differences**. These are not the differences that create species, which make another species, and which in their turn exist in the first things. 

But now, concerning the **difference**, this much is enough.

->Concerning the Property<-

The **Property** <note>original: "Յատուկն" — in Greek logic, the 'idion'. This is a quality that belongs only to a specific species, but is not part of its core definition.</note> is divided into four kinds. For there is that which happens to only one **species**, though not to every [individual member], such as for a man to **heal** <note>to be a doctor</note> or to **practice geometry**. And there is that which happens to every [individual] of the species, though not to that species alone, such as for a man to be **two-footed**. And there is that which belongs to only one [species] and to every [individual member], and at all times, such as for a man, [the capacity for] every activity...

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of the five "predicables" (genus, species, difference, property, and accident) established by Porphyry. The previous page concluded the discussion on "Difference" and began the transition into "Property."</meta>

...but also what the fourth kind [of property] is, when it comes together and is gathered in one place; belonging both to one [species] alone and to every [individual of that species], such as the "capacity for laughter" <note>original: "ծիծաղականն" — a classic example in ancient logic: only humans can laugh, and all humans have the potential to laugh.</note> in **man**. For even if he is not laughing at the moment, he is called "laughable," not because he is actually laughing, but because he is naturally disposed to it; it is a quality that always dwells within him. The same applies to "neighing" for a **horse**. These are called "properties" <note>original: "յատուկք"</note> in the strictest sense because they are also **convertible**; for if it is a horse, it neighs, and if it neighs, it is a horse.

An **Accident** <note>original: "պատահականութիւն" — a quality that is not essential to a thing's nature, like being tall or sitting down.</note> is defined as that which comes and goes without the destruction of the **subject** <note>original: "ենթակայ" — the underlying being or thing to which qualities belong.</note>. It is divided into two types: those that are **separable** and those that are **inseparable**. For example, "sleep" is a separable accident. But "blackness" is an inseparable accident, as it occurs in an **Ethiopian**. Yet even so, one can conceive in the mind a "white" Ethiopian, mentally removing the color without the destruction of the subject <note>The author argues that while you can't actually wash the color off, you can imagine the person without the color and they are still a "person," whereas you cannot imagine a person without "reason" and still have them be a "man."</note>. And they define an accident this way: that which can be present or absent in the same [subject] while being neither a **genus**, nor a **difference**, nor a **species**, nor a **property**; yet it is always present in the subject. Having now distinguished and outlined all these—difference, species, property, and accident—we must discuss what they have in common and what is unique to each.

<column-break/>

**On things in common:** What is common to them?
**Living beings** <note>original: "կենդանի" — often translated as 'animal' in this logical context.</note> and **species** both have the role of encompassing others. It is common to all of them to be predicated <note>To "predicate" means to state or assert something about a subject (e.g., in "Socrates is a man," 'man' is the predicate).</note> of more than one thing. However, the **genus** is predicated of species and individuals, and the **difference** is the same. But the **species** is predicated only of individuals. The **property** is predicated of the species it belongs to and of the individuals [within that species]. And **accidents** are predicated of both species and individuals. For "animal" is predicated of both the "horse" and "man" as species, as well as of specific individuals. But the species "man" is predicated only of individuals. The **property**, such as "the capacity for laughter," is predicated of both the species "man" and specific individuals. But "black" and "white" are accidents that are either inseparable or separable, being predicated of individuals. Thus, [the genus and difference] encompass individuals generally, but they encompass an infinite number of secondary things.

### On what is common to Genus and Difference

It is common to both the **genus** and the **difference** to encompass species. For the genus encompasses species, and the difference also encompasses the species, though not all of them in the same way as the genus. For example, "rational" [as a difference]...

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis based on Porphyry's *Isagoge*, a standard textbook on logic in medieval Armenia. It transitions from discussing specific properties and accidents to a comparative analysis of the "Genus" and the "Difference."</meta>

Although [the genus] contains the same things—the **irrational animal** as much as the [living] **animal**—it also encompasses God <note>original: "զած" (զաստուած). In ancient and medieval logic, "God" was often categorized as a rational, immortal being for the sake of defining the hierarchy of existence.</note> and the **human species**. It is necessary, therefore, that whatever things are predicated of the **genus** <term>genus: a general category or "family" of things (e.g., 'Animal')</term> as a genus, those same things are also predicated of the **species** <term>species: a specific sub-type within a genus (e.g., 'Human')</term> through it. And whatever is predicated of the **difference** <term>difference: the specific trait that separates one species from another, such as 'rationality'</term> as a difference, those same things are also predicated of the species through it.

For the genus "**animal**" predicates "**substance**" and "**animate**" <note>original: "շնչաւորն" — literally 'breathing' or 'having a soul,' distinguishing living things from inanimate objects.</note> as a genus; and these predicate all the species of animals down to the individual. The difference was "**rational**"; it is predicated as a difference, and the species of the rational are practiced <note>original: "վարժիլ" — here referring to the exercise or manifestation of reason through speech.</note> by speech. But it is common to both those that are removed—whether the genus or the difference—that their consequences are also removed. If the genus is removed, so that there is no "animal," then there is no "man" and no "horse"; likewise, if "rational" is removed, nothing is exercised as an animal through reason.

### Concerning the properties shared by genus and difference:

It is a property of the **genus** to be predicated over more things than the **difference**, the **species**, the **accident**, or the **property**. For "**animal**" encompasses man, horse, dog, and snake; but "**four-footed**" <note>original: "չորս ոտս ունին"</note> is predicated only over those that have four feet. And "**man**" is predicated only over individuals,

<column-break/>

and the capacity for learning. But [the property of] "**neighing**" belongs to the horse alone and its descendants. The **accident** likewise applies to fewer things.

And we must consider the differences by which [things] are completed: not the encompassing ones, but the **genus of substance**. Its name encompasses the difference potentially; for "**animal**" is a genus under which there is the "**rational**" and there is the "**irrational**." This genus is prior to the difference, for the genus is not removed by the removal of the specific differences, nor is it removed by others; for if "animal" is removed, "rational" and "irrational" are removed, but the differences do not likewise remove the genus. For even if all [rational] things were removed, the existence of the **animate** would still be understood, which is "animal."

The **genus** tells "**what**" a thing is <note>original: "զինչն" — the essence or 'quid' in Latin logic.</note>, while the **difference** tells "**what kind**" <note>original: "որպիսի ինչն" — the quality or 'quale' in Latin logic.</note> of thing it is when it is acquired. Each species has only one genus—such as "animal" for man—but there are many differences, such as "rational," "mortal," and "capable of mind and knowledge," by which it differs from others. The genus is like **matter** <note>original: "նիւթոյ"</note>, and the difference is like the internal form. Regarding the commonalities and properties of genus and difference, this is sufficient. Having taken up the essence [of the topic] as an introductory adverb regarding appearances. <summary>This page analyzes the logical relationship between a "genus" (a broad category like 'Animal') and a "difference" (a defining characteristic like 'Rational'). It explains that the genus is broader and more fundamental, acting like the "matter" of a definition, while the difference provides the specific "form."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of Porphyry's *Isagoge*, moving from the relationship between Genus and Difference to the comparisons between Genus, Species, Properties, and Accidents. The Armenian text uses the technical vocabulary of the "Philhellene school," which translated Greek philosophical concepts into a highly structured Armenian terminology during the 5th-7th centuries.</meta>

<margin>Concerning</margin>
# Concerning the Commonalities of Genus and Species

<margin>how a species is</margin>
**Genus** <term>genus: a general category, like "Animal"</term> and **species** <term>species: a specific sub-category, like "Human"</term> have in common that they are both said to be **predicated** <term>predicated: to be stated or affirmed of a subject</term> of many things. Now, it has been said that the species is also the definition of others. Nevertheless, the species and the genus are the same in that they are both prior to the things of which they are predicated, and each of them is a kind of whole.

# Concerning the Differences between Genus and Species

They differ in that the **genus** contains the species, and similarly, the genera encompass the species. For the genus is more [extensive] than the species. Genera are prior to the subjects <note>original: "ենթակայացն" — the underlying things or individuals</note> and must underlie the **specific difference** <term>difference: the trait that distinguishes a species, such as "rationality" for humans</term> to complete the species. Hence, they are prior by nature. Among these, the genus is also prior in the order of arrangement. If the species is removed, the genus remains; but if the genus is removed, the species is not maintained in all respects. Furthermore, genera are predicated of species **synonymously** <note>original: "փոխանունաբար" — meaning the name and definition of the genus apply to the species</note>, but species are not predicated of the genus. The genera contain the species by their comprehension, while the species contain the genus through the addition of their own specific difference. Neither can a species become "more generic," nor a genus "more specific."

# Concerning the Commonalities of Genus and Property

It is common to both **genus** and **property** <term>property (proprium): a quality that belongs only to one species, like "the ability to laugh" for humans</term> to follow the species. For if something is a "human," it is an "animal" <note>the genus</note>; and if it is a "human," it is "capable of laughter" <note>the property</note>. And the genus is predicated of the species, just as the property is predicated of those who possess it.

<column-break/>

For "animal" constitutes both the pair and the human; and "capable of laughter" constitutes the individuals and the unmixed. It is common for the genus to be predicated of its species by its prior name, and the property of those things to which it is specific.

# Concerning the Distinctions between Genus and Property

They differ in that the genus is prior and the property is posterior. For it is necessary first to be an "animal," and then to be divided by difference and property. Also, genera are predicated of many species, while a property is predicated of the one species to which it is specific. A property is **reciprocally predicated** <note>original: "հակասատորոգի" — meaning if it is a human, it can laugh; and if it can laugh, it is a human</note> of that which it is a property; but the genus is not reciprocally predicated of anything. For it is not the case that "whatever is an animal is a human," nor "whatever is an animal is capable of laughter." By insight, the property belongs to the species of which it is the property—to it alone, and always. But the genus belongs to every species of which it is the genus—and always—but not to only one. Furthermore, if properties are removed from things, they do not destroy the genus; but if the genus is removed, it destroys the species to which the properties belong. If those species are removed, the properties are also destroyed.

It is common to the **genus** and the **accident** <term>accident: a non-essential quality that can come or go, like "sitting" or "being tan"</term> to be predicated of many, <term>as</term> <gloss>as</gloss> it was said. This applies both to individuals and to indefinite things. And since they move through many things, one sees the accident in all [kinds of] accidents, and some of them exist always.

# Concerning the Difference between Genus and Accident

<vocab>genus, species, property, accident, predicate, difference, animal, capable of laughter, man</vocab>

<summary>This page compares the five "predicables" of logic (genus, species, difference, property, and accident), explaining how categories like "Animal" relate to "Human" and unique traits like "the ability to laugh." It establishes the hierarchical rules of definition, such as why removing a general category destroys the specific one, but not vice versa.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical breakdown of Porphyry's *Isagoge*, focusing on the comparison between "Properties" and "Accidents" and concluding with a mathematical summary of the relationships between all five predicables (Genus, Difference, Species, Property, and Accident).</meta>

# Concerning Properties and Accidents
<margin>Concerning Properties and Accidents</margin>
Now, if we consider that **genus** is prior to **accidents** <term>accident: a non-essential quality, like "sitting" or being "brown," which can change without the thing ceasing to be what it is</term> just as it is prior to the **species**, we see that the accident is posterior even to the **difference**. For even if you speak of an **inseparable accident** <note>original: "անզատելի պատահումն" — an accident that always stays with the subject, like blackness on a crow, even though it isn't part of the crow's definition</note>, the distinction of things is prior to the accident that happens to them. 

Furthermore, those things under a genus receive its name **synonymously** <note>original: "զուգընդունողքն" — meaning both the name "Animal" and the definition apply to "Human"</note>, but this is not the case with accidents. In accidents, there is **intensity and remission** <note>original: "սաստկութի և թուլութի" — meaning a quality can be "more" or "less," like one thing being whiter than another</note>, but a genus does not admit of being "more" or "less." 

Accidents exist primarily in **individuals** <term>individuals: specific, single things like "this dog" or "Socrates"</term> as their subjects, but genera and species are **prior by nature** to individual substances. Genera are predicated <term>predicated: stated or affirmed</term> of subjects in answer to the question **"What is it?"**, while accidents are predicated in answer to the question **"What kind of thing is it?"** or **"In what state is it?"** 

For if you are asked **"What kind of thing"** is a crow, you would answer **"Black"**; and if asked **"In what state"** is Socrates, you would say he **"Sits"** or **"Walks."**

Now, the genus has been described in how it differs from the other four <note>The other four are: Difference, Species, Property, and Accident</note>. And each of the others also differs from the remaining four. If we multiply the five by four, there would seem to be twenty differences in total; but if we remove the duplicates, the actual number of unique comparisons is fewer.

<column-break/>

After the four comparisons for Genus, there are three for Difference, two for Species, and one for Property. All the unique comparisons of difference total **ten**: four, then three, then two, then one. <note>This is a logical calculation: Genus vs. the other 4; then Difference vs. the remaining 3; then Species vs. the remaining 2; and finally Property vs. Accident (1). 4+3+2+1 = 10 unique pairs.</note>

For it was said that **Genus** is distinguished from **Difference**, **Species**, **Property**, and **Accident** (which are four). 

Then, **Difference** was distinguished from **Genus** when we discussed how the genus differs from it; and it remains to be discussed how **Difference** differs from the remaining three: **Species**, **Property**, and **Accident**. 

Likewise, **Species** was discussed in how it differs from **Difference** and **Genus**; it remains to be shown how it differs from the remaining two: **Property** and **Accident**. 

And finally, it follows that we must discuss how the **Property** differs from the **Accident**. Since its differences from **Species**, **Difference**, and **Genus** were already covered in the previous comparisons, it takes its final unique distinction here. 

Thus, there are:
*   Four distinctions for Genus.
*   Three for Difference.
*   Two for Species.
*   One for Property relative to Accident.

In total, there are **ten** relationships which constitute the order we have established, beginning with the genus as we said at the start.

<summary>This page concludes Porphyry's comparison of logical categories, specifically distinguishing "Accidents" (changeable qualities) from "Genus" (essential categories) and providing a mathematical breakdown of the ten unique relationships between the five predicables.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Isagoge, Accident, Property, Predicables, Logic, Socrates, Armenian Philhellene School</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical analysis of Porphyry’s *Isagoge*, specifically moving into the detailed comparison of the "Five Predicables." Having previously discussed Genus and Species, the text now focuses on "Difference" (Differentia), comparing it sequentially to "Property" (Proprium) and "Accident" (Accidens).</meta>

### On the Five [Points of Comparison] Regarding Difference

Now, a **common difference** <term>difference: the specific characteristic that distinguishes one species from another within a genus, like "rationality" distinguishes humans from other animals</term> is understood as being shared. For instance, humans share common traits, yet 
<margin>Individuals</margin> are distinguished from one another by the "rational" difference. But "common" and "separated" are used in regards to those who receive these traits; for the "rational" is always limited, and the "human" is limited and separated.

### On the Specific Characteristics of Difference

Difference is "specific" <note>original: "Յատուկ" — here referring to the technical 'specific difference' that creates a species</note> in the way it is predicated <term>predicated: stated or affirmed of a subject</term> to be what a thing is. For if "human" is taken as a certain quality, it does not necessarily follow that all qualities in the genus in which they are taken will constitute it. Furthermore, the "difference" that has occurred is seen in many species. For example, "four-footed" is seen as a characteristic of many animals. However, the species is only one of those which are specific. And other differences are prior to the species. For the "rational" <note>the difference</note> when added constitutes the "human" <note>the species</note>. But if the "human" is removed, it does not necessarily remove the "rational" from existence <note>This is a standard logical argument: the concept of "rationality" can exist even if a specific human does not</note>. And a difference is joined with another difference; for "rational" and "mortal" are **constitutive** <term>constitutive: the essential parts that "make up" the definition of a subject</term> of the human subject. But a species is not joined to a species to beget another species; for one and the same thing is produced, and the naming of the result is a specific occurrence.

<column-break/>

But even from matter, it is not further joined and completed in this same way.

### On the Five [Points of Comparison] Between Difference and Property

Difference and **Property** <term>property: a quality that belongs only to one species, like "capable of laughter" for humans, but is not part of its essential definition</term> are common in that they both are shared by the subjects. For just as those who are rational are rational, those capable of laughter are capable of laughter. This is common to both in every instance. For if "two-footed" is removed, it always results in a different description. For "capable of laughter" is a natural state to possess, even if one is not laughing at all times.

### On the Specifics of Difference and Property

A "specific difference" is often said to apply to many species, such as "rational" being applied to humans <note>In ancient logic, "rational" could also apply to God or stars, whereas "capable of laughter" was unique only to humans</note>. But the **property** belongs to only one species. Furthermore, the difference follows those things of which it is a difference, but not vice versa. However, the property is **convertible** <note>original: "հակաստորոգի" — a logical term meaning if a thing is human, it can laugh; and if it can laugh, it is human</note> and is specific to those things because they are reciprocally predicated.

### On the Five [Points of Comparison] Between Difference and Accident

Difference and **Accident** <term>accident: a non-essential quality, like being "tall" or "sitting," which can change without the thing ceasing to be what it is</term> are common in that they are both predicated of many things. In terms of separation, accidents are always similar to fleeting things. For "two-footed" always acts upon and characterizes the species, whereas the accident does not.

### On the Specifics of Difference and Accident

They differ because the "difference" contains...

-> On the Properties of Species and Property <-

Species and **Property** <term>property: a characteristic that belongs to only one species and all members of that species, but does not define its essence—like "the ability to laugh" for humans</term> have in common that they are reciprocally predicated <term>predicated: stated or affirmed of a subject</term> of one another. For if there is a **human**, then there is the **risible**; and if there is the **risible**, then there is a **human**. It has been stated many times that the "risible" refers to the natural disposition to laugh. The species is equal to the properties of those things to which they belong.

---

-> Concerning the Distinctions between Species and Property <-

Species differs from Property because a species can be under other genera <note>referring to how a species like "human" sits under the genus "animal"</note>, whereas it is impossible for a property to belong to other species. Also, the species exists prior to the property. But the pro-

<column-break/>

-perty is a subsequent reduction <note>meaning it is a secondary attribute that follows from the essence</note>. For a human might happen not to be laughing at a given time. The species is always joined to the subject in **actuality** <term>actuality: the state of currently being something in fact</term>; but the property is sometimes present only in **potentiality** <term>potentiality: having the inherent capacity for a trait even if it isn't currently visible</term>. For a human is always Socrates in actuality, but he is not always laughing, even though he is naturally always "risible" <note>original: "ծիծաղական" (tsitsaghakan) — the standard logical term for the human capacity for laughter</note>. Furthermore, things whose definitions are distinct are themselves distinct. Species are predicated according to their essence, entering into the count of differences, whereas properties are predicated only as belonging to one [species] and not to others.

---

-> On the Commonalities of Species and Accident <-

Species and Accident have in common that both are predicated of many things. However, it is very rare for other commonalities to exist because an accident is something that exists in isolation from the subject it happens to be in. Regarding the specificities of Species and Accident:

It is characteristic of each species to be predicated in the "**what it is**" <term>what it is: the essential definition or "quiddity" of a thing</term> of those things of which it is the species. But an accident is predicated in the "**what kind**" or "**quality**." Each substance is formed by a single species, but accidents are many and individual, and even personal to the specific figure. And species are understood prior to accidents, even if they are individual [accidents]; for the subject must first exist so that something else...

We shall now speak regarding **Property** and **Accident**, since Property has already been distinguished from Species, Difference, and Genus.

<margin>On the similarities between Property and Accident.</margin>
Common to both **Property** <term>property: a characteristic unique to one species, like 'the ability to laugh' for humans</term> and inseparable Accident is that without them, the subjects in which they are seen cannot exist. Just as a human cannot be a subject without the capacity for laughter, an Ethiopian cannot be a subject without blackness. Thus, just as every Property is an immediate attachment, so too is the inseparable accident.

---

<margin>On the differences between Property and Accident.</margin>
But they also differ from each other. Property is attached to only one species, just as the capacity for laughter is only for humans. But an inseparable accident, such as blackness, belongs not only to the Ethiopian but also to the crow, the eagle <note>original: 'արծուոյ'—though typically logic texts use 'raven' or 'ebony' as examples, the scribe here includes the eagle/vulture as an example of dark color</note> in color, to ebony, and to certain other things. Because of this, Property is convertible <term>convertible: a logical term meaning the subject and the trait are interchangeable; e.g., if it is human, it can laugh, and if it can laugh, it is human</term> because it is both a property and is equal to the subject. The inseparable accident, however, is not convertible. Furthermore, for Properties,

<column-break/>

there is no lack of equality, but for accidents, there is "more" and "less" <note>Meaning an accident can vary in degree—one thing can be 'blacker' than another—whereas a property like 'the capacity for laughter' is considered a constant</note>. There are other commonalities and specific characteristics of these terms, but these are sufficient for their explanation and for the demonstration of their shared nature.

# The Categories of Aristotle
## CONCERNING HOMONYMS, WHICH
### SHARE THE NAME ONLY

<margin>
common-
definition
and the portrait
</margin>

**H**omonyms <note>In Aristotelian logic, "homonyms" (equivocals) are things that share a name but have different definitions, like a real person and a painting of a person.</note> are those things where only the name is common, but the definition of the substance is different and distinct <gloss>their</gloss>; for example, both a human being and a portrait <note>original: "գրեալն" (drealn). This refers to a "written" or "drawn" man, i.e., a picture.</note> are called "man." For in these cases, only the name is common, while the definition of the substance according to that name is different. For if one were to explain what it means for each of them to be a "living being," a unique and specific definition would be provided for each.

---

<insert>+</insert> Synonyms <note>Also called "univocals." These are things that share both a name and a fundamental nature.</note> are those things where the name is common and the definition of the substance according to the name is the same; for instance, both a human and an ox. For both a human and an ox are called by the common name "living being," <note>original: "կենդանի" (kendani). In this context, it refers to the genus of "animal" or "animate creature."</note> and the definition of their substance is the same. For if one were to explain the definition <gloss>their</gloss> of what it means for each of them to be a living being, the same definition would be provided for both.

<meta>This page continues the translation of Aristotle's *Categories*, specifically concluding the definition of paronyms (derivatives) and moving into the fundamental distinction between combined and uncombined speech, as well as the relationship between linguistic sounds and mental concepts.</meta>

...differ from some member [of the word] by inflection <note>The Armenian "հոլով" (holov) translates the Greek "ptosis" (case/inflection), referring to how a word's ending changes to create a derivative.</note> but possess the fullness of the name's meaning. For example: from **grammar** comes **grammarian**, and from **resurrection** <note>original: "յարութենէն" (yarut'enen)</note> comes **the risen**.

---

Of those things which are said: some are said according to **combination** <note>original: "շարամանութիւն" (sharamanut'iun). This refers to syntax or phrases, like "the man runs."</note> and some are said without combination. Now, according to combination are such as: "a man runs," or "men conquer." But without combination are such as: "man," "ox," "runs," "conquers."

Since speech <note>original: "բան" (ban), meaning word, speech, or reason.</note> is of two types: that which is in the mind <note>original: "խորհրդումն" (khorhrdumn), referring to internal mental discourse.</note>, which has its own expression, and the sound resulting from that expression, which is a significant voice. That which is in the mind is the comprehension <note>original: "իմացումն" (imats'um)</note> of the purpose of things. It should be known that the sounds and the comprehensions do not always follow one another in the same way. Sometimes it happens that the sounds are **simple**, but the comprehensions are **composite**; such as "I run" or "I read" <note>These are single words in Armenian/Greek but imply a subject and an action.</note>. At other times, the sounds are composite while the comprehensions are simple; as when one says "a mortal, rational, living being" to define a **man**. And there are times when both the sounds and the comprehensions are simple; such as "**Socrates**" or "**Homer**." And there are times when both the sounds and the comprehensions are composite; such as "**Socrates philosophizes**" or "**Homer composes poetry**."

However, there is that which is said **of a subject** but is not **in a subject**; for instance, "man" is said of a subject—that is, of a certain individual man—but is not **in**

<column-break/>

a subject. <note>In Aristotelian logic, "man" is a universal; it describes what a person is, but it isn't an "accident" like a color that exists inside them.</note> There is also that which is **in a subject**, but is not said **of any subject**.

By "in a subject," I mean that which exists in something, not as a part of it, but which cannot exist separately from that in which it is. For example: a certain **grammatical knowledge** is in a subject—the soul—and as a particular thing it is in a subject, but it is not said **of any subject**. <note>Meaning, you cannot use "this specific bit of knowledge" as a general category to describe other things.</note> Also, "this particular white" is in a subject—the body—for all color is in a body.

But it is not said **of a subject**. Then there is that which is both said **of a subject** and is **in a subject**; for example, **knowledge** is in a subject—namely, the soul—and is also said of a subject, namely **grammar**. <note>Knowledge is a general category (said of grammar) and also exists within the person (in a subject).</note> Finally, there is that which is neither **in a subject** nor said **of a subject**; such as "a certain man" or "a certain horse." For nothing of this sort is in a subject, nor is it said of a subject. In short, things that are individual and numerically one are never said **of a subject**. However, nothing prevents some of them from being **in a subject**; for a certain "grammatical knowledge" is one of those things that exists in a subject.

**Substance** <note>original: "գոյացութի" (goyats'ut'i). Here referring to primary substance or individual things.</note> is neither in a subject nor said of a subject. But an **accident** <note>original: "պատահումն" (patahuman). A property that "happens" to a thing, like its color or weight.</note> must by necessity be in a subject, for it cannot exist without a subject.

Whenever one thing is predicated of another as of a subject, then whatever is said of the predicate will also be said of the subject. For example: "man" is predicated of a certain man, and "living being" <note>original: "կենդանի" (kendani), i.e., "animal."</note> is predicated of "man." Therefore, "living being" will also be predicated of the certain [man].

...animal shall be **predicated** <note>original: "ստորոգեսցի" (storogests'i). To predicate means to assert or declare something about a subject.</note> of the man; for the individual man is both a "man" and an "animal." In the case of different **genera** <note>original: "սեռիցն" (serrits'n). A genus is a broad category or class.</note> which are not subordinate to one another, the **species** and the **differentiae** <note>original: "տարբերութիւնքն" (tarberut'iunk'n). Differentiae are the specific traits that distinguish one species from another within the same genus.</note> are also different; for example, the genus of "Animal" and the genus of "Science." For the differentiae of "Animal" are "walking," "two-footed," "winged," and "aquatic," but none of these belong to "Science." For one science does not differ from another science by being "two-footed." However, in the case of genera that are subordinate to one another, nothing prevents them from having the same differentiae; for the higher genera are predicated of the lower genera. Thus, all differentiae of the predicated genus will also belong to the **subject** <note>original: "ենթակային" (ent'akayin). In logic, the subject is that which is being described or defined.</note>.

### The Categories

Of those things which are said without any **combination** <note>Refers to single words rather than full sentences.</note>, each signifies either: **Substance**, or **Quantity**, or **Quality**, or **Relation**, or **Place**, or **Time**, or **Position**, or **State**, or **Action**, or **Passion**. 

**Substance** is, for example: "man," "horse." 
**Quantity** is, for example: "two cubits long," "four cubits long." 
**Quality** is, for example: "white," "grammatical." 
**Relation** is, for example: "double," "half," "greater." 
**Place** is, for example: "in the Lyceum," "in the market." 
**Time** is, for example: <term>for example</term> "yesterday," "last year." 
**Position** is, for example: "sitting," "standing." 
And **State** is <term>for example</term> "to be shod," "to be armed." 
And **Action** is <term>for example</term> "to cut," "to burn." 
And **Passion** <note>In this context, passion means "being acted upon."</note> is <term>for example</term> "to be cut," "to be burned."

<column-break/>

And each of these things mentioned, when taken by itself, is not said according to any **affirmation** <note>original: "ստորասութի" (storats'ut'i)</note> or **negation** <note>original: "բացասութի" (bats'asut'i)</note>. However, by the combination of these with one another, an affirmation or negation arises. For every affirmation or negation seems to be either true or false; but of those things said without any combination, none is either true or false—such as "man," "white," "runs," or "conquers."

### Concerning Substance

**Substance**, in the most proper, primary, and principal sense, is that which is neither predicated of a subject nor is it in a subject <term>for example</term>: an individual man or an individual horse. 

But **secondary substances** are those in which the primary substances exist, namely their species. These include the species and the genera of these species. For instance, the individual man is included in the species "Man," and the genus of this species is "Animal." Therefore, these are called secondary substances—for example, "Man" and "Animal." 

For it is evident from what has been said that if something is said of a subject, it is necessary that both its name and its definition be predicated of the subject. <term>For example</term>, "Man" is said of a subject (an individual man), and the name is predicated; for "Man" shall be predicated of the individual man. And the definition of "Man" shall also be predicated of the individual man; for the individual man is both a man and an animal. Thus, both the name and the definition are predicated of the subject...
