<meta>This page continues the translation of Aristotle's "Categories" from Folio 91v. The text explores the relationship between "Primary Substances" (individual things) and "Secondary Substances" (the categories or groups those things belong to), specifically focusing on why species are considered more fundamental than genera.</meta>

...be predicated. <note>original: "ստորոգեսցի" (storogests'i). This refers to the logical act of saying something about a subject.</note> But of those things which exist in a **subject** <term>Subject: <note>original: "ենթակայ" (ent'akay). In logic, the underlying thing or individual being discussed.</note></term>, neither the name nor the definition is predicated of the subject. However, for some, nothing prevents the name from being predicated of the subject, but they are unable to predicate the definition. For example, "white" is in the subject of a body and is predicated of the subject; for a body is called "white." But the definition of "white" will never be predicated of the body. 

But all other things are either said of subjects—that is, of **primary substances**—or they exist naturally in subjects. This becomes clear from each of the individual cases previously set forth. For if one predicates "animal" of "man," then it is predicated of every man; for if it were not predicated of some individual man, then it would not be predicated of "man" as a whole. Again, if it is predicated of "body," then <margin>if</margin> it is also predicated of some individual body; for if no individual partook of the "body," the whole could not be a "body." 

Therefore, all other things are either said of the subjects of primary substances or they follow from the subjects. Since only primary substances can be the subjects for all other things; for all other things are either said of them as subjects or are mixed within them as subjects.

### Third Section

The **species** <term>Species: <note>original: "տեսակք" (tesakk'). A specific class of things, like "man."</note></term> of secondary substances are more truly substances than the **genera** <term>Genera: <note>original: "սեռս" (serrs). A broad category, like "animal."</note></term>. For the species is closer to the primary substance. For if one

<column-break/>

were to explain what a primary substance is, he would explain it more clearly and more appropriately by explaining the species rather than the genus. For example, one would explain the individual man more clearly by saying "man" than by saying "animal." For the former is more specific to the individual man, while the latter is more general. Similarly, one would explain a certain tree more clearly by explaining it as a "tree" rather than as a "plant." 

Furthermore, primary substances are called "primary" because they underlie all other things, and everything else is predicated of them or exists in them. For this reason, they are especially called primary substances. Now, just as primary substances stand in relation to all other things, so also does the species stand in relation to the genus. For the species is the subject of the genus; the genera are predicated of the species, but the species are not predicated of the genera in reverse. Therefore, based on these reasons, the species is more truly a substance than the genus.

### Fourth Section

Among the species themselves, those which are not also genera are no more truly substances than any other. For one would <margin>but</margin> explain an individual man no more appropriately by explaining the "man" than one would explain an individual horse by saying "horse." Similarly, among primary substances, one is no more a substance than another; for the individual man is no more a substance than the individual ox.

->**Predications**<-

<meta>This page continues the translation of Aristotle's "Categories" from Folio 92v. The text explores why species and genera are designated as "Secondary Substances" and examines the logical rules governing how they are predicated of "Primary Substances" (individual entities).</meta>

# The Categories of Aristotle

Quite appropriately, after the primary substances, only their **species** and **genera** are called **secondary substances**, for they alone serve to reveal the primary substance among all the things predicated <note>original: "ստորոգելոցն" (storogelots’n); to predicate is to assert a quality or category of a subject.</note>. For if one were to explain what a specific man is, he would explain it more appropriately and more clearly by providing the **species** <term>Species: <note>original: "տեսակն" (tesakn); the specific class to which an individual belongs, e.g., "human."</note></term> rather than the **genus** <term>Genus: <note>original: "սեռն" (serrn); a broader category that includes multiple species, e.g., "animal."</note></term>. One makes the man more known by saying he is a "man" than by saying he is an "animal." However, regarding anything else one might say to explain him, that explanation will be foreign <note>original: "օտարաբար" (ōtarabar); meaning external or not essential to the definition.</note>, such as if one were to say he is "white," or a "runner," or any other such thing. 

Therefore, it is appropriate that among all things, only these are called **substances**. Furthermore, because primary substances underlie everything else, they are called substances in the most authoritative and truest sense. And just as the primary substance stands in relation to all other things, so also the species and genera of the primary substance stand in relation to all other things; for all other things are predicated of them. For if you say that a certain man is a "grammarian," then it follows that you say both "man" and "animal" are "grammarian," and so it is with others.

->Sixth Section<-

It is evident that nothing among the substances is "in a subject" <note>Aristotle distinguishes between being "said of" a subject (like a category) and being "in" a subject (like a property or accident).</note>, for a primary substance is neither in a subject nor is it said of a subject. As for the secondary substances, it is clear that they are like this:

<column-break/>

[They are said] of the subject; for "man" is said of the subject—that is, of any individual man—but it is not *in* a subject. For "man" is not an internal property of the individual man <note>i.e., humanity is the essence, not a quality like color.</note>. Likewise, "animal" is also said of the subject, of any individual man, but "animal" is not *in* any man. Furthermore, while nothing prevents the name of things existing in a subject from being predicated of the subject, it is impossible for their definition to be so predicated. But for secondary substances, both the name and the definition are predicated of the subject; for you will predicate the definition of "man" to any individual man, and likewise the definition of "animal." Therefore, it is not a property of substance to be "in a subject."

->Seventh Section<-

But this is not unique to substance; rather, the **differentia** <term>Differentia: <note>original: "տարբերութիւնն" (tarberut’iunn); the specific characteristic that distinguishes one species from another within the same genus.</note></term> also does not exist "in a subject." For "walking" and "two-footed" are said of the subject "man," but they are not *in* a subject; for "walking" or "two-footed" are not properties contained within the man [like a color]. For the definition of the differentia is predicated of that of which it is said; the differentia "walking" is said of "man," and the definition of "walking" will be predicated of the man, for the man is a walking being.

->Eighth Section<-

But let us not be troubled by the parts of substances <note>The author is clarifying that "parts" of a substance, like a hand or an eye, are not "in a subject" in the way an accident is, but are parts of the whole.</note>, as if they were in subjects like a part of nature; let us not be forced to say these are not substances, for they are not "in a subject" in that specific [accidental] sense.

<summary>This page explains why species and genera are "secondary substances" and argues that substances (both primary and secondary) and their defining characteristics (differentia) are never "in a subject" as accidental properties.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Categories, Primary Substance, Secondary Substance, Species, Genus, Differentia, Predication, Subject, Logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Armenian translation and commentary of Aristotle's "Categories" (Ստորոգութիւնք). It follows the discussion on Primary and Secondary substances, specifically focusing on how they are predicated of subjects and their logical characteristics, such as the lack of contraries.</meta>

... are said to be as if participating in many individuals.

### Section Nine

It is common to all **substances** and all **differences** <note>original: "տարբերութեց" (tarberut’yents); refers to the specific characteristics that distinguish one species from another within a genus.</note> to be spoken of **synonymously** <note>original: "փաղանունաբար" (p’aghanunabar); in Aristotelian logic, this means that both the name and the definition of the predicate apply to the subject.</note> from them. For all predicates are predicated either of **individuals** <term>Individuals: <note>original: "անհատիցն" (anhatits’n); literally 'indivisibles,' referring to primary substances like a specific person or horse.</note></term> or of **species**. 

For no predicate is derived from a **primary substance**, because it is not spoken of any subject whatsoever. However, regarding **secondary substances**, the **species** is predicated of the **individual**, and the **genus** is predicated of both the **species** and the **individual**. Likewise, **differences** are also predicated of both species and individuals. The primary substances admit the definition <note>original: "զբանն" (zbann); here meaning the essential logical definition or formula.</note> of the species and the genera, and the species admit the definition of the genera. For whatever is said of the predicate will also be said of the subject. Similarly, both species and individuals admit the definition of the differences. A **synonym** <note>univocal term</note> is that for which both the name is common and the definition is the same. Therefore, all substances and differences are spoken of synonymously.

### Section Ten

**E**very substance seems to signify "a certain this" <note>original: "զայս ինչ" (zays inch); translating the Greek 'tode ti,' referring to a specific, identifiable object.</note>. Now, in the case of **primary substance**, it is indisputably true that it signifies "a certain this," for that which is revealed is an **individual** and **one in number**. But in the case of **secondary substances**, it appears 

<column-break/>

<page-num>94</page-num>

likewise, by the form of the name, to signify "a certain this" whenever one says "man" or "animal." However, this is not strictly true; rather, it signifies a **certain quality** <note>original: "որակիմն" (orakimn); suggesting a classification or sort rather than a single entity.</note>. For the subject is not one, as the primary substance is, but "man" and "animal" are said of many. Yet it does not signify a quality simply or accidentally, like the word "white" does. For "white" signifies nothing other than a quality. But the species and the genus determine the quality in relation to substance; for they signify a "substance of a certain quality." But the **genus** provides a broader determination than the **species**, for he who says "animal" encompasses a wider range than he who says "man."

### Section Eleven

**I**t is also characteristic of substances that nothing is **contrary** to them. For what could be contrary to a primary substance? For example, nothing is contrary to an individual "man" or "animal." This is not unique to substance, but is also true of many other things, such as **quantity**. For nothing is contrary to "two cubits long," nor to "ten," nor to any such thing—unless one were to say that "many" is the contrary of "few," or "large" is the contrary of "small." But among definite quantities <note>original: "բաց որոշելոցն" (bats’ oroshelots’n); referring to specific numerical values.</note>, nothing is contrary.

### Section Twelve

**B**ut it seems that substance does not admit of the **more or less** <note>original: "զբ..." (zb...); likely 'զբնաւ' (entirely) or 'զբազմութիւն' (multiplicity), but contextually referring to degrees of intensity.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the synonymous nature of substance predication and argues that while primary substances signify a specific "this," secondary substances (species/genera) signify a "certain quality" of substance. It also introduces the logical rule that substances have no contraries and do not admit of degrees.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Aristotle’s "Categories" from the Armenian tradition. The previous page concluded the discussion on whether substances have contraries. This page explores whether substance admits of degrees (more or less) and examines the most defining characteristic of substance: its ability to receive contraries while remaining numerically one.</meta>

...the **more** and the **less**. And I say this not because one **substance** <note>original: "գոյացութի" (goyats'ut'i); the fundamental being or "ousia" in Greek.</note> is not more of a substance than another—for that has already been stated—but because each substance, in terms of what it is, is not said to be "more" or "less." For example, if this substance is a **man**, he will not be more or less a man, neither than himself nor than another. For one man is not "more" of a man than another, in the way that one **white** thing is more white than another, or as a thing is said to be more or less than itself. For a body that is white is said to be more white now than it was before; and that which is **hot** is said to be more or less hot. But substance is not spoken of as "more" or "less." For a man is not said to be more of a man now than he was before, nor is this true of any of the other things that are substances. Therefore, substance does not admit of the **more** and the **less**.

---

### Section Thirteen

The most characteristic property of **substance** seems to be this: that being one and the same in number, it is capable of receiving **contraries** <term>Contraries: <note>original: "ներհակացն" (nerhakats’n); things that are opposite to one another within the same class, such as black and white.</note></term>. Among other things that are not substances, one could not find anything that, being one in number, is capable of receiving contraries. For instance, a **color** which is one and the same in number is not both white and black; nor is the same action one in number both **wicked** and **virtuous**.

<column-break/>

The same applies to other things that are not substances. But a **substance**, while being one and the same in number, is capable of receiving contraries. For example, a certain individual man, while remaining one and the same, sometimes becomes **white** and sometimes **black**; or **hot** and **cold**; or **wicked** and **virtuous**. Among other things, nothing of this sort is ever seen.

---

### Section Fourteen

But if someone should object, saying that **speech** <note>original: "բանն" (bann); meaning a statement or proposition.</note> and **opinion** <note>original: "կարծիսն" (kartsisn); a belief or thought held by the mind.</note> are among such things, [they are mistaken]. For the same statement seems to be both **true** and **false**. For instance, if the statement "someone is sitting" is true, after he has stood up, this same statement will be false. The same applies to opinion. For if someone truly opines that a person is sitting, once that person has stood up, he will have a false opinion regarding him. Even if one accepts this [objection], there is a difference in the **manner** [of change]. For in the case of substances, they are capable of receiving contraries by **changing themselves**. For that which was cold turned into heat, having changed; and the black into white, and the virtuous into wicked. Likewise with each of the others, the things themselves receive a change and are said to be capable of contraries. But speech and opinion themselves remain entirely **unmoved** <note>original: "անշարժք" (ansharzhk’); static or unchanging in their own nature.</note> and unchanged in every way. It is because the **fact** <note>original: "իրին" (irin); the actual reality or thing in the world.</note> is moved...

<summary>This page discusses how substances do not admit of degrees (one cannot be "more" or "less" a man) and defines the unique property of substance as the ability to undergo change and hold contrary qualities while remaining the same entity.</summary>
<keywords>substance, degrees of being, contraries, change, logic, Aristotle, Armenian translation, truth and falsehood</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the translation of Aristotle’s "Categories," specifically the end of the chapter on Substance (Ousia) and the beginning of the chapter on Quantity. The previous page discussed how substance, while remaining numerically one, can admit contrary qualities.</meta>

...[of the thing] having changed, the contrary occurs. For the statement remains the same, as does the act of someone sitting. However, when the actual thing has moved, the statement becomes true or false. The same is true regarding opinions. Therefore, it is in a unique way that a **substance** <note>original: "գոյացութի" (goyats'ut'i); the Greek "ousia," referring to an individual thing like a specific man or horse.</note> is capable of receiving contraries according to its own change. 

But if someone were to accept even those—stating that opinions and statements are capable of receiving contraries—this is not true. For a statement and an opinion are not said to be "capable of receiving contraries" because they themselves have received something, but because a **passive state** <note>original: "կիրքն" (kirkn); here referring to an affection or modification in the external world.</note> has occurred in something else. For it is by the thing being or not being that the statement is said to be true or false; the statements do not themselves change to become capable of receiving contraries. 

Simply and once and for all: neither speech nor opinion is moved by anything, therefore they cannot be capable of receiving contraries, as nothing happens within them. But a substance is said to be "capable of receiving contraries" because it changes to receive the contraries themselves. 

For it [the substance] receives sickness and health, and whiteness and blackness. By receiving each of these into itself, it is said to be "capable of receiving contraries." Therefore, it is the unique property of substance that it is one and the same in number, yet through its own change, it is capable of being...

->**The Categories**<-

<column-break/>

...contraries. And this is as much as I shall say regarding substance. —

### On Quantity
<image-desc>A large ornamental ink-flourished design framing the start of the section. It features stylized botanical and abstract motifs with the Armenian letter 'Յ' (Y) integrated into the decorative structure.</image-desc>
Of **quantity** <term>Quantity: <note>original: "քանակն" (k'anakn); the category of "poson," dealing with how much or how many.</note></term>, some is **discrete** <note>original: "տարորոշ" (tarorosh); literally "separated" or "distinct," like numbers.</note> and some is **continuous**. And some [quantities] consist of parts that have a position in relation to each other, while others do not. 

Examples of discrete quantities are **speech** and **number**. Continuous quantities include the **point**, the **line**, the **surface**, and the **body** <note>original: "մարմին" (marmin); referring to a 3D solid.</note>; and furthermore, **time** and **place**. 

For in the parts of a number, there is no common boundary where the parts are joined. For instance, five and five are parts of ten, but the two fives join at no common boundary; rather, they are discrete. Likewise, three and seven join at no common boundary. Indeed, you will never find a common boundary for the parts of a number; they are always discrete. Therefore, number is a discrete quantity. 

The same is true for **speech** <note>original: "բան" (ban); here Aristotle is referring to the phonemes or syllables of spoken language.</note> which is also discrete. <margin>i</margin> 
<note>margin: This letter 'i' likely serves as a section marker or a reference for the student.</note>
That speech is a quantity is evident, for it is measured by long and short syllables; I refer here to the speech that is produced by the voice. For its parts do not join together at any common boundary. For...

->**Aristotle**<-

...because there is no common boundary at which the parts of the series **join** <note>original: "շարամօտին" (sharamōtin); a technical term in the Armenian "Hellenizing School" translations meaning to be contiguous or to meet at a joint.</note>; rather, each part is distinguished and exists separately by itself.

## Section Three

A **line** <note>original: "գիծն" (gitsn); the OCR "լուսագիծ" (lusagits) is likely a decorative or scribal variation for "the line."</note> is continuous. For it is possible to find a common boundary where its parts join, which is the **point** <note>original: "կէտն" (ketn); in geometry, a point has no parts but serves as the boundary where two line segments meet.</note>. Similarly, for a **surface** <note>original: "մակարդակին" (makardakin); refers to a two-dimensional plane.</note>, the boundary is the line; for the parts of a surface join together at a common boundary. Likewise, you will find that for a **body** <note>original: "մարմնոյ" (marmnoy); referring to a three-dimensional object or physical volume.</note>, one can take a common boundary: the line or the surface, at which the parts of the body join and are united. There are also **discrete** <note>original: "անջատուածականքն" (anjatuatsakank'n); quantities whose parts do not share a common boundary, like numbers or speech.</note> quantities, which have the smallest boundary. But smaller parts do not [always share boundaries], as was said of the body having parts with a common boundary, whether a line or a surface. However, a surface is no longer a boundary once it becomes a body, for one is triple <note>three-dimensional</note> while the other is double <note>two-dimensional</note>. **Time** and **place** also belong to these [continuous] types, since the past and the future join together at the present moment. And again, place is among the continuous things, for the parts of a body occupy a certain place which join at a common boundary; and consequently, the parts of the **place** <note>original: "տեղոյն" (teghoyn)</note> which contain each of the parts of the body join at the same boundary as the parts of the body. Therefore, place must also be continuous, because its parts join at one common

<meta>catchword: Categories. -</meta>

<column-break/>

boundary.

## Section Four

Furthermore, among these, some are composed of parts that have a **position** <note>original: "դիր" (dir); the Greek "thesis," referring to how parts are situated relative to one another.</note> relative to each other, and there are those whose parts do not have a position. For the parts of a line have a position relative to each other, because each of them is situated somewhere; you can distinguish and explain where each part lies on the plane and at which point it joins to another part. Similarly, the parts of a surface have a certain position, for it can be explained in the same way where each part is situated, which ones join together, and their respective placements. The same applies to place.

## Section Five

But regarding **number** <note>original: "թուոյ" (t'vōy)</note>, no one could perceive that its parts have any position relative to each other, or that they "stay" anywhere, or that they have boundaries where the parts join. Nor do the parts of **time** have position, for none of the parts of time endure <note>meaning they do not "stand still" to have a relative spatial position.</note>; and that which does not endure, how could it have a position? Rather, you would say they have a certain **order** <note>original: "դաս" (das); the Greek "taxis," meaning a sequence (first, second, third) rather than a spatial arrangement.</note>, because one part of time is prior and another is later. The same is true of number: in counting, one is prior to two, and two is prior to three; in this way they possess a certain order, but you cannot find a position.

<summary>This page discusses Aristotle's category of Quantity, distinguishing between "continuous" quantities (like lines, surfaces, and time) which share common boundaries, and "discrete" quantities (like numbers) which do not. It also introduces the concept of "position" versus "order."</summary>

...to be taken. And regarding these, nothing remains as a part <note>original: "ենթամնայ մասն" - referring to the underlying parts of a quantity</note> of it, because what has been said is that it is a triad. Therefore, there would be no position for its parts, because nothing remains underlying. Now, there are those things composed of parts having a position relative to one another, and there are those whose parts do not have a position.

### Lesson Six

Truly and properly, only these aforementioned things are called quantities. All others are called so by accident <note>Aristotle distinguishes between essential quantities (like numbers or lines) and accidental quantities (like "white" being called "much" because the surface it covers is large).</note>. For looking at these, we call the others quantities as well; for either "white" is called "much" because the surface is large, or a duration is called "long" because the time is much, and a motion is called "much." For each of these is not called a quantity in itself, but rather if one explains how much of a reality it is, one defines it by time. In the case of a year's duration, one explains it in such a formal way. And "white" is explained as a certain quantity when the surface is defined; for however large the surface is, so also one will speak of the white. Thus, only these aforementioned are called quantities truly and in themselves; but of the others, nothing is so in itself, but only by effect of accident.

### Lesson Seven

In quantity, there is nothing contrary to quantity. This is evident in those things clearly defined; for there is nothing

->The Categories<-

<column-break/>

contrary to a triangle, or to a surface, or to any such things. Nothing would be contrary to them. But if someone were to say that "much" is contrary to "few," or "great" to "small," these are not quantities, but rather belong to the category of Relatives <note>original: "առընչիցն" - referring to things 'toward something else' or relations</note>. For nothing is called great or small in itself, but is referred to something else. For instance, a mountain is called small, and a grain of millet is called great; this is because the latter is called great in comparison to those of its own kind <note>original: "համասեռիցն" - of the same genus or species</note>, and the former is called small compared to those of its own kind. Therefore, it is a relation of one to another. If "small" and "great" were so in themselves, a mountain would never be called small, nor a grain of millet great. <margin>and</margin> Again, we say there are "many" people in a village and "few" in Athens, even though the latter are many times more than the former; and "few" in a theater, but "many" in a house. Furthermore, "two cubits long" and "three cubits long" and each such thing signifies a quantity. But "great" or "small" does not signify quantity, but rather a relative, for "great" and "small" are viewed in relation to something else. Therefore, it is evident that these belong to the Relatives.

### Lesson Eight

Furthermore, whether one posits these to be quantities or does not posit them, there is nothing contrary to them. For that which is not taken in itself, but is referred to others—how could it have a contrary?

->Of Aristotle<-

<keywords>Aristotle, Categories, Quantity, Relatives, Logic, Armenian Manuscript, Philosophy, Accidental Properties</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Aristotle's "Categories," specifically focusing on the nature of Quantity and the concept of contraries. It follows the previous analysis of relative terms.</meta>

nothing is contrary to these. Furthermore, if the **great** and the **small** were to be considered contraries, it would happen that the same thing would receive contraries at the same time, and things would be contraries to themselves. For it happens that the same thing is both great and small at the same time; for in comparison to one thing it is small, and in comparison to another, this same thing is great. Therefore, it happens that the same thing is both great and small in the same way, such that it receives contraries at the same time. But nothing seems to receive contraries at the same time. In the case of substances <note>original: "goyatsut'iwnk'" - referring to Aristotle's primary category of Being</note>, it is thought that they are capable of receiving contraries, yet no one is both sick and healthy at once, nor white and black at once. Nor does anything else possess contraries simultaneously. And things would happen to be contraries to themselves; for if the great is contrary to the small, and the same thing is simultaneously great and small, it would be contrary to itself. But it is among the impossible things for something to be its own contrary. Therefore, the great is not the contrary of the small, nor is "much" the contrary of "few." So then, even if one does not call these "receivers" <note>referring to the subjects that hold these qualities</note>, but calls them quantities, they possess no contrary.

### Lesson 8. Ninth <note>This likely refers to a chapter or section division in the medieval Armenian commentary tradition of Aristotle</note>

Especially since contrariety of quantity seems to exist in regard to place; for they place "above" as the contrary to "below," saying that the region at the center is contrary to the furthest extremes,

<meta>catchword: Categories</meta>

<column-break/>

because they defined the distance from the center to the limits of the world as being the greatest. And they define other contraries by this explanation; for they say that those things in the same genus which are most distant from each other are contraries.

### Lesson 9. Third Part

But quantity does not seem to admit of "more" and "less." For example, a "two-cubit" measure <note>a cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm</note> does not seem to be "more" than any other; this two-cubit measure is not "more" than another in terms of its number. Nor is "five" said to be "more" five than "three" is three. Nor is one "three" more of a "three" than another "three." Also, time is not said to be "more" or "less" of a time at all. In short, regarding none of the mentioned quantities is "more" or "less" spoken of. Therefore, quantity does not admit of the "more" and the "less." And now, the most distinctive mark of quantity is to be called **equal** and **unequal**. For each of the mentioned quantities is called equal and unequal. For example, a body is called equal and unequal, and a number is called equal and unequal <note>The Armenian "zoyg" can mean "even" or "equal/pair"; in this context of Aristotle's Categories, it refers to equality of magnitude</note>. Here, regarding each of the other things spoken of, they are called equal and unequal. But for other things, as many as are not quantities, they cannot very well be called equal and unequal. For example, a disposition <note>a state of being</note> cannot very well be called equal and unequal, but rather "similar." And "white" is not called equal and unequal, but "similar." Therefore, the most unique characteristic of quantity would be to be called equal and unequal.

## On Relatives

<note>The text begins with an ornamental drop-cap 'A' shaped like a bird, typical of medieval Armenian manuscript illumination.</note>

Those things are called **relatives** which, by their very nature, are said to be of other things, or are in some way referred to something else. For example, "the greater" is what it is by being greater than something else; for it is called greater than another. And "the double" is what it is by being double of another; for it is called the double of something. And so it is with all other such things.

Furthermore, such things as **habits** <note>original: "ունակութիւն" (unakt'yun) - a stable disposition or state of being</note>, **dispositions**, **perceptions**, **knowledge**, and **positions** are also relatives. For all these mentioned are what they are by being of other things, or are referred to something else in some manner. For a "habit" is called the habit of someone, "knowledge" is the knowledge of something, and "position" is the position of something, and so on for the rest.

Therefore, relatives are those things which, whatever they are, are said to be of others, or are related to something else in some way. For instance, a "mountain" is called great in relation to something else, for the mountain is called great compared to another. And "similar" is called similar to something; and other such things are likewise called relatives.

## Second Part

Now, **reclining**, **standing**, and **sitting** are certain kinds of positions, and positions are among the relatives. However, to be reclining, or to be standing, or to be sitting, are not themselves positions, but are named paronymously <note>derived from the name of the category</note> from the aforementioned positions.

<column-break/>

There is also **contrariety** <note>opposition</note> in relatives. For example, **virtue** is the contrary of **vice**, and each of them is a relative; likewise **knowledge** is the contrary of **ignorance**. But not all relatives have a contrary; for "the double" has no contrary, nor does "the triple," nor any such numerical relations.

However, it seems that relatives admit of **more and less** <note>degrees of intensity</note>. For something is called "more similar" or "less similar," and "unequal" is likewise called "more" or "less" unequal, and each of these is a relative. For the similar is called similar to something, and the unequal is called unequal to something. Yet, not all relatives admit of the more and the less; for "the double" does not admit of being "more double" or "less double," nor does any such definite ratio.

## Fourth Part

All relatives are spoken of in **reciprocity** <note>original: "հակադարձանս" - a turning back or mutual relationship</note>. For example, a "slave" is called the slave of a "master," and a "master" is called the master of a "slave." And "the double" is the double of "the half," and "the half" is the half of "the double." And "the greater" is called greater than "the less," and "the less" is called less than "the greater." This applies to other cases as well.

However, they may sometimes differ in their grammatical case <note>referring to linguistic inflection</note>. For instance, "knowledge" is called the knowledge of the "knowable," and the "knowable" is that which is known by...

<summary>This page discusses the Aristotelian category of Relatives, defining them as things that exist in relation to others (like "double" or "knowledge") and exploring their properties such as contrariety and reciprocity.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Categories, Relatives, Logic, Philosophy, Reciprocity, Contrariety, Habits, Knowledge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical discussion on relational logic, specifically focusing on the necessity of "reciprocity" or "reversibility" in definitions, likely from a translation of Aristotle's Categories.</meta>

...perceived by perception, and the sense of perceiving <insert>the</insert> perception; and the sensible is perceived by the sense. But, however, it sometimes happens that it does not seem to reverse unless it is explained appropriately <note>original: "ընտանեբար" (entanebar) - meaning naturally, properly, or domestically related</note> to that which it is said to be; rather, let that which is explained be changed. For example: a wing. If it is explained as a wing "of a bird," it does not reverse as "a bird of a wing." For the wing has not been explained appropriately to the first term as a wing of a bird; for it is not insofar as it is a bird that its wing is spoken of, but insofar as it is "winged." For there are many other things that have wings which are not birds. Therefore, if it is explained appropriately, it also reverses; for example: a wing is the wing of a winged thing, and the winged thing is winged by the wing.

### Chapter Five <note>original: "Պրակք" (Prakk) - section or chapter</note>

But it is sometimes necessary perhaps even to coin a name <note>original: "անուանագործել" - to create a word</note> if there is no name existing to which it may be appropriately explained. For instance: if the rudder of a ship is explained, the explanation is not appropriate; for it is not insofar as it is a ship that this is called its rudder, since there are ships that do not have rudders. For which reason it does not reverse; for a ship is not called "of a rudder." But perhaps the most appropriate explanation would be if it were explained in this manner: a rudder is the rudder of a "ruddered" thing <note>original: "ղեկաւորի" (ghekavori) - one possessing a rudder</note>, or however else it might be phrased, since a specific name does not exist. And it reverses—

<column-break/>

—if it is explained appropriately; for the "ruddered" thing is ruddered by the rudder. The same applies to others. For instance: a head should be appropriately explained as the head of a "headed" thing <note>original: "գլխաւորի" (glkhavori)</note> rather than being explained as the head of a living being. For it is not insofar as it is a living being that it has a head, since many living beings do not have heads. And in this way, one might perhaps easily grasp those things for which names do not exist, if from the primary terms one establishes names for those things which reverse with them. For example, from the previously mentioned: from "wing" comes "winged," and from "rudder" comes "ruddered." Now, all these things, if they are explained appropriately, are said in relation to their reverse. But if they are explained in relation to something accidental <note>original: "դիպաւոր" (dipavor)</note> and not to that which they are truly said of, they do not reverse.

### Chapter Six

And I say: that even among those things acknowledged to be said in relation to a reverse, and where their names are established, nothing reverses if it is explained in relation to its accidents and not to that which it is. For instance: if a "slave" is not explained as being of a "master," but of a "man" or a "biped" or any such thing, it does not reverse; for the explanation is not appropriate. Furthermore, if it is explained appropriately to that which it is said of, while stripping away all other things which are merely accidental, but leaving only this—

<meta>This page continues the translation of Aristotle's *Categories* (specifically the section on Relatives) from the Armenian version attributed to the school of David the Invincible. The text transitions from the discussion of Relatives to the category of Quality.</meta>

If someone knows a certain thing that belongs to the category of **relatives** <note>original: "առընչիցն" (aranchitsn) — referring to Aristotle's category of *Ad Aliquid* or Relatives</note>, and knows the identity of that relation, they will also know that to which it is related. For if one does not know at all that to which it is related, one cannot know how it relates to it. This is evident in individual cases: for example, if someone knows for certain that a thing is "double," they must immediately know for certain what it is the double of. For if they do not know it as the double of a specific thing, they do not truly know that it is "double" at all. Likewise, if one knows that something is "more beautiful," they must necessarily know what it is more beautiful than. 

However, if one only knows indefinitely that "this is better than something inferior," this is merely an **opinion** <note>Greek: doxa</note> and not **scientific knowledge** <note>original: "մակացութիւն" (makatsutyun), translating the Greek *episteme*</note>; for they do not accurately know that it is better than a specific inferior thing. If it happens by chance, there might be nothing inferior to it at all. Therefore, it is evident that it is necessary for anyone who has certain knowledge of a relative to also know that to which it is related.

### Chapter 13: The Third [Category] <note>Referring to Quality</note>
<margin>|</margin> But if one does not know a "head" or a "hand"—each of which are **substances** <note>original: "գոյացութիւնք" (goyatsutyunk), translating *ousia*</note>—one might still know exactly what they are

<column-break/>

in themselves. But for the sake of learning, it is not necessary [to know the relation]. For whose head it is, or whose hand it is, need not be known by a defining rule. Therefore, these are not among the **relatives**. And if they are not relatives, it would be true to say that no **substance** 
<margin>5</margin> is a relative. But perhaps it is difficult to demonstrate such things clearly by a definitive decree without having questioned and investigated many times; yet, to doubt and to hesitate over each of these points is not an unprofitable exercise.

**Qualities: Second [Part].**
Now, one species of quality is called **habit** <note>original: "ունակութիւն" (unakutyun), Greek: *hexis*</note> and **disposition** <note>original: "տրամադրութիւն" (tramadrutyun), Greek: *diathesis*</note>. For science seems to be something permanent and difficult to change, even if one possesses science only to a moderate degree, provided it is not altered by sickness or some other such cause. Likewise with the virtues, such as justice and temperance, and each of the other such qualities.

# On the Unchangeable Nature of Disposition

Every unchangeable **disposition** <note>original: "tramadrut’iwn" (տրամադրութիւն) — a term used in Armenian philosophy to translate the Greek 'diathesis', referring to a state or condition</note> is said to be a single, minute, and detailed entity, because qualities are perceived through them. In these, those who remain invisibly saddened are said to possess a certain state. And perhaps the name itself remains; it is called a "hachi" <note>This term is obscure; it may refer to a specific phonetic or logical value in the original school of translation</note> which is invisibly called a "forceful quality" according to others. That which is revealed beyond this is called the word of the state.

For that which was said above is said to possess a single word <note>In this context, "word" (ban) likely refers to the Greek 'logos', meaning definition or rational principle</note>; but rather the continuous, invisibly in the first, the first of the state. However, such things are not found among the many.

— Those qualities which appear through this are said to be those revealed invisibly as the aforementioned **qualities**. As for the "accidental" <note>original: "ekamut" (եկամուտ) — literally 'that which has come in', used to translate the philosophical concept of accidents or non-essential properties</note>, how should it come to be in that manner from them? —

<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of Aristotle's "Categories," specifically focusing on the chapter regarding Quality (Grabar: Որակութիւն). It follows the discussion on whether qualities admit of degrees (more or less) and transitions into the relationship between Quality and Relatives (Grabar: Առ ընչիցն).</meta>

they admit the definition in the same way. For one person is called **more grammatical** than another, or **more courageous** or **more just**, and the same applies to other such qualities. However, a triangle and a square do not seem to admit the definition of "more" or "less," nor do any of the other geometric figures. For those things which once admit the definition of a triangle or a circle are all equally triangles and circles. But those things which do not admit the definition cannot be said to be one more than the other; for a square is not "less" of a circle than an oblong shape is, because neither of them admits the definition of a circle at all. But simply put, if both do not accept the proposed definition, one cannot be said to be more or less than the other. Thus, not all qualities admit the definition of degree in the same way.

---
->Qualities: Twelve, Three<- <note>This likely refers to the numbering of chapters or sections in the commentary tradition.</note>
---

Whatever is necessary to say, nothing is as specific to quality as the fact that **similar** and **dissimilar** are spoken of only in respect to qualities. For one thing is similar to another not by any other measure, but according to its quality. Therefore, it would be a unique characteristic of quality to be called "similar" or "dissimilar" in itself. But one should not be troubled lest someone says that we, in making our propositions regarding quality, have included many things that are actually considered **relatives** <term>Relatives (Grabar: Առ ընչիցն): things that are defined by their relationship to something else, like "double" or "knowledge of."</term> and 

<column-break/>

placed them among qualities. For we say that **habits** <term>Habit (Grabar: ունակութիւն): a stable, long-term disposition like knowledge or virtue.</term> and **dispositions** are relatives. For in almost all such cases, the genera are called relatives, but the individual instances are not. For **knowledge** <term>Knowledge (Grabar: մակացութիւն): used here as the genus for specific sciences.</term> is a genus, and what it is, is said of something else; for it is called "knowledge *of* something." But the individual instances are not said to be of something else in their own right. For example, **grammar** is not called "grammar of something," nor is **music** called "music of something." Perhaps, indeed, according to the genus, these are called relatives—as grammar is called a "knowledge of something," but not "grammar of something," and music is called a "knowledge of something," but not "music of something." Therefore, according to the individual instances, they are not relatives. And we are called "qualified" according to these; for we possess them, and we are called "knowledgeable" because we possess some of these individual talents. Therefore, these would be qualities in their individual instances, according to which we are sometimes called "qualified." And these are not relatives. Furthermore, if the same thing should happen to be both a quality and a relative, it would not be out of place. Both are mixed in the account <unclear>to fall into</unclear> <unclear>the class</unclear>.

### Concerning Acting and Being Acted Upon

There is a contrariety <note>original: "nerhakanuti"; refers to the logical opposition between opposites</note> in acting and being acted upon, between like and like. For heating is contrary to cooling, and being heated is contrary to being cooled, and feeling pleasure is contrary to feeling sorrow. Therefore, they admit contrariety, like and like; for heating and taking <note>learning</note> are things that can be taught, while sorrowing is more or less intense. Therefore, acting and being acted upon admit of like and like <note>degrees of intensity</note>. And now, this much is said concerning them.

And it has also been spoken concerning "Position" <note>the category of Situs</note> in the section on Relation, for it is spoken of by a name derived from its existence. As for the other three—Time, Place, and State <note>Habitus</note>—because they were clearly manifested previously, nothing else is said about them other than what was once discussed regarding them. For "to have" <note>State/Habitus</note> signifies being shod or being armed; while "where" <note>Place</note> <unclear>is</unclear> like being submerged, and others which were once discussed concerning these things.

Now, regarding the aforementioned genera <note>the Categories</note>, the things previously said are sufficient.

### Concerning Opposites

Now, concerning opposites: in how many ways are they usually discussed as being opposed? It is said that one thing is opposed to another in four ways: either as relatives, or as contraries, or as privation

<column-break/>

<page-num>110</page-num>

and possession <note>original: "pakasuti ev unakuti"; referring to the Aristotelian concepts of Privatio and Habitus</note>, or as affirmation and negation. And each of these opposites is spoken of, so to speak, by way of illustration: as relatives, like the half to the double, by a half; as contraries, <unclear>as</unclear> good to evil, by the good; as according to privation and possession, <unclear>as</unclear> blindness to sight; and as affirmation and negation, <unclear>as</unclear> "he sits" and "he does not sit."

Now, those things which are opposed as relatives are said to be what they are of their opposites, or in whatever other way they relate to them. For example, the double of the half is what it is; it is called the double of something else. And knowledge is opposed to the object of knowledge as a relative; and knowledge itself is what it is of the object of knowledge, and the object of knowledge itself is spoken of in relation to the opposed knowledge.

Now, those things which are opposed as relatives are themselves what they are, or are spoken of in relation to one another in some way. But as for contraries, they are themselves what they are and are not in any way spoken of in relation to one another. Nevertheless, contraries are called [opposites] of one another; for the good is not called the "good of the evil," but its contrary. And white is not called the "white of the black," but its contrary. Therefore, these types of oppositions differ from one another.

both are said of it. However, it is not rightly said that a man is "of blindness," nor is a man called "blindness" in any way at all. And these things—the having of a <term>privation</term><note>original: պակասութի (pakasut’i); the absence of a natural quality</note> and a <term>possession</term><note>original: ունակութի (unakut’i); the presence of a quality, like sight</note>—seem to be opposed to one another, just as privation and possession are. For the mode of opposition is the same; for just as blindness is opposed to sight, so too is being blind opposed to having sight.

### Section Five

But those things which fall under <term>affirmation</term><note>original: ստորասութի (storasut’i)</note> and <term>negation</term><note>original: բացասութի (bats’asut’i)</note> are defined thus: affirmation and negation. For an affirmation is an affirmative statement, while a negation is a negative statement. However, those things which fall under affirmation or negation are not themselves statements. Yet these too are said to be opposed to one another, just as affirmation and negation are. For in their case as well, the mode of opposition is the same. For as at one time an affirmation stands against a negation—such as "he sits" and "he does not sit"—so also the things falling under each of them sometimes stand opposed: "sitting" against "not sitting." And it is evident that privation and possession are not opposed as <term>relatives</term><note>original: ատինքն (atink’n); things defined by their relation to one another</note>. For that which they are is not said of the things they are opposed to. For sight is not "sight of blindness," nor is it said in any other way or by any other right in relation to this. Furthermore, blindness is not called "blindness

<column-break/>

of sight," but rather blindness is called the "privation of sight." Moreover, all relatives are spoken of in terms of <term>reciprocity</term><note>original: հակադարձ (hakadardz); a back-and-forth relationship</note>. Therefore, if blindness were a relative, if sight were possessed, it would be reciprocal to that which it is said of. But it is not reciprocal, for one does not say "sight of blindness."

### Section Six

And it is clear from the following that those things spoken of according to privation and possession are not opposed in the same way as <term>contraries</term><note>original: ներհակքն (nerhakk’n)</note>. For among contraries which have no intermediate, it is necessary for one of them to always belong to that in which they naturally occur or of which they are predicated. And for these, there was nothing in between them, where one of them must necessarily be in the recipient, such as sickness and health, or <unclear>even and odd</unclear>. But for those which have something in between, it is never necessary for one of them to be present. For it is not necessary for every recipient to be either white or black, nor either hot or cold; for nothing prevents an intermediate from existing. And there was also an intermediate for those where it was not necessary for one to be in the recipient, except for those in which one belongs by nature. For example, it is the nature of fire to be hot, and of snow to be white. And in these cases, it is necessary for one to be in the recipient, and it is necessarily one specifically—not just whichever one happens to be.

# Seventh Treatise

For fire does not admit the cold, nor does snow admit the heat. Therefore, it is not necessary in all cases for the receptive subject <note>original: "ընդունական" - the thing capable of receiving a quality</note> to be one of them, but only for those things in whose nature it exists; and for these, it is never the case that one of them is not present in a defined way.

But regarding privation <note>original: "պակասութիւն" - the absence of a natural property</note> and possession <note>original: "ունակութիւն" - the presence of a natural property</note>, none of the aforementioned things are true. For it is not always the case that the receptive subject is inclined to be one of them. For that which is not yet naturally disposed to have sight is called neither blind nor seeing. Therefore, these are not among those kinds of contraries which have nothing in between them. 

But neither are they among those which have something in between; for it is never necessary for the receptive subject to be one of them in a defined way. For only when it is naturally disposed to have sight will it then be called blind or seeing. And it is not defined as to which one it will be; for it is not necessary to call it either blind or seeing, but rather whichever one happened to occur. 

However, regarding contraries that have something in between, it was never necessary for one of them to be present in everything, but rather the intermediates. But for possession and privation, it is not necessary for the receptive subject to receive certain other things, for there is no intermediate, yet it is also not necessary for one of them to be present in a defined way. Thus, it is evident that in whatever manner contraries are opposed, they are opposed according to privation and possession.

<column-break/>

**V** <note>The text begins with a decorative bird-shaped drop cap for the letter V.</note> as much as regarding contraries, when there is a receptive subject, a change into one another can occur, unless one of them exists by nature. For instance, that which is hot <note>can become cold</note>, because he who is healthy can certainly become sick, and the sick man can become healthy, and the hot can become cold, and the virtuous man can become base, and from the base man, a virtuous one can be made. 

For the base man, having been led toward better observations <note>or reflections</note>, might take at least a small step in growth. And if he once takes a small growth, it is evident that he will either be completely transformed or will take a very great growth. For he always becomes more easily moved toward virtue. And if various growths have been taken in reason, so that it is more likely for growth to occur, this always happening, he will eventually be restored to the opposite possession <note>habit</note> perfectly, unless he is hindered by time. 

But regarding possession and privation, it is impossible for a change into one another to occur. For change occurs from possession to privation, but from privation to possession, change is impossible. For no one who has become blind has ever seen again, nor has any bald man become long-haired, nor has anyone ever regrown teeth once lost.

<summary>This page discusses the Aristotelian logic of opposites, distinguishing between "contraries" (which can change into one another, like health and sickness) and "possession and privation" (which are irreversible, like sight and blindness).</summary>

# Eighth Section

As for those things which are opposed as affirmation and negation, it is evident that they are not opposed according to any of the previously mentioned modes; for in their case alone is it necessary that one of them always be true and the other false. For it is not necessary in the case of contraries <note>original: "ներհակացն" (nerhakats’n) - things that are diametrically opposed but can both be false, like 'black' and 'white'</note> that one be true and the other false; nor is it so in the case of others, nor in the case of possession and privation. For he who says "health" and "sickness" <note>original: "առողջութի և զհիւանդութի"</note> does not produce a statement, neither true nor false. Likewise, "double" and "half"; for these are opposed as relatives, and none of them is either true or false. Nor is it so according to privation and possession; for "fruitfulness" and "blindness," and generally all those things which are spoken of without any composition <note>term: composition (շարամանութե) - the logical joining of a subject and a predicate to form a complete sentence</note>, are neither true nor false. Indeed, the things mentioned are spoken of without composition. But nevertheless, this [truth or falsehood] might seem most likely to occur in the case of contraries spoken of according to composition. For "Socrates is well" is the contrary form of "Socrates is sick." But even in these cases, it is not always necessary for one to be true and the other false. For if Socrates exists, one will be true and one false; but if he does not exist, both are false. For neither "Socrates is sick"

<column-break/>

nor "Socrates is well" is true if he does not exist at all. But in the case of possession and privation, whether he exists or does not exist, it is not the case that one is true and one false. For "Socrates has sight" and "Socrates is blind" are opposed as privation and possession; and if he exists, it is not necessary for one to be true or false; for when he is not yet naturally able to have [sight], both are false. And if Socrates does not exist at all, then both are likewise false: both that "he has sight" and that "he is blind." But in the case of affirmation and negation, whether he exists or does not exist, one will always be true and the other false. For in "Socrates is sick" or "Socrates is not sick," it is evident that one is true or false. And if he does not exist, <margin>un-</margin> <note>The marginal note "ան" (an-) likely completes a word or indicates a negation/exception</note> it is similar; for "he is sick," if he does not exist, is false; but "he is not sick" is true. Thus, it is unique to these alone—those which are opposed as affirmation and negation—that one of them is always true or false.

### Ninth Section

The contrary of a good thing is necessarily an evil thing; and these are revealed according to their respective inductions <note>term: induction (մակածութե) - the process of deriving a general principle from specific examples</note>. For example, health and sickness; justice and injustice; and courage and cowardice. Similarly also in the case of others. But

### Concerning Motion

<image-desc>Decorative vertical floral and foliate ornament in black ink located in the left margin, marking the beginning of the section on motion.</image-desc>
There are six types of motion: generation <note>coming-into-being</note>, corruption <note>decay or passing away</note>, increase, decrease, change of place <note>locomotion</note>, and alteration <note>change in quality</note>. Now, these motions are clearly distinct from one another, for generation is not corruption, and increase is not decrease. Nor is change of place the same as the others. However, regarding alteration itself, it might seem necessary for it to be the same as some of the other motions. But this is not true. For it happens that we undergo alteration almost according to our affections <note>states of being or "accidents"</note>, or according to things that grow. For none of the other motions are common to it; it is not necessary for that which moves according to an affection to increase or decrease. This applies to the others as well; therefore, alteration must be different from the other motions. For if it were the same, it would be necessary for an alteration to immediately result in increase or decrease, or some other

---

<page-num>117</page-num>

motion to follow; but this is not necessary. Likewise, that which increases, or moves with some other motion, would have to undergo alteration. Yet there are some things that increase which do not undergo alteration. For example, the square <note>original: "փարագերեցելոյ" - likely referring to a geometric figure or gnomon</note> increases when a shape is added to it, but it does not become anything different in quality; the same applies to other such things. Therefore, the motions must be distinct from one another.

### Second Chapter

It is well said that, generally speaking, rest is the opposite of motion. But specifically: corruption is the opposite of generation, and decrease is the opposite of increase. For change of place, the opposite is rest in a place. Furthermore, it appears there is an opposition, perhaps, in the change to an opposite place; for example, downward motion is the opposite of upward motion, and upward is the opposite of downward. But regarding alteration, among the explained motions, it is not easy to explain what its actual opposite is. It appears there is nothing that is its own opposite, unless one were to set rest in quality against the change of that quality. And as with change of place, there is rest in place, or change to an opposite place. Since alteration is change according to quality, then it must be opposed according to

### On Having

<note>This section corresponds to Aristotle's "Categories," Chapter 15, discussing the various senses of the verb "to have" (Greek: ἔχειν, ekhein).</note>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap Armenian letter 'Ո' (U) featuring two stylized birds with interlaced necks and tails, colored with red and black ink.</image-desc> "To have" is spoken of in many ways:
Either as a <term>habit</term> <note>original: ունակութիւն (unakut’iun); a stable disposition</note> and <term>disposition</term> <note>original: տրամադրութիւն (tramadrut’iun); a temporary state</note>, or according to some other quality; for we say one "has" a certain knowledge or virtue. Or as a quantity, such as the size someone happens to have; for one is said to "have" a height of three cubits or four cubits. Or as things around the body, such as a cloak or a tunic. Or as on a part of the body, such as a ring on the hand. Or as a part of the body itself, such as "having" a hand or a foot. Or as in a container, such as the measure <note>original: կեղնն (keghnn); likely referring to a dry measure or husk</note> "has" the wheat, and the jar "has" the wine; for the jar contains the wine and the measure contains the wheat. And all these are said to "have." Also, as a possession, for we say one "has" a house or a field. One is also said to "have" a wife, and a wife a husband. But this last sense of "having" just mentioned seems the most remote; for by "having" a wife we signify nothing other than that he is <insert>cohabiting</insert> <note>literally: "dwelling together"</note> with her. Perhaps there may appear to be other ways of "having" as well, but those usually spoken of have almost all been enumerated and set in order here.

But nevertheless, no one of your simple names holds it thus, and that which is introduced is as the second to itself.
Together, the Second.

In the one, there is no perfection; for by nature it is stubborn, nothing without, even if it is useful according to nature.

<meta>This page continues a philosophical and linguistic treatise, likely a translation or commentary on Aristotle's "De Interpretatione" (On Interpretation). The text focuses on the nature of propositions, declarations, and the logical relationship between affirmation and negation.</meta>

# Section 3: The Third

A declarative statement <note>original: "բաց երեւակաւն" (bats erevakuyn) - likely a translation of the Greek 'apophantikos logos', referring to a statement that can be true or false</note> is not simply any utterance of truth, nor is it merely its own expression; rather, it is a sign that possesses life <note>meaning it conveys a complete thought</note>, yet it is not inherently true or false in isolation, but rather something else entirely. For the tools of rhetoric <note>original: "ճարտասանու"</note> or grammar <note>original: "քերականու"</note> define these distinctions. But regarding declarative statements, they belong to a specific genus. Whether a single declarative statement is a distinct expression or a complex explanation, others are made one through a conjunction. However, it is necessary that every declarative statement proceeds from an external source <note>referring to the external reality the statement describes</note>. Or from those who speak; the sign is not a single human word unless it signifies something, or something of this sort is applied to the declarative statement.

And it is not just one thing, but many: "animal," "two-footed," "tame." For if these are not arranged into one, they do not become a single statement; but this is the function of another <note>logic or syntax</note>, to say:

### Section 3 (Continued)

Furthermore, a single declarative statement is either that which reveals something clearly or that which is joined by a conjunction. Whereas "many" are those which reveal many things and not one, or are without conjunctions. Now, the genus or the verb—if it exists alone—reveals something about another, so that when it is declared, or asked by someone, it does not simply show each thing clearly. A declarative statement is that which serves a certain purpose or reveals something specific. But the conjunction joins that statement to its own intellectual meaning.

<page-num>121</page-num>

# Section 2: The Second

Furthermore, a simple declaration is a signifying sound <note>original: "ձայն նշաննեան" - a translation of the Aristotelian 'phone semantike'</note> regarding what is or what is not, without being entirely divided. Here, if it is a declaration, it enters into someone's mind. A negation <note>original: "բացատումն" (batsatumn) - used here for 'apophasis'</note> is a declaration that someone is deprived of something. First, because the two occurrences are like the primary principles <note>original: "իշխանն" (ishkhann) - literally 'prince' or 'ruler', used here for 'archai' or primary elements</note>; the occurrences are simpler now, all-encompassing. That which is present is not deprived of the underlying things. Therefore, it is clear that everything is an affirmation <note>original: "ստորասամի" (storasami) - likely 'kataphasis'</note>. A negation is the opposite sign to an affirmation. If there is a contradiction <note>original: "հակառումն"</note>, it is thus: an affirmation and a negation are opposed. I call "opposing" the awakening of this essence; but not in a general sense. The other occurrences shall be simplified, while the distinction belongs to the intellectual difficulty.

# Section 4: The Fourth

Furthermore, there are those things which are universal <note>original: "ըստ բոլորէ" - literally 'according to the whole'</note> and those which are particular <note>original: "ըստ իւրաքանչիւր իւրոյն" - literally 'according to each its own'</note>. Universal things are those which are predicated <note>term: Predicated <note>To assert or declare something about a subject</note></note> of many, and the particular is that which is a man according to the whole <note>referring to an individual instance of a species</note>. But the harm <note>likely a scribal error or specific logical term for 'negation of property'</note> remains according to each individual. The elements of the declaration are for each person.

### Chapter Twenty-Two

Regarding these distinctions, we must inquire
how affirmations <term>affirmations: <note>original: "storasut'iwnk'"</note> positive statements</term> and negations <term>negations: <note>original: "bac'asut'iwnk'"</note> negative statements</term>
stand toward one another regarding being possible <term>possible: <note>original: "karogh linel"</note></term> and not being possible,
and regarding the contingent <term>contingent: <note>original: "ner-əndunakan"</note> that which is admitted or possible</term>, and the non-contingent;
and concerning the impossible and the necessary. For
this contains certain perplexities. For if,
among those things that are combined, those
contradictions <term>contradictions: <note>original: "hakasut'iwnk'"</note></term> are opposed which are judged according to "to be" or
"not to be"—for example, the negation of "to be a white man"
is "not to be a white man," but not "to be a non-white man." For if of everything there is
<margin>either negation</margin> or affirmation, it would be true to say of a piece of wood
that it is a "non-white man," which is absurd. And if
this is how contradiction is to be made, I say
that wherever "to be" is not added, the same should be done
instead of the word "to be." For example, of the
affirmation "the man walks," the negation is
not "the non-man walks," but "the man does not walk." For
it makes no difference to say "the man walks" or "the man is walking." So then, if this is the case
everywhere, the negation of "to be able to be" <note>Aristotle is discussing the placement of the negative particle in modal logic.</note> will be "to be able not to be," but rather "not to be able to be."

### Chapter Twenty-Three

But it seems that the same order follows "to be able not to be."

<column-break/>

For everything that is able can either be cut or walk, and
can also not walk and not be cut. And since everything
that is thus possible does not always act, then
the negation of this will follow. For "to be able
not to walk" belongs to "to be able to walk," and "not to see" to "to be able to
see." But yet, it is impossible to truly affirm
opposites of the same thing. Therefore,
this is not the negation. For it happens
that we either affirm or deny the same thing at once; or that "to be"
and "not to be" are added as extras, not being the affirmation
and negation themselves. For if this is the case,
it would be chosen. Therefore, the
negation of "to be able to be" is "not to be able to be,"
but not "to be able not to be." And the same reasoning applies to the
contingent to be; for its negation
is "not to be contingent." And it is the same
for others, such as the impossible and the
necessary. For it happens as in those cases where "to be"
and "not to be" are additions. While the subjects are
"white" and "man," here "to be" and "not to be"
become as the subject, while "to be able" and "to be contingent" are the additions,
determining—as in those other cases—the truth and the
falsehood. Similarly, these apply to "to be able to be" and "not to be able to be."

### Chapter Twenty-Four

But of "to be able to be," the negation is "not to be able"

It may not be that the order is established such that the crime <note>original: եղեռնուի; referring to a logical error or a "sin" in reasoning</note> of counting does not fail. For the same thing can both be and not be. For contradictions do not fail for the one who repents <note>The scribe uses "repentant" (ապաշխարողս) metaphorically for one correcting a logical premise</note>; it can be and it can not be. But it is not that it "cannot not be," and what can you do to make it possible not to act? Never is this true of failing itself, whether they are opposites or not; that is "impossible to be" and "not possible not to be," just as in the case of becoming a child. And likewise, the "non-potential" might be a negation, not "necessary not to be," but "not necessary to be."

But the "necessitating" would not be "not necessary not to be," and the "impossible" would not be "not impossible not to be," but "not impossible to be." But the "impossible to be" is not "not impossible not to be," brothers <note>The address "brothers" suggests this text was used in a monastic or scholastic setting</note>; as it has been said, "to be sensible" and "to be" must always be placed as the subjects.

But regarding the assertion and the negation: here, concerning "to be" and "will be," it is a composition. And the classes of the square <note>term: square (դասս... հակակայս); referring to the Logical Square of Opposition</note> of opposites, with assertion and negation:
* Possible, not possible (in man, not man)
* Impossible, not impossible
* Necessary, not necessary
* True, not true

And the consequences of what has been done shall be thus in pairs. For what is "thought to be" is "able to be," and I say this is the co-ordinate. The same is "impossible to be," and so on, the contrary is "not to be." But the "possible to have" is not to be; to him, "to have in the ear" <note>likely a corruption or specific idiom for "perceiving"</note> is not to be, not necessary not to be, and also impossible not to be. But the "not possible to be" is also "impossible to be," and also "possible to be," and "not necessary to be," and also "not thought to be," and also "the receptive." Not "not to be," but "possible not to be," and "necessary to be"—let it not be seen to fall into this distortion.

Possible not to be; possible to be.
Not able not to be; able not to be.
Able not to be; able not to be. <note>The repetition reflects the scribe's attempt to map out all modal permutations</note>
Impossible not [to be]; impossible not to be.
Impossible not to be; impossible not to be.
Not impossible not to be.
Necessary to be; not necessary.
Not necessary not to be; not necessary not to be.

By which [it is] more and clear. In two.
The possible; then the impossible. Possible and also capable. And not capable and not possible, it follows not by contradiction but contrariwise. For "to be capable" is the negation "not possible to be," while the negation is the assertion. "To be impossible" is "not to be impossible," for it is an assertion. "Not to be impossible" is the "possible" negation. But the "necessary"—how is it intolerable? It is evident that it is not so, but follows the opposites; this is the contrary, because the negation "not necessary" is not "to be," because it is able [to be] true.

# On the Logic of Necessity and Possibility

on the same two. For it is not necessary <note>original: "հարկաւոր" - referring to logical necessity</note> to be, and not necessary to be. And the reasons do not follow similarly to the others; because in a contrary manner, the impossible is denied by the necessary. The same applies to the possible. For if it is impossible for this not to be, then it is necessary for it to be. And if it is impossible for this to be, then it is necessary for it not to be. But then, if those are similar, the "possible" and these others are contraries. For "necessary" and "impossible" do not signify the same thing, but as it has been said, they are related inversely.
<margin>Lecture Twenty-Six.</margin>

|| In the same way that the "possible" is held, so too should we hold the contradiction of the "necessary." For if it is necessary to be, it is possible to be. For if it were not, the negation would follow; for it is necessary either to affirm or to deny. But then, if it is not possible to be, it is impossible to be. Therefore, it would be that "it is impossible to be" follows from "it is necessary to be," which is absurd <note>literally "out of place" or "illogical"</note>. However, from "it is possible to be," it does not follow in order that "it is not necessary to be." For then it would happen that "it is necessary to be" implies "it is not necessary to be," which is absurd. Yet, from "it is necessary to be," it does not follow that "it is possible to be" and "not necessary not to be." For both can happen to the former, but for the latter, if it is true, the other will no longer be true. For both can be possible to be

<column-break/>

and not to be. And if it is necessary to be and not to be, it will not be possible for both; it is lacking. Henceforth, "not necessary not to be" does not follow from "possible to be." For this is true also of "necessary to be," because there would be a contradiction in the following: "not possible not to be." But that follows from "not possible to be," namely "necessary not to be," the negation of which is "not necessary not to be." And therefore, other contradictions follow according to the stated mode. And nothing impossible happens to things that occur in that way.

<margin>Lecture Twenty-Seven.</margin>
But someone might doubt whether "it is possible to be" follows from "it is necessary to be." For if it does not follow, the contradiction would follow: "it is not possible to be." And if someone were to say this is the case, yet call the contradiction of the necessary "possible not to be," then both are false regarding the "necessary to be." But yet again, the same thing seems to be able to be established and not established; to be and not to be. Then it would be that even for the "necessary to be," it is possible to be <note>meaning "merely possible"</note>, and this is false. And it is evident that not everything that is possible to be can also be its opposite. But in some cases, it is not true. First, regarding those powers <note>term: <term>զօրութիւնք (powers/potencies)</term> - refers to the Aristotelian concept of 'dynamis' or the capacity of a thing to act or be acted upon</note> that are not according to reason, such as that which is heated without fire, and possesses an irrational power. For all those powers that are accompanied by reason...
