### Section Twenty-Eight

But some powers are homonymous <note>terms that sound the same but have different meanings</note>. For "the possible" is not spoken of loosely; but one is true as an actuality <note>original: ներգործութիւն (energeia/actuality)</note>, such as it is "possible" for one to walk because he is walking, and generally for it to be possible to be. And another is what is set as a potential according to actuality; what sort of thing is possible to be, and only when it might act. Such as it is "possible" for one to walk when he might walk. And this [belongs] to the state of mobile things alone, while the [other] power belongs to the state of immobile things. And in both cases, it is true to say it is "possible" for the walking or the being to occur; for behold, he still walks, both the actuality and the capacity to walk. Now, in this way, "possibles" are not truly said of the "necessary" simply; but only truly in a sense. For the part follows the whole, and that follows from necessity, to which alone it follows to be "possible." But however, not entirely. Perhaps the beginning, the necessary and the non-necessary, is for all things to be or not to be. And

the others must be sought and requested in order. It is evident from what has been said, that in the necessary, the being is according to actuality. If the first and most glorious is actuality, it is prior to power. And those which are always in actuality without powers, such as the first substances. But those which are with power are first by nature, but later in time; and some are never in actuality, but in power alone.

<meta>This page continues a philosophical or dialectical treatise, likely a translation of a Greek Neoplatonic or Aristotelian commentary (possibly Proclus or Porphyry), focusing on the nature of "the present" or "the immediate" (energeia/presentness) in relation to the Good and the True.</meta>

->The true will of the heart is in the present.<- Now, there are seven oppositions that pass through those who wish for the opposite to exist; and it is a particular search. There is a certain opinion <note>original: "kartsik" - can mean opinion, thought, or hypothesis</note> of the true good that is not good, and another that is not "not-good-named." And if there are no non-goods of the good among these, they belong to the present truth. And if there is one, according to which it is held: the present.

Only thirty-two.

2. That these opinions concern the one present thing.

— The opinions: to distinguish the present things from the false <note>original: "stuts" - falsehood or vanity</note>. For the non-good of the good, and the non-evil of the good; the others are in appearance and true. If it is uncultivated <note>original: "ankert'i" - unrefined or raw</note>, it is one. These are the non-present; not that the non-present is the present, but the false is the present-seeming. And if there are no non-goods of the good, it is a sermon of opinion. With the not not-good, and the other not not-certainly to arrange the habitable; of the others, not one of the matter—nowhere do they make the "whatness" seem to be. And not in any way do they make the "nothing" seem, for both are not un-wonderful; and by quantifying, they make the "what" seem; and in no way the "what," but the world-quantities—these are the affliction of those things which have come into being.

And then also the anchor.

Only 8 of the thirty-two.

2. Not as if good, and good, not evils.

— The will according to itself, and everything according to the cause of the truth, does not cause [evil] to this; not by evil.

<page-num>134</page-num>

More accurately and more specifically, each true will is either by itself or according to a cause. And if it is not true—that which is not good—and for the good, coming out by itself is false. But for the evil, it is according to a cause and not true. Wherein the same falsehood is not an opposition to the good, but rather to the present. For it is not for the sophistry <note>original: "imastakanut'iun" - often used for sophistical reasoning</note> which, by holding the present, has the opinion; rather the non-present is among the many things differed toward itself. Separately, if the present of these should unite, and the false of these present things—if in this the present should not be, and that if the evil is continued from the good; and rather than non-good, evils are in two opinions.

Only thirty-two.

2. (If the understood thing—of the others—truly: it is necessary to have these; they might seem to be goods, not love; for there is not one opposition to this anywhere, or anywhere that which is not present has emerged. If in other words there is nothing present—of such things—there is a false [thing] to the truth, an opposing memory, which men do not think of as the only un-worded thing; its present is very shadow-beautiful, and the other is not an opposition.

Only 8 of the thirty-two.

2. It possesses impartially and each of the non-good; of the non-good, the non-evil, and not by evil.

<meta>This page continues the cosmological discussion from the previous section, specifically defining the structure of the universe (the World) and the celestial spheres. The catchword from the previous page, "Of the World," connects directly to the opening definitions here.</meta>

accessible to us. And we shall speak divinely <note>original: ածաբանեսցուք (adsabanests’uk’); to speak theologically or in a manner befitting the divine</note> of the Creator <note>original: աւազանորին (avazanorn); likely a reference to the Architect or Author of the universe</note> and those things with Him: every individual nature, position, and motion. For I believe it is truly fitting for you, a noble member of the royal court, to know the history concerning these things, and to consider this philosophy no small matter; rather, by such means, to succeed the most valiant.

Now, the World <term>World: <note>original: աշխարհ (ashkharh); here meaning the Cosmos or the totality of the universe</note></term> is a composition of heaven and earth, and the natures contained within them. Thus, the "World" is said to be the orderly arrangement of all things, and its preservation is through all things and by means of those things within it. While the life-bearing earth remains motionless and stable, it has happened to become the home for all kinds of living creatures.

The highest part of this, entirely and in every way complete, is called the Heaven <note>original: երկին (erkin)</note> in the heights. It is full of divine bodies, which we are accustomed to call stars. It moves with the eternal motion of the heaven in a single revolution; and those elements that are brought along with it are perpetual companions for eternity. The whole world is with it, for the heaven is spherical in form and moves, as I said, as a single ruler.

Two necessary points, opposite one another,

<column-break/>

are the motionless poles, like the axis around which the whole revolves. Upon these, the entire circle is fixed and turns, and all things are mixed and established with proportion. Now, the world is turned in a circle, and that which is called heaven—which we understand to be similar—some call the "tent" <note>original: տունակ (tunak); a metaphorical term for the celestial vault</note>; it is the straight measure of the world. The sphere that revolves around it holds the earth in the middle of the heavens, and the horizons are placed as the second boundary.

<margin>1</margin> However, of these two motionless points, one is always visible above, being at the summit according to the northern region, and is called "Northern." The other is always hidden beneath the earth according to the southern region, and is called "Sub-Northern" <note>meaning the Antarctic or Southern pole</note>.

Now, as for the substance of the heaven and the stars, we call it "Essence" <note>original: էութիւն (eut’yun); likely referring to the Aether or fifth element</note>. It is not as some have said, that because it appears like fire, it is of a burning nature. They are mistaken in many things, for its power is free from fire; rather, because it is always running in a circular motion, it is an element distinct from the four <note>the four terrestrial elements: earth, water, air, fire</note>, incorruptible and divine.

But among the contained stars, the "unwandering" ones <note>the fixed stars</note> revolve together with the heaven itself, having their stability in the same place. In the midst of these, the so-called "Zodiac" <note>original: կենդանատեսակքն (kendanatesak’n); literally "animal-shaped" or "living-forms"</note> circle [revolves] along the side...

Through the rotation, they are seen in part, and the **twelve zodiac signs** <note>original: "երկոտասան կենդանիքն" (the twelve living beings/animals)</note> are divided into regions.

But the **planets** <note>original: "մոլորք" (wanderers)</note> are not naturally disposed to move in unison with the former [the fixed stars], nor with each other; rather, they travel in different regions according to their own laws. It is as if one were near the earth and another higher up.

While the multitude of the **fixed stars** <note>original: "ան մոլորիցն" (the non-wanderers)</note> is known to men—even though they appear to move in the likeness of the heavens on the first surface—the planets are divided into seven primary parts. These remain in order in such circles, as if the higher one were always larger than the lower. These seven are contained and encompassed by the sphere of the planets, always maintaining their position in this way:

The highest is **Saturn** <note>original: "Երևակն" (Erevak); also called "Cronus" in Greek</note>, also called the circle of **Cronus**. which is bounded as far as the earth.
Now, the **ether** <note>original: "եթերն"</note> encompasses these divine bodies. After the ethereal and divine nature—which we define as always ordered, immutable, unalterable, and impassible—comes that which is entirely subject to change and is called **corruptible** and **mortal**. 

<margin>N.</margin>
The first element of this [mortal realm] is the unstable and fire-like substance, ignited by the ethereal nature due to its great size and the swiftness of its motion. This is called the **fiery and corruptible element**. From it, rays are emitted, and flames leap out, and "spears" <note>meteorological phenomena like streaks of light</note> and "pits" and **comets** <note>original: "գիտաւոր" (the 'long-haired' ones)</note> are established, and often extinguished.

Next in order is the **air**, which is poured out, being equal [in its distribution] and of a cold nature. But by shining together with the upper region and being ignited, it becomes bright and most beautiful. Yet, while it has a fiery power, it is also the source of all changes, where clouds are composed, rains flow, and likewise snows, frosts, and hail; the breath of winds, the thundering of clouds, crashes of thunder, and flashes of lightning, and unceasing wonders.

**A**nd from this airy nature, the sea and land were constructed, enriched with plants and animals, springs and rivers; some spreading across the land and others bursting forth into the sea. ->**islands in the sea and the sea upon the dry land.**<-
**N**ow, common reason knows how to divide the boundaries of nature into islands and mainlands, recognizing that the whole world, along with the islands, is encircled by the sea called the "Abyssal" <note>original: "անդընդական" (andndakan) - referring to the deep or primordial ocean</note>. It is perhaps fitting to think that many other islands may be situated opposite this one on the other side—some larger than this and some smaller. But to us, they are invisible and like a dream, for we only know the islands that we have encountered within these deep seas. The same must hold true for the universal Abyssal sea, for there are many islands in that same sea, and the large ones among them are encircled by great rings of water, including this world at its edges. For the linear nature of the waters, by flowing into very great seas and gathering into themselves, revealed the so-called inhabited world <note>term: "տիեզերք" (tiezerk) - the ecumene or known inhabited world</note>. And it may be that the water is ordered alongside the airy nature, and after that, toward the firmament, the whole earth was constructed as a single material site; and having been established by its composition, it stands and remains immovable. And this is the whole of the world which we call the lower realms. But these foundations are contained within a spherical place, and the smaller is always held by the larger, as I am saying here: the <gloss>entire</gloss> universe is made of water, air, and fire—from the fiery nature by which this whole world was composed.
<margin>the whole</margin> And the word showed the place of the gods <margin>of God</margin> as a dwelling, and the lower parts for ephemeral living beings. <meta>This page continues the natural philosophical discussion of atmospheric phenomena, likely following a section on the formation of clouds or the nature of the upper atmosphere. The moisture of birth <note>original: "ծննդական ջրոյ" - likely referring to the generative or elemental nature of water</note> is buried and then rained down from the shaking of a cloud, especially one that has become quite thick. It possesses a seasonal entry <margin>and</margin> according to its density or lack thereof; for being vaporous, softer drops are sown. And when it is more forceful, it becomes harder. But freezing occurs in the place of the so-called falling, where the drops are larger than rain and are brought to the earth by frequent shaking. And snow occurs from the breaking of a thickened cloud; the water, previously intense, undergoes a change and a cutting, and is made into a foam-like and very white cutting. And being constructed from the cold, the moisture is held so as not to flow but remains separated; for which reason, when such is frequently brought down, it is called "snow-bearing."

But hail occurs according to the snow-bearing process, turning from a state of invisible wetness into a firm and harsh delivery. For the density of things is torn and broken; and according to the measure of the density, so is the size. And <margin>the</margin> movements are made more violent. Now these things are naturally disposed to fall outward from the moisture. But the dry part, being driven out into the cold from the flowing, becomes wind; for this is nothing else but a great deal of air flowing forcefully and continuously, which is also called breath.

<column-break/>

But there is also something else called <gloss>ed</gloss> breath <note>The gloss "եց" likely completes a verb or suffix</note> which arises in mixtures and in living creatures, which is breathed and generative through penetration; concerning which it is not necessary to speak at present. But those breaths which breathe through the ether in their own way, we call winds; while those born beneath the earth, it brings forth as exhalations. But of the airs that are breathed in front of the earth, they are called "earthly." And those which are intensified in the gulfs of the sea are "sea-winds," to which those breathed from rivers and from ship-distributing <note>likely referring to harbors or estuaries</note> waters have some similarity. And those which, from the tearing of a cloud in its upward striving, take into themselves the density of the cloud, are called "weavings" <note>original: "հիւս" - perhaps referring to vortexes or wisps</note>. And those which are suddenly torn along with water are called "watery." And those which arise from the east are continuous and are called *Euros* <note>The East Wind</note>. *Boreas* are those which breathe from the north. *Zephyrs* are those from the setting of the sun. And *Notos* are those from the midday <note>The South Wind</note>. But however, of the *Euros*, that which breathes from the rising sun in the summer season is called *Kekias* <note>The North-East wind</note>. And that which is at the peak of the solar day is called *Apeliotes* <note>The East wind</note>. And that which breathes from the east in winter is called *Euros*. And the opposite of these are said to be the *Zephyrs*. And it is called *Argestes* <note>The North-West wind</note> which...

<meta>This page continues a classical or medieval cosmological discussion regarding the classification of winds and atmospheric phenomena, likely following a section on the cardinal directions. The terminology reflects a synthesis of Aristotelian meteorology translated into Armenian.</meta>

In the summer, the wind blows from the west, which we call *Olympitas* <note>original: ղոմպիտաս; likely a corruption of the Greek 'Olympias'</note>; others call it *Apiga*. And the wind that rises from the west at midday is called *Zephyros* <note>The West Wind</note>. The one that rises from the west in the winter is called *Lips* <note>The Southwest Wind</note>. Likewise, among the northern winds, the one that rises near *Ikykias* <note>Caecias or the Northeast wind</note> is specifically called *Vornas*. 

There are also certain *aspars* <note>technical term for gusts or wind-types</note> which are sounded from the constellation of the Wain <note>The Great Bear/Ursa Major</note> itself; and from the northern meridian, the wind called *Thraskias* <note>The North-Northwest wind</note> arises, which is adjacent to *Argestes* <note>The Northwest wind</note>, though some call it *Ekekios*. 

Regarding the southern winds: those that are entirely circular are brought forth by the *Hanamartu Aparotioy*. One is named *Nastos* <note>Notos, the South Wind</note>, and another is called the North-*Nastos*. The wind that rises between *Lips* and *Nastos* is called by some *Liphoynoyni*. And *Euronastos* is the name given to the wind that rises between *Euros* <note>The Southeast wind</note> and *Nastos*.

Furthermore, some winds are "straight-blowing," breathing directly forward. Others are "whirling-breathers," such as the aforementioned *Kekias*. Some are powerful in the winter, like the *Nastos* winds, and others in the summer, which are called "annual" <note>Etesian winds</note>, having a mixture of the *aspars* of the tropics and the *Zephyros* winds. Others are called southern winds, being springtime winds, and they themselves are northern-types. 

But the most violent of winds is called the "furious one," which is the storm-wind <note>original: մրրիկ (mrrik)</note> that strikes suddenly from above. 

<margin>L</margin>
And *Daphs* <note>possibly 'taps', heat or pressure</note> is a firmament-force; the wind tears from the inside upward, a blowing rising from the earth, a wind from certain depths; or it is a rising from a rupture when it has become violently intense. 

<margin>H</margin>
Then there is the *Prester* <note>Greek: 'fiery whirlwind'</note>, which is called "the Scorcher." This is a wind entangled within a moist and very thick cloud. It tears through the continuous edges of the cloud itself, creating a parching hiss and a roar. 

And thunder is distributed in this way: a certain wind, driven with great force, enters the middle of a cloud and is trapped there on all sides. Struggling to escape, it churns in every direction and heats the cloud according to the rupture it makes. This creates the flash of lightning, from which the completion of thunder arises. Although the sound is heard later, delayed by the air, it is inherent to the event. The flash is seen from a distance, while the thunder is heard only when it reaches the ears. Therefore, the appearance of the fire has a prior occurrence to our perception of the fact, but the thunder is heard only when it strikes the hearing. 

Furthermore, the lightning-fire, which by the violence of the cloud-mass pours down to the earth, is called the "half-beam." Then—

<meta>This page continues a natural philosophy discourse, likely from a translation of Pseudo-Aristotle's *De Mundo* (On the Cosmos), focusing on meteorological and celestial phenomena.</meta>

If it becomes inflamed and takes on fire, striking the clouds with force, it is called *prester* <note>original: "պրեստերկոչի" (prester-kochi); a Greek term for a fiery whirlwind or waterspout</note>, which means "scorcher." But if it is entirely without fire, it is called *typhon* <note>original: "տիւփոն"; a hurricane or gale</note>, which translates as "burner." And when each of these strikes and reaches the earth, it is named *skiptos* <note>original: "սկիպտոս"; a thunderbolt or lightning strike</note>.

Furthermore, among such cloud-disturbances, those that bring ash-like dust are called *psolus* <note>original: "փօլուր" (likely a corruption of psolus); soot or smoky flame</note>. Those that pass quickly in flight are called *argestes* <note>original: "արգիստես"; a swift-moving wind</note>. And <margin>there are</margin> *leikes* <note>likely "leikai," bright flashes</note>, those which are brought forth like lines; these are *skiptoi* that perform their action by cutting. Taken together, these are all appearances that occur in the air. For some are merely optical appearances, while others have a physical substance.

The optical appearances include *irides* <note>rainbows</note>, which are bows and rods and such things. Those with substance include *selas* <note>original: "սէլք"; Greek for bright flashes or auroras</note>, which are beams and comets; these are "flights" and "fixed points," which are the comets and those similar to them.

Now, the *halos* <note>original: "ողնիբիան" (a corruption of the Greek 'halos')</note> is an appearance caused by the refraction of the sun or the moon in a moist or deep cloud; it continues as an appearance, seen as if in a mirror according to a complete circle. But the *rhabdos* <note>original: "րոդոսքն"</note> is a straight appearance, which translates as "rod." And the *halo* is a brightness of the

<column-break/>

stars that is spread out in three variations. And the *halo* occurs around the three, appearing with the sun and the moon. But the "boundless" one, as it is said, occurs around all the stars. And there are other *selas* <note>flashes</note>, which are many-flamed burnings in the air. Of these *selas*, some are fleeting and some are fixed.

Now, the "leaping" of fire is a phenomenon caused by the proximity of the elements, passing quickly through the air; it has a double appearance by means of its speed. But the "fixed" one is a long-stretched extension without motion. In the likeness of a moist nature, it widens on both sides, which is then called a "comet" <note>original: "գիսաւոր" (gisavor); literally 'long-haired'</note>. Yet many times, some of these *selas* are lasting for a long time, and others are quickly extinguished.

There are many other appearing types called "torches" <note>original: "դամբառք"</note>, and "vessels," and "pits," and "thunderings," according to their likeness to these things. These are seen in two ways: as "half-moons" and "jars." However, all such things are unstable, for none of them are ever recorded as being permanently visible. Such, then, are the atmospheric things.

Concerning those things which the earth contains, there is much...

<meta>This page continues a natural philosophical discussion regarding the elements of the earth, specifically focusing on the subterranean origins of fire, water, and wind, and the classification of earthquakes.</meta>

<margin>90</margin> in their own way within themselves; just as there are springs of water, so too are there springs of fire and wind. Of these, some are invisible beneath the earth, yet they possess many vents for breathing and blowing upward—such as Lipari and Etna <note>original: "Lipara" and "Etna," referring to the famous volcanic islands and mountains in the Mediterranean</note>, which is the island of Sicily. And there are others that frequently pour out fire like a river, and because of this, they carry up material along with the fire. Others, being beneath the earth near spring waters, heat them; some they bring up from the depths as lukewarm, some as intensely boiling, and some as well-tempered <note>meaning pleasantly warm or therapeutic</note>. Likewise, the wind has many open sources in the earth, from which some emit a resinous vapor <note>original: "giwt's," likely referring to bitumen or naphtha</note>. Others provide a kind of nourishment, and some provide oracular commands <note>original: "hramanatrut'i," referring to divine or prophetic utterances</note>, such as those at the shrines and at Lebadeia <note>The site of the subterranean oracle of Trophonius in Greece</note>. And others kill entirely, such as the one in Phrygia <note>likely the Plutonium at Hierapolis, a cave known for its lethal carbon dioxide vapors</note>. Frequently, a wind related to the lower regions, having come out from its own place into the deepest foundation of the earth, causes its parts to tremble. Having passed through many hollows of the earth and being shut up within them, it shakes the earth. This force works within the earth to produce that which we are accustomed to call an **earthquake** <term>sharzhum: literally "movement" or "shaking"</term>. Furthermore, those earthquakes that shake from side to side at acute angles are called *epiklites* <note>Greek: "epiklintai," meaning "leaning" or "inclining" movements</note>, which

<column-break/>

translates as **"the inclinants."** And those that thrust upward from below at acute angles are called **"the boilers"** <term>errats'uts'ich'k: those that cause a seething or upward eruption</term>. And those that open the earth with a chasm are called **"the throwers"** <term>ynkets'uts'ich'k: those that cast down or submerge</term>; from these, some bring up winds, some rocks, some mud, and others reveal springs <margin>90</margin> that did not exist before. Some cause a crashing sound with a single thrust, which we call **"the thrusters."** Others cause trembling by inclining and then shaking again, always bringing back the part that was moved; these are called **"vibrators"** <note>original: "palmakanq," from the Greek "palmatiai," referring to a rhythmic, throbbing tremor</note>, similar to the shivering of a fever. Others are called **"roaring earthquakes,"** which shake the earth with a hollow sound. Furthermore, many roarings occur in the earth without a movement, whenever the wind is not sufficient to cause a shaking, but is instead cut off and swirled within itself. In this state, it becomes turbulent, fighting with violence, for the winds enter into those places where the moisture of the earth is united and mixed, becoming proportionate to them. And in the sea, it often gives way, and the waves surge upward, sometimes meeting resistance and sometimes only a thrust, as the historian relates.

->And now I shall speak of the whole.<-

<margin>2</margin> The ages are mixed with one another, joined and composed in knowledge, and from their body, the corruption through mixing becomes the generation of diverse things. And the whole is itself incorruptible and unchangeable by hand. Looking upon this, one is amazed at how this world was composed of opposing principles—that is, from four materials: from the cold and the heat. By previously mixing these, the multitudes of all things we encounter were formed from them.

<margin>10</margin> Again, it seems wonderful to someone how it stands firm, this driving of opposing kinds; I speak of the poor and the rich, the young <note>literally "unripe" or "inexperienced"</note> and the old, the strong and the weak, the good and the evil. Who does not know the civic harmony—

<column-break/>

—I mean the magnificent one? For one is perfected out of many and dissimilar things. And in its single disposition, nature and fortune contain all things. For nature also delights in the harmony of opposites. And nature delights in this, according to which it makes a knot <note>or "bond"</note> of characters, and not each of the same kind. According to this, it also joined the first harmony from opposites, and not from likes.

Art also taught me this: by imitating nature, it does the same. For the art of painting, having mixed white and black, yellow and red pigments, perfected an image similar to the aforementioned harmony.

And music, having mixed high and low notes, and simultaneously long and short sounds, perfected a single composition with diverse voices.

And grammar, having made a mixture of vowels and consonants, arranged the whole art from these.

According to what was said by Heraclitus <note>original: "հերակլիտոսէ"</note>: "You shall join with the wholes the things that are not whole," so that the harmonious and the discordant, being brought together, [become] one out of all and...

<summary>This page discusses the philosophical concept of universal harmony arising from the union of opposites, citing examples from meteorology, social classes, painting, music, and grammar. It concludes with a reference to the Pre-Socratic philosopher Heraclitus to validate the idea that diversity creates unity.</summary>

<keywords>harmony of opposites, four elements, Heraclitus, music theory, grammar, painting, natural philosophy, social order</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a cosmological discussion, likely from a late medieval Armenian translation of a Greek patristic text (resembling the style of Gregory of Nyssa or Pseudo-Aristotle's 'De Mundo'). It focuses on the harmony of opposing elements in the creation of the universe.</meta>

...of mine shall be all. Now, in this way, I say the composition of all things—of heaven and earth, and with them the world—was formed from the mixing of opposites and adorned through their joining: the slanted line <note>likely referring to the ecliptic or the zodiacal path</note> with the straight; and heat mixed with cold; heavy with light; the straight with the crooked. All the earth within its borders, the ether, the sun, the moon, and all the adornments of the heavens <note>original: "zardareants erkin," referring to the celestial bodies as the 'jewelry' of the sky</note>. A single power permeates through all, unmixed yet diverse, consisting of air, and of earth, and of fire, and of water; and in these, it brings the whole world into existence and encompasses it in a single sphere. For it mixes the harmony of opposites with one another, and from these, it provokes <note>or "governs"</note> the salvation <note>preservation</note> of all things.

**F**urthermore, the cause of these elements is harmony and a smooth marriage, where nothing is more powerful than another. For they possess an equal opposition: heavy against light, and heat against cold; teaching us that equality requires harmony and mutual aid. And by being fellow-combatants in harmony, they become the parents of the same whole—this most beautiful world. For what nature could be more beautiful than this? Its surname is "The Good and Orderly" <note>a reference to the Greek 'Kosmos', which implies both order and beauty</note>. And if one should wish to speak of its adornment with praise,

<column-break/>

perhaps from a small part he might be able to tell the tale. That is to say: the arrangement of the heavens; the motion of the stars; the rising of the sun; the movements measured with the most precise scale in every wisdom of all things. And what indeed is such truth? It is like the keeping of the generative times, the most beautiful revolution, the order of summer and winter, wherein the day and night complete the months; they define the year by the revolution of months—things which are supreme in honor, swiftest in motion, and glorious in brightness. By a power that is ageless and incorruptible, it <margin>?</margin> divided the natures of sea-creatures, of great whales, and of those in the air; and it measured out lives by its own movements. From its breath, everything is revealed and receives the breath of life. By this, the orders of its wondrous renewals are perfected; the winds joining together and casting upon the earth the cloud-cover from above, and the marvelous flows of the downpours. And through these, a measured moisture is granted to the earth, upon which the fiery [sun] kindles the life-giving plants. All is in harmony with the earth; and from here, there are raised up from the earth all kinds of leafy plants, irrigated by water and [nourished] by fire.

<summary>This page describes the cosmological harmony of the universe, explaining how opposing elements like heat and cold or heavy and light are balanced to create a stable and beautiful world. It highlights the orderly movements of celestial bodies and the natural cycles that sustain life on earth.</summary>

The **living beings** <note>original: կենդանացեալսն - those brought to life</note>, each according to their own kind, are entirely removed; those which, having become a genus of the completed whole, later emerge from the surrounding environment. Why is it without aging? Because its own nature surrounds it; furthermore, it is born through **knowledge** <note>or "recognition"</note> and is formed as a son, and with precision it bites and makes it flaming. To whom does this seem to be something? It also exists in the first whole, which does not turn toward desirable change, but possesses its own joy. It stands at the completion; for having moved upon the earth, the deviations of the winds occur directly in the end, as was said of the finishing. The earthly soul itself <margin>The soul's relation to the material world</margin> tends toward growth and corruption, and through understanding, the nature of the demonstration occurs.

For a change would be improper for all, being entirely contrary to the lands. Sometimes the victor, sometimes by the victor, it is preserved to lead all toward **great corruptions**. For henceforth, concerning all these things, one must speak of a certain continuous cause of the living law, as also concerning others; because the proofs concerning the world do not bypass those who grasp the mystery, then the causes are necessary and with manifest truth, yet as if without cause. For those who possess something by **reason** <note>original: բանաւ - through the Logos or rational faculty</note> and seek cognitive things, as if for someone else, it shall not be without truth. Along with which, if the rational being exists in itself, it remains a desert toward living change.

For which reason some have thought to say of living things, that these are those which are "abundantly-pacing"—that is, they move quickly—and the expectations of the indicators of knowledge should not bypass those things which are voluntary. If someone signifies a companion, they show nothing else than to change. Truly it is, and by the truth it shall be, from which the eyes are manifest in themselves. <meta>This page continues the Armenian translation of the pseudo-Aristotelian treatise "De Mundo" (On the Cosmos), specifically Chapter 6, discussing the relationship between God's power and the physical world.</meta>

Standing upon the highest summit, all the earth is established beneath Him; yet from His power, those things which are nearest always receive [influence] first, and then after these, those which follow them, and so on according to this order, until it reaches these places in which we are. For this reason, the earth and the things upon the earth, appearing to be at the greatest distance from the help of God <note>original: "այ օգնականութենեն" (ay ognakanut'enen); 'ay' is a common abbreviation for 'Astutsoy' or God</note>, are weak and also inconsistent. Nevertheless, according to how the divine power <term>divine power: ածային զօրութին (atsayin zorut'yun), referring to the Greek 'dynamis'</term> is naturally disposed to penetrate through all things, that which happens to us occurs similarly to that which is above us. However, according to the proximity to God, there is more or less a beneficial communion. But it is better to think thus: especially since it is fitting for God to harmonize the heavens, where His power is sown. And for those things which are most distant, He is at once the cause of their salvation; either by penetrating through them, or rather by reaching where beauty is not absent. The First Cause <note>Aristotle's 'First Mover'</term> performs this Himself; and that which is on earth He holds by power and not by [physical] nature. For this is not fitting for the rulers of men—

—to attend to every detail of the works <gloss>in</gloss> person. They were removed by the distance of many stages <note>original: "ասպարիսաց" (asparisats), a unit of distance</note> from the lower dwellings. And thus, they were situated in the primary place. For which reason, in the **Orphic hymns**, it is not improperly said:
**Zeus is the first, Zeus the thunderer.** <note>original: զևս (Zevs). The text provides a series of epithets for Zeus, identifying him as the supreme principle.</note>
**Zeus is the foundation of the earth and the star-driven sky.**
**Zeus is the sun and the moon. Zeus is the ruler of all.**
<margin>2</margin> For He brings back all hidden things into the light, having taken great care for them. But he wishes to say nothing else of Him except as the invincible cause of the powerful; He came without hindrance and penetrates through all.
He is the last, because He completes all things, and there is nothing incomplete behind Him. And He is said to be a "part," because He distributes the all-giving, for He is distributed to each of the beings.
And He is called "man-like" <note>original: անդրիասս (andriass), likely from the Greek 'andrias' (statue/image of a man).</note> because He is the immovable cause according to nature.
<margin>3</margin> Well said, and the three parts in the body are seen in Him; for the three parts are divided according to the three ages <note>or "spans of time"</note>. That which has been done and offered signifies the **past**.
And that which is still being spun signifies the **present**. And that which is

---

still on the loom signifies the **future**. And the wisdom of these things is: those which were, those which are, and those which shall be. The stretched thread is brought and smoothed.
But as for that which is to come: how difficult it is to reach! To know all these things is possible for no one except God alone. As **Plato** says: "Now God, as the ancient word goes, holding the **beginning, the end, and the middle** of all beings, completes His circuit according to the straight path, following the nature of the heavenly ones." For the sake of justice belonging to God in these punishments. Likewise, he who is about to be fortunate and blessed receives grace from Him.

<meta>This page continues a Neoplatonic or Aristotelian commentary on physics and cosmology, likely following a discussion on the nature of matter and form in the celestial versus terrestrial spheres.</meta>

# On the Nature of Beings

Natural things come into existence, but for some, existence is more substantial. Matter exists more fully in those things which are subject to **generation and corruption** <note>The Aristotelian process of coming-into-being and passing-away</note>. However, for the heavenly beings, there is no matter, but rather something analogous to matter; for matter is receptive to opposites and various forms, whereas the substrate <note>The underlying essence</note> of the heavens is fixed to a single form. It remains eternally as a substrate according to a single number. And if the form of the heavenly beings exists more fully than that of the stars <note>Referring here to the sublunary or changing elements</note> which are subject to generation and corruption, it is because the form of these stars is yoked with corruption and deficiency. It does not belong to that which never and nowhere is-not, but to that which sometimes is and sometimes is-not, following a fleeting form. 

And if the place of these stars exists more fully than that of the heavenly spheres, it is because every wandering star <note>A planet</note> is not fixed in one place. Hence, in the difficulties raised by <term>Aristotle</term> <note>The Greek philosopher whose "Physics" and "On the Heavens" are being discussed here</note>, they ask: "How can you say the fixed stars move if they are not in a place?" For everything that moves is in a place. To this he says: "This moves according to its parts, and thus according to its parts it is in a place." 

Furthermore, time exists more fully for the heavenly beings than for these stars, for it does not measure the movements of the whole, since nothing is swifter than the heavenly motion. The movement of the fixed stars and the heavenly movement exist more fully than

<column-break/>

those of these stars, because in these stars, movement has a cessation, whereas the heavenly beings do not cease at all, for they are **ever-moving**. 

However, the "void" and the "infinite" are seen with difficulty; the infinite in movement, and the void in place. And if these exist, they are neither more nor less <note>than required by the system</note>. For these reasons, because natural things have a body, and as a body they have matter and form; and as they are natural bodies, they also have motion; and as they have motion, they have place and time. For the movement of these natural stars occurs in time and in place. But the void and the infinite, as said before, are seen with difficulty. 

These are the reasons why there are principles for all natural things, neither more nor less. Every moving body is naturally moved by another, for a body is not self-moving; rather, the **soul** is self-moving. Now, we see the heavenly body is in motion; it is evident that it is moved by another. And if it is moved by someone else, then there is something that moves the heavenly body. And since the movement of heaven is one and the same, it is evident that it was naturally disposed to be moved by a single being. And since the heaven is ever-moving, it is evident that the one who moves the heavenly sphere possesses an infinite power; for if...

<summary>This page discusses the Aristotelian distinctions between celestial and terrestrial matter, focusing on why heavenly bodies are superior in their form, motion, and relationship to time and place. It concludes by arguing that the eternal motion of the heavens requires a single, infinite, self-moving prime mover (soul).</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, cosmology, generation and corruption, matter and form, celestial motion, the infinite, the void, prime mover, Armenian Aristotelianism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a philosophical and cosmological discourse, likely from a commentary on Aristotle or a Neoplatonic text, discussing the nature of the First Mover and the inherent faculties of living beings.</meta>

...possessed a finite power, it would not be able to endure moving the heavens incessantly and in an orderly fashion. Therefore, it is evident that it is incorporeal <note>original: "anmarminn"; literally "without body"</note>. For if the one who moves the heavens possessed a body, it could not move the heavens forever, because every body is finite, and I have encountered <note>or "observed"</note> finite power [in such things]. But as for finite power, how could it possess infinite <note>original: "anban"; here used in the sense of "unlimited" or "measureless" power</note> strength, or how could it move the heavens incessantly? And from all this, it became clear that it is uncreated <note>original: "anegh"</note>, and is distinct from that which has come into being, and has no end. For that which has come into being cannot possess infinite power, because everything that has come into being is made of matter and form. But that which is made of matter and form is not incorporeal, nor does it possess infinite power. 

However, that which moves the heaven is shown to be incorporeal and possesses infinite power. Furthermore, if it is uncreated, it is evident that it is incorruptible <note>original: "anapakan"</note> and was not made by anyone; rather, things came into being from it by its own self-creative will. For it exists before all things, and it is followed by the eternal, the beginningless, and the endless. Now, it is established according to the order of what has been said: that there is someone who moves the celestial body, and he is one, and he possesses infinite power, and is incorporeal, and is uncreated and incorruptible, and

<column-break/>

unmade <note>original: "anararogh"</note>. And these attributes, which are not naturally suited to any other created beings, belong to him alone; they should be attributed to him alone. Those who wish to know the nature of things at once must know these three things: first, the beginning of things and the cause; second, that the beginning is also the cause of others; third, whether it is possible for the thing to be otherwise. By these three, it is possible to achieve comprehension. 

The exercise of all things is achieved through that [comprehension], as if by a training ground for the tongue and for reason <note>original: "ban"</note>; and this is just. And the wise, before having authority, [move] through training grounds as if by nature, habitually. In the observation of what lies ahead, the nostrils of the deer often move, as do the ears of the donkey, the sense of smell in the dog, and the forehead-butting in the sheep, and others. Furthermore, the horse senses war beforehand, the vulture senses a carcass, and the bee senses the hive. Likewise, the grazers [sense] the grass, the aquatic swimmers sense the wild streams, the creeping things sense the autumn, and the birds sense the scents of spring. And what these [animals] possess by nature, man possesses through the deliberation of the mind and the training of reason.

<meta>This page continues a cosmological and philosophical discussion, likely from a commentary on Aristotle or a similar Neoplatonic Armenian text (such as David the Invincible). ...or like a single watermelon; for the heavens entirely encompass it above and below, and all around. And by constantly revolving around it, they define the parts of the Universe <note>original: "Ti" or "Tiezerk," meaning the cosmos</note>. And they call the place "unchanging," for the heavens are one hemisphere in place, in the ether; and the ether is in the air, and the air is in the water, and the water is in the earth. Likewise with the other hemisphere. Now, it has these boundaries: the Universe, the hemisphere, and the earth in the center. And from all sides, the heavens are called the "edge" and the earth the "center." Now, some have said that there is a "contrariety of place" <note>the philosophical concept of spatial opposites, like Up and Down</note> in the world; for the northern part is contrary to the southern part, and so on, regarding the spheres. They called the upper edges the two hemispheres of the heavens which are the boundaries, and the center of the Universe—the earth and the rest—the "inner" or "lower" part. For in this way, in the whole, the upper part is the boundary and the middle is the interior. Because of the distance between the edges of the Universe and the center, they named the edges "upper" and the center "lower." And by this, they spoke of contrariety in place. And the definition of other contraries is clear; it is evident from this that they refer to place. They said that just as excellence and evil are of one genus—from quality—and because of being very far from one another they are contraries to each other, so too are the edges of the Universe, which are "upper," and

<column-break/>

the center, which is "lower." Although they are of the same genus in terms of place, because of the great distance between the edges and the center, the contraries are distinct.

-> But let one thing not disturb us: <-
the parts of a substance. For those parts are included <note>literally "fall into"</note> which are the twelve <note>possibly referring to the zodiac or a specific division of members</note>, and the head, and the hands, and the wings of birds, and the horns of horned creatures, and the feet of four-legged animals. Likewise with the parts of others in the remainder. But contrarieties are not like a part of a whole as these are, but rather they are distinct from that in which they exist. It is not that they can be separated from the matter; no. But like whiteness in matter: even if the matter changes to another color, the accident <term>accident: a philosophical term for a property (like color) that can change without destroying the object itself</term> cannot exist by itself. And these are like the solid, the dense, like iron.

<meta>This page begins a treatise attributed to Aristotle addressed to King Alexander, focusing on the nature of virtue and the tripartite soul. It follows the traditional medieval Armenian curriculum where Aristotelian ethics were studied alongside Platonic concepts.</meta>

# Aristotle: On Virtue. To King Alexander.

Wisdom is a fountain.
It irrigates many lands.
It causes various flowers to bloom.
It brings wisdom to the buying mind. <note>The "buying mind" likely refers to a mind eager to acquire knowledge, as if in a marketplace.</note>
Where genius flows in abundance.
It is adorned with brilliant words.
It decorates the mind with rich wisdom.
It bridles the character of all.
It subdues the wild impulses.
The lover of learning is highly renowned.
Make your vision familiar with it.
Be a vigorous and serving foster-child [of wisdom].
Honor the philosopher willingly.
Be receptive to their words.
And make their counsel your own.
May this seem pleasing to you.
So that you may become a good child.
To truly love the words of the wise is very
sweet, and this love is most becoming
to the ingenious.
To ignore the desires of the mind is
unseemly, and for the prudent of this world
it is not misguided.
If one receives this with effort and sincerity,
they shall receive very rich praise from the tribunal.
->Understand the art<-
->for you shall benefit greatly.<-

<image-desc>Intricate decorative headpiece with symmetrical floral and scrollwork designs in black ink. To the right of the title area, a vertical floral ornament extends down the margin.</image-desc>

# AS IS EVIDENT.
## AND A PRAISEWORTHY DEED.

As they are full; for from the good <margin>arise</margin> virtue, and from the loathsome, vice. Furthermore, the causes of virtues, the things following virtues, and the things resulting from them are praiseworthy. Conversely, those things which are contrary are blameworthy. Now, taking the tripartite [nature] of the soul according to Plato <note>referring to the Platonic theory of the soul divided into rational, spirited, and appetitive parts</note>: the virtue of the **Rational** part is **Prudence**; of the **Spirited** part, **Gentleness** and **Courage**; of the **Appetitive** part, **Temperance** and **Continence**; and of the whole soul, **Justice**, **Liberality**, and **Magnanimity**. Conversely, the vice of the **Rational** part is **Folly**.

<summary>This page introduces a philosophical discourse on virtue addressed to Alexander the Great, utilizing a poetic prologue followed by a Platonic classification of the soul's virtues and vices.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Alexander the Great, Plato, Virtue, Vice, Prudence, Courage, Temperance, Justice, Tripartite Soul, Armenian Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical categorization of the soul's faculties, specifically contrasting the virtues and vices associated with the rational, spirited, and appetitive parts of the soul. It follows the Platonic-Aristotelian tradition common in medieval Armenian scholasticism.</meta>

What is the vice of the **spirited** <note>original: "ցասմնատեսակին" (tsasmnatesakin) — the irascible or emotional part of the soul</note> part? It is wrathfulness and cowardice. What is the vice of the **appetitive** <note>original: "ցանկականին" (tsankakanin) — the part of the soul driven by desire</note> part? It is lewdness and profligacy. What is the vice of the whole soul? Injustice, illiberality, and smallness of soul <note>pusillanimity</note>.

Now, **prudence** is the virtue of the **rational** part; it is prepared for those things which cooperate toward happiness. And **meekness** is the virtue of the **spirited** part, according to which people live free from the terrors concerning death. And **temperance** is the virtue of the **appetitive** part, according to which they remain incorruptible regarding the knowledge and enjoyment of base pleasures. Furthermore, **continence** is a virtue of the **appetitive** part, according to which they restrain by the mind the desire that rushes toward the worst pleasures. And **justice** is the virtue of the soul that distributes according to merit; and **liberality** is the virtue of the soul that is ready to spend on the most beautiful things; and **magnanimity** <note>literally "greatness of soul"</note> is the virtue of the soul according to which one is able to endure good fortune and misfortune, honor and dishonor.

<column-break/>

Conversely, **imprudence** is the vice of the **rational** part, being the cause of living wickedly. And **wrathfulness** is the vice of the **spirited** part, according to which people are easily moved by anger. And **cowardice** is the vice of the **spirited** part, according to which they are terrified by the fears concerning death. **Lewdness** is the vice of the **appetitive** part, according to which they are eager for the enjoyment of base pleasures. **Profligacy** is the vice of the **appetitive** part, according to which they are eager for base pleasure, not being restrained by the **rational** part. **Injustice** is the vice of the soul, according to which they are greedy for what belongs to others. And **illiberality** is the vice of the soul, according to which they are desirous of gain from every side. **Smallness of soul** is the vice of the soul, according to which they are unable to endure good fortune or misfortune, or honor or dishonor.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on ethics and the classification of vices, likely following a discussion on virtues. The text uses a systematic approach to define specific moral failings and their sub-categories.</meta>

The lover of song <note>original: "երգասէր" (ergasēr); likely referring to one obsessed with sensory pleasures or theatrical spectacles</note> is of the same color as the worker of Edipus <note>original: "եդիպագործոյն" (edipagortsoyn); possibly a reference to tragic or dramatic arts</note> in both words and deeds, and is followed by **dissoluteness** <term>zeghkhut’yun: excessive indulgence or profligacy</term>. Disorder, shamelessness, indecency, softness, obscenity, negligence, contempt, and abandonment follow it.

But **extravagance** is the inclination toward the enjoyment of pleasures; the rational mind does not prevent one from imagining that it is better not to participate in such things, yet participating in them is no less frequent. And one ought to think to do good things—those that are reasoned—rather than these other matters. Regarding pleasures, extravagance is followed by: wantonness, remorse, and many of those things that followed dissoluteness.

<margin>÷</margin> Now, of **injustice** <term>aniravut’yun: unrighteousness or lawlessness</term>, there are three types: **impiety**, **greed**, and **insults**. Impiety consists of errors toward the gods <note>The text uses the plural "gods," suggesting a translation of a Classical Greek philosophical source</note>, toward demons <note>original: "դիւն" (diwn); in a philosophical context, this refers to *daemons* or guiding spirits</note>, toward one's departed ancestors, toward parents, and toward the fatherland. Greed is found in commerce, being the inclination to profit more than what is worthy. Insults are when, while preparing pleasures for themselves, they bring reproach upon others; hence it is said of such a person that although he appears to gain nothing, he is nevertheless unjust. It is characteristic of injustice to transgress against the affairs of the fatherland, and against the patterns and boundaries of the

<column-break/>

laws and the rulers; to take oaths falsely, and to transgress against promises and faithfulness. Injustice is followed by: slander, arrogance, hypocritical philanthropy, malice, and craftiness.

Of **illiberality** <term>anazatut’yun: literally "non-freedom," referring to a lack of generosity or a base spirit</term>, there are three types: **sordid gain**, **niggardliness**, and **un-communicativeness** <note>refers to a refusal to share resources or participate in the common good</note>. Sordid gain is when they seek to profit from all sides, considering profit to be higher than shame. Niggardliness is when they spend meagerly and miserably, and are harmed by being without grace. Un-communicativeness is when they [fail to] spend their possessions on what is appropriate. It is characteristic of illiberality to take extreme care of possessions, and to consider nothing a reproach if it provides an occasion for profit. They are like hirelings of life; even the clothing suitable for servants is neglected and illiberal.

Illiberality is followed by: small-talk, heaviness of mind, smallness of soul <note>pusillanimity</note>, extreme baseness, ignobility, and misanthropy. And it is characteristic of **smallness of soul** that neither honor nor dishonor, neither good fortune nor misfortune, can be endured, but [only] honor...

...a lover of song, and one who is prone to ease in both words and deeds. And from **dissoluteness** <note>original: "zghkhut’iun" — refers to luxury, excess, or debauchery</note> follow: disorder, shamelessness, indecency, delicacy, vanity, negligence, and utter contempt.

As for **extravagance**, it is the inclination toward the enjoyment of pleasures, where the rational mind does not prevent one from imagining that partaking in such things is the best goal. However, partaking in them is no less a fault, even if one thinks they are doing good; for the cause of such things is the pursuit of pleasures. And from extravagance follow: wantonness, remorse, and many of those same things that followed from dissoluteness.

The forms of **illiberality** <note>original: "anazatut’iun" — literally 'non-freedom', referring to a lack of generosity or a base spirit</note> appear as: profiteering, stinginess, and lack of communal spirit. Profiteering is when men seek to gain from every side, considering profit more important than shame. Stinginess is when they spend very little and with great difficulty, and are harmed by their lack of grace. Lack of communal spirit is when men do not spend their wealth on what is appropriate. <meta>This page continues a dense philosophical and theological discourse, likely from a Scholastic or Neoplatonic Armenian context, regarding the nature of "being," "essence," and "unity." To make it felt in this way is called the **universal** <note>original: "bnashkharhakan" - pertaining to the whole world or universal</note> way of speaking. If I am to truly imprint this, I shall speak of it simply by calling it **unity**. If not, what is it according to the first meaning, according to the **natural definition**? <term>Natural definition: The essential boundaries that make a thing what it is.</term> Insofar as the same nature exists: if by having such a door of the same type, you simultaneously possess the **distinction** of created things. If such a corruption exists in these—if you do not possess them—the same would be a "lamented" <note>likely meaning "regrettable" or "erroneous" in this logical context</note> way of saying "non-nature" by those same terms by which one speaks of the **signifier**. To speak of the one nature **ineffably** <note>original: "ancharapes" - beyond words</note>, which is great and in no way small, is also distinct according to their own natures, having variety. What is it, indeed, in which the one and the individual are here arranged naturally, brought forth by ten thousand degrees?

As if according to **species**.

As if according to **places**.

As if according to **theoretical genera**.

But if these things are so:

The signs are different.

First, the giving of the incomprehensible wall <note>metaphor for a logical barrier or boundary</note> to say "non-nature."

and also from the **senses**. Some say nature is the offspring of the nature of the **mind** alone.

Not according to the individual nature alone, according to the "one," when speaking of the "son" in a simple sense. And on the same day in which one speaks of the **unnatural**, the distinction—only the "non-nature" according to the same signs—when one speaks of the "one." That is to say, the distinction of the "son" cannot be corrupted.

One, according to the lengths, is not the meaning; to say "one" is "non-nature" according to the same signs. The **distinguishing** wall, the door spread out from the various, is the same when one speaks of the "one." That is to say, those things which you called "non-nature" by the "one" are corrupted, as if speaking according to the **natural**. The distinguishing wall, the door: those things which you say are of the same, in which [they are] of the gods as if by the same—by those terms—where the signifying is not the meaning, to say "non-nature" as if according to the **unnatural**, to say the distinction is corrupted. If to speak of the "unnatural" is to speak of their "non-living" <note>original: "och kendaninn" - the inanimate or non-living</note> according to the same preceding things, it is as if speaking of the distinction of the **species**. But I shall not call this "non-nature"...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and logical discourse from the previous section, focusing on the relationship between the individual (particular) and the nature (universal), and the methods of logical division.</meta>

### By the reduction of these:
### Fifth: Whether each of the individuals 
### is capable of being a mediator.

<margin>Whether</margin> Now you shall see, according to the likeness of unity, that some things are naturally simple and some are composite. Now, regarding the simple and the individuals, they remain the same because they are naturally <margin>is</margin> disposed by the first intention. But concerning the composite, let us say that it is not possible to speak of or for there to be mediation outside of them. First, because we called <margin>2</margin> one nature the same universal natural definition. But the individual is not universal, but particular. For did we not <margin>mere</margin> previously show that the individual is nature to us, along with others of the same universal, and is said to be one nature? And thirdly, let us speak of those things: the non-composite mediation. The same creating genus, being the same along with other individuals. But how is it said to be a mediation? <margin>6</margin> Fourthly, in addition to these: every composite single nature is naturally disposed to have a multitude of diverse species. But the individual is so by its effects; as the soul and the body being simple and individual. I ask: the species was naturally disposed as a mediation, which is also brought forth according to the many who are distinguished by number. But how can the individual be outside of the species and not be brought forth from the many?

<column-break/>

Therefore, it is not possible to speak of or for there to be a mediation. For it was said regarding mediation that those which do not possess the unity of such things are never said to be the same anywhere.

->Concerning the same [subject] regarding division.<-

In the logic of divisions, it is necessary for them to be manifest from existing things: either to be a body, or incorporeal, or a mean between both. Now, having remained silent here regarding the incorporeal and the immediate, let us proceed to the first. In the same way, it is necessary [to consider] the flow of material bodies: either in the flow of matter, or in appearances, or to be divided by lines. But for us, regarding those divided by appearance, it is necessary for some to be natural <term>natural: things possessing an inherent nature or essence</term> and some to be non-natural. Of the natural, again, some are botanical <note>literally: "growing things"</note> and some are animate <note>literally: "possessing breath/soul"</note>. And others are animate-botanical. And for these to be moved, it is necessary for such botanical things to be specifically only small functional joints. But the animate [possess] noble members. But for the animate-botanical definitions: they are neither noble members nor specifically particularized. Then, for the non-natural, it is necessary for some to be uncrafted <note>natural objects not shaped by art</note> and some to be crafted <note>products of art or skill</note>. And the crafted are specifically particularized, while the uncrafted are non-specifically particularized. And for these constrained things, it is necessary for each to be some whole, some non-specifically.

<summary>This page explores the logical relationship between individuals and universal natures, arguing that mediation exists within species rather than individuals. It also introduces a system of logical division (diaeresis) categorizing beings into corporeal/incorporeal, animate/botanical, and crafted/uncrafted.</summary>

One who unrepentantly busied himself with plundering his city became a tyrant and was caught seizing his own goods. He was brought before Demetrius <note>Likely Demetrius Poliorcetes</note>, and when asked what he had lost before the people, he said to the public: "From my own, you have taken nothing at all by words."

### Concerning Providence

> **Epicurus** says: Thanks be to blessed Nature, for she has made that which is necessary easy to acquire, and that which is difficult to acquire, unnecessary. <note>A famous Epicurean maxim regarding the simplicity of true needs.</note>

> **Democritus** said: Do not consider God the cause of evil; rather, it is the lack of discipline <note>original: "ամուսանց" likely referring to a lack of harmony or restraint</note> in humans and their fortune that makes us think so.

> **Plato** says: Whenever it is necessary for God to do good for a city, He then makes the citizens good. But when He considers something ill for it, He removes the good from that city. Likewise, by learning virtue through nature, one attains the divine Word <note>original: "ածային բանին" - the Logos</note> through acquisitions.

> Fortune and time, O how much they steer all things for man!

> **Imados** <note>Likely a corruption of a Greek name, possibly Isocrates or a minor philosopher.</note> says: To the extent that God is a co-worker with the righteous, to that same extent He opposes the unrighteous.

### Concerning Wealth and the Love of Money

> **Callisthenes** says: In a day of war, iron is better than gold; the latter causes more pain than the birth itself. <note>original: "ծնութի" - birth/origin</note>

> It is possible to reach the depths of ruin without caution, just as it is possible to have a wicked heart without a muzzle and pride without care.

> A fishhook without bread <note>bait</note> and not...

<column-break/>

<page-num>179</page-num>

...virtue without sobriety is like a beautiful [thing] without grace.

> The lives of those who love money are like the tables of funeral feasts: they have everything, but it is others who enjoy what they possess.

> One ought to seek the grace of the Father of the dead <note>God</note> for those who are generous.

> All things reach us quickly through knowledge, but as for the profit of silver:

> The triple-ranked <note>term: "Եռակարգ" - perhaps referring to degrees of intensity</note> misery of those who love money: through the desire for profit, the insatiability for possessions brings about death, just as a leech <note>original: "ժաւականն" - likely a variant for a blood-sucker</note> brings death through blood.

> The rich man is a sign of those who suffer from dropsy <note>term: "ջրողից" - a medical condition where the body swells with fluid</note>; for just as they are thirsty despite being full of water, so are these men despite their possessions.

> Do not call one "noble" or "of a great brave race" because of his many-folded silver coins, nor a man "honorable" because of his manifold possessions, but rather him who is brave in virtue.

> Despise those who hasten to acquire possessions but cannot enjoy them; for they are like those who acquire noble horses but do not know how to ride.

> Consider those to be of great mind not who put on great and wide works as if they were capable, but those who are lovers of moderate things and are capable in whatever they set their hand to.

### Concerning Prayers

**I**socrates says that those who ask God for an undisciplined life, He makes them mindless. For a spirit is not given to a pharmacist through prayers, nor is beauty given to painters, nor

<column-break/>

to musicians their music and harmonious melody, unless you gain the skill through study according to their rules. In the same way, it is not possible for a man to become wise through prayers alone, if he does not gain the skill of wisdom by taking care to study.

### Concerning Drunkenness

**P**lato says: [The mind] is corrupted by fire and water when mixed with the wine of drunkards.
**D**iogenes, in a certain place where they were serving him much wine, took it and poured it on the ground. When a man approached him with accusations that he was not doing what was proper, he said: "If I drink it, the wine destroys both itself and me; but it is better, as I have done, that it alone should perish."
**P**ythagoras, when asked by someone how one might abstain from drunkenness, said: "If he always hates whatever is done during drunkenness, and keeps the shame of it before his eyes."

# On the Nature of Women and Discipline

...he casts it away because he does not wish to care for children, and for the pain of which he finds the medicine in a woman. 

-> <-

Diogenes, being at a gathering of women where one was boasting of her great wealth, was the only one among them who did not wear ornaments. When asked about a woman’s finery, he said: "What kind of ornament is it that strips a man of his own?" 

-> <-

Epictetus <note>original: "Epakritos," likely a corruption of Epictetus</note> was asked why Apocles <note>possibly a reference to a known figure or a corruption of a Greek name</note> brings such sweet apples, and he called out many reasons, but said it was to honor the women; yet he said the same even if the women spoke against him. Diogenes <note>Diogenes of Sinope, the Cynic philosopher</note> was asked what evils are. And he said: a storm that sinks a man's house; for the soul of a man, to remain ignorant of life's path; envy that clouds the mind. To the one asking: a dragon sharing the throne; a living evil; a leavened deception; a self-willing conflict that enters the master's house to make him feel like a dancing partridge <note>a metaphor for being made a fool or being easily hunted</note>; the sneeze of a double-minded cat; a deceptive struggle of the heel; a drop of poison mixed with a friend; and a fine for the innocent. Diogenes said: when you travel on the road and the horns <note>perhaps of a stag or a dilemma</note> burst, and robbers are there and you are afraid, he said to him: flee from things through me, for the weapon of reproach begins with the woman who knows her own or is afraid.
Aegisthus <note>likely a corruption of a philosopher's name, possibly Aristotle or a Stoic</note> says: there is nothing worse than an evil woman; for the father adds bread to the children's portion, but they cook it and pour it out on the road, and they take the bread from the house to serve their own desires; and he who receives fines and evils in the house is mixed with them, yet is still aware of her evil.
A cart that moves from its place and falls into the hand of milk <note>unclear; possibly a metaphor for slipping or instability</note> is doubly destroyed; one because of the strangers and the other because of the wife. Linus <note>the mythical poet/philosopher</note> and the chosen one fell into the cunning of the speaker.
In the same way, a woman who is defiled and thirsty for honor...

<margin>Cyril</margin>
says: O mothers who have renounced living things, the dead are not well adorned.
And the woman might say: if I seek a joke for my deeds, you instead be prepared for the rising of your own soul. One should not believe a man's face, for everything is placed in the tomb.
Nicander the philosopher <note>likely Nicander of Colophon</note>, speaking to a pure person, said: every woman is good, and she stands ready; but when you say they have passed on, they were transformed. The master tested the mother, and in her lack of restraint, he said: this is not a woman of visitation <note>meaning a virtuous or modest woman</note>. And the mother died of shame, and he cut out the tongue of the one who had spoken, and he questioned him with the grip of hunger.
Nicander says: a scorpion said to a woman, "My sting is more evil than your tongue." The woman said, "Why is that?" The scorpion said, "When I sting, a medicine can be found; but you wound with a multitude of evil words and pains, and there is no medicine for their healing. For you do not act like the sun, you are not an evil path, and you do not kill like death." Diogenes says: one must cry out against an evil woman, for it is better to dwell in a den with a lion than to live mixed with an undisciplined and rowdy woman.
If you wish to live in peace, do not speak with a woman at all.

## It was said concerning the Door <note>Likely a metaphorical "door" to wisdom or a specific chapter heading.</note>

My son, wake up to the work of rest, and you shall seem to reach the heights with upper wings. Consider in your mind the things to come through trial; for the occurrence of fortune, through an undefined good, wanders everywhere. It knows neither king nor peasant, nor the multitude. Alexander <note>Likely Alexander the Great, often cited in medieval wisdom literature.</note> says: if all were in a state of idle harmony, the world would be a stagnant work; the sea would be motionless, and the earth uncultivated. Marriages would not occur, and childhood would not exist. If everyone took from everyone else and gave nothing back in exchange, then nothing would belong to anyone. 

Perhaps when Elymas <note>original: "եղիւսաց" - possibly a corruption of a classical name</note> was praying from his <term>apsis</term> <note>A semi-circular recess or vaulted space</note>: "When you receive the kingdom, what will you give me in return for my distress?" And he said regarding the things prepared beforehand: "Why do you ask now, not having the staff of tomorrow? At that time I will tell you what seems pleasing to you, when time commands the payment to be given." And having heard this, he praised his wisdom. May you live as a pious son.

Theodorus says: every perfect thing is the beginning of its own creation. Harmony...

<column-break/>

...leads; but he who works the good shall follow the same.
In the lessons he says: I call "Philosopher" <note>original: "Իմաստասէր" - lover of wisdom</note> not the one who knows the measure, nor the one who is able to acquire many ideas, but the one who has acquired within himself a blameless and pure way of life. For the perfect philosopher, who knows many things, is able to subdue what is lacking here. For this reason, he said that philosophy is to meditate upon death; and to die beforehand is not a flight from existence, nor is it to kill oneself, but rather the death of the passions—nothing other than a flight from evils. 

For this reason he says, that he who lives a life of truth is not slain. There was a certain man among the crowd named Cleombrotus <note>A reference to Cleombrotus of Ambracia, who famously jumped to his death after reading Plato's Phaedo.</note> who misunderstood the writings of Plato concerning the soul; he thought that Plato spoke of killing oneself, and he ran from the wall and died in vain because of ignorance. Also for the other, many lives are [given]... and let them not die in vain through [misplaced] confidence in the power of words. Let him know what it is that kills, but the spirit gives life.

# 4. Definitions for the Multi-faceted Thinker
From the *Definitions* of David the Philosopher <note>David the Invincible (Dawitʻ Anyatʻ), a 6th-century Neoplatonist scholar whose works became foundational to Armenian philosophy</note> and other lovers of wisdom, arranged alphabetically according to the letters of the script.

---

<margin>Greed - Insatiability</margin> **A**varice is an insatiable and involuntary desire for glory or other worldly freedoms. **A**varice, in its specific form, is a disordered love of silver or those things which are traded for silver. **A**varice is the insatiability and involuntary craving of each individual. **P**assion <note>original: "Akht" (ախտ) - translates to passion, suffering, or disease, similar to the Greek 'pathos'</note> is an irrational movement of the soul according to an elevation or an unchangeable state. **P**assion is a sensory movement of the necessary desiring power regarding the nature of good and evil. Furthermore, **P**assion is an irrational movement of the soul based on opinions of good or evil. **P**assion is a movement in one thing caused by another. **P**assion is a homonym <note>a word with multiple meanings</note> for both bodily illness and wounds, as well as for the soul's desire and sorrow. **P**assion is that which is followed by pleasure or sorrow. However, it is not the same as sorrow itself, for those without sensation may suffer a passion <note>in the sense of being acted upon</note> but they do not feel sorrow or become disturbed. For passion is one thing and disturbance is another; disturbance is the sensory perception of a passion. **E**xpectation is a shuddering...

<summary>This page presents a series of philosophical definitions for "Avarice" and "Passion," drawing from the Neoplatonic tradition of David the Invincible. It distinguishes between physical ailments and the psychological "passions" that move the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a series of philosophical and theological definitions, likely from a medieval Armenian dictionary or a collection of "Definitions" (Sāhmank‘) attributed to figures like David the Invincible.
